## Exam 53 — Part 7: Condo Possession Delay

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Elena,

I know you've been expecting photos of me settled into my new place by now, so I wanted to explain what's been going on. The move-in date for the condo has been pushed back, and it's been quite a frustrating experience.

When I signed the purchase agreement back in March, the developer, Rideau Homes, promised that the building at 45 Laurier Avenue would be ready by October 15th. I was so excited because that timing worked perfectly — it gave me enough time to finish up at the Ottawa office and sort out everything with my old apartment on Elgin Street. Well, about three weeks ago, I received a letter saying the completion date had been moved to December 1st. Apparently, the inspection revealed plumbing issues on the upper floors, and repairs are taking longer than expected because of a shortage of specialized contractors.

I was honestly devastated when I read that letter. I had already given notice to my landlord and arranged for the movers, so I couldn't exactly stay put. Luckily, my coworker Priya offered to let me store most of my furniture in her garage, and I've been staying at Mom's place in Kanata since the beginning of November. She's been wonderful about it, but her apartment is small, and I can tell we're both ready for a little more space.

The financial side has been stressful too. I lost the $300 deposit I paid to the moving company because I cancelled with less than a week's notice. On top of that, I had to pay $475 to extend my storage unit rental for two extra months. Rideau Homes offered a $1,500 credit toward closing costs as compensation, which helps, but it doesn't quite cover everything.

The silver lining is that Priya actually lives in the same development — she moved into Building A last spring — and she says the units are beautiful once they're finished. That gives me hope that the wait will be worth it. My unit is in Building C on the ninth floor, and it has a view of the Rideau Canal, which I've dreamed about since I first moved to Ottawa.

I'd love for you to come visit once I'm finally settled. Maybe around the holidays? We could go skating on the canal together if it's cold enough by then.

Miss you lots.

Love,
Nadia

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** Nadia is writing to Elena to .....
- 1. ask for help with her moving costs.
- 2. invite Elena to stay at her new condo.
- 3. explain why she hasn't moved in yet.
- 4. complain about her mother's small apartment.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. ask for help with her moving costs: Nadia describes financial losses but never asks Elena for money or help with costs.
❌ 2. invite Elena to stay at her new condo: The invitation at the end is secondary; the main purpose of the email is explaining the delay.
✅ 3. explain why she hasn't moved in yet: Nadia opens by saying 'I know you've been expecting photos of me settled into my new place by now, so I wanted to explain what's been going on.'
❌ 4. complain about her mother's small apartment: Nadia mentions the apartment is small but is grateful her mother has been 'wonderful about it.'

**Q2:** The building's completion was delayed because of .....
- 1. plumbing problems discovered during inspection.
- 2. issues with the purchase agreement signed in March.
- 3. a shortage of building materials for the upper floors.
- 4. the developer's decision to redesign the units.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. plumbing problems discovered during inspection: Nadia says 'the inspection revealed plumbing issues on the upper floors' and repairs are slow due to a contractor shortage.
❌ 2. issues with the purchase agreement signed in March: The purchase agreement was signed without problems; it's the construction that was delayed.
❌ 3. a shortage of building materials for the upper floors: The shortage is of 'specialized contractors,' not building materials — the upper floors detail is used incorrectly here.
❌ 4. the developer's decision to redesign the units: No redesign is mentioned; the delay is due to unexpected repairs.

**Q3:** Nadia had to leave her old apartment because she .....
- 1. was asked to move out by the developer.
- 2. wanted to live closer to her mother.
- 3. needed to save money for closing costs.
- 4. had already informed her landlord she was leaving.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. was asked to move out by the developer: The developer delayed the condo; they had nothing to do with her old apartment.
❌ 2. wanted to live closer to her mother: Staying at her mother's was a temporary solution, not her reason for leaving Elgin Street.
❌ 3. needed to save money for closing costs: No connection is made between saving money and leaving her apartment.
✅ 4. had already informed her landlord she was leaving: Nadia says 'I had already given notice to my landlord,' so she couldn't stay even when the condo was delayed.

**Q4:** The $475 Nadia spent was for .....
- 1. cancelling the moving company booking.
- 2. extra time at a storage facility.
- 3. a deposit on the new condo.
- 4. renting a temporary apartment in Kanata.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. cancelling the moving company booking: The cancellation cost was $300, not $475 — the amounts are swapped here.
✅ 2. extra time at a storage facility: Nadia says she 'had to pay $475 to extend my storage unit rental for two extra months.'
❌ 3. a deposit on the new condo: The $1,500 credit is related to closing costs, but $475 was for storage — these are different expenses.
❌ 4. renting a temporary apartment in Kanata: Nadia is staying at her mother's place in Kanata for free, not renting.

**Q5:** Nadia probably feels .....
- 1. grateful toward Priya for her support.
- 2. jealous that Priya moved in before her.
- 3. annoyed that Priya gave her false hope.
- 4. disappointed in Priya's description of the units.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. grateful toward Priya for her support: Priya stored Nadia's furniture in her garage and reassured her that the units are beautiful — Nadia calls this 'the silver lining.'
❌ 2. jealous that Priya moved in before her: While Priya moved in last spring, Nadia doesn't express jealousy; she focuses on Priya's helpfulness and encouragement.
❌ 3. annoyed that Priya gave her false hope: Nadia says Priya's comments 'give me hope,' and she sees this positively, not as false hope.
❌ 4. disappointed in Priya's description of the units: Nadia says Priya's description gives her hope, not disappointment.

**Q6:** Nadia has dreamed about .....
- 1. working at the Ottawa office.
- 2. living on Laurier Avenue.
- 3. seeing the canal from her home.
- 4. skating with Elena in the winter.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. working at the Ottawa office: Nadia mentions finishing up at the Ottawa office, but it's not something she dreamed about.
❌ 2. living on Laurier Avenue: The condo is at 45 Laurier Avenue, but Nadia's dream is specifically about the view, not the street address.
✅ 3. seeing the canal from her home: Nadia says her ninth-floor unit 'has a view of the Rideau Canal, which I've dreamed about since I first moved to Ottawa.'
❌ 4. skating with Elena in the winter: Skating is a suggestion for Elena's visit, not something Nadia says she's dreamed about.

**Q7:** None
- 1. frustrating
- 2. exciting
- 3. confusing
- 4. overwhelming
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. frustrating: Nadia explicitly describes the delay as 'quite a frustrating experience,' making this the most accurate word for Elena to echo back.

## Exam 53 — Part 9: Pantanal Wetland Ecosystem

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. The Pantanal is the world's largest tropical wetland, spanning approximately 150,000 to 195,000 square kilometres across western Brazil, with smaller portions extending into Bolivia and Paraguay. Located in the upper Paraguay River basin, this vast floodplain undergoes dramatic seasonal transformations. During the rainy season, from October to March, up to 80 percent of the landscape becomes submerged, creating a mosaic of lagoons, marshes, and flooded grasslands. In contrast, the dry season reveals expansive plains and isolated pools. The Pantanal sits at a relatively low elevation, averaging between 80 and 150 metres above sea level, which facilitates its extensive annual flooding.

B. The Pantanal's ecological richness is remarkable, supporting one of the highest concentrations of wildlife in the Americas. Biologists have documented over 3,500 plant species, approximately 480 bird species, 260 fish species, and more than 120 mammal species within this single ecosystem. Notably, the region harbours the largest population of jaguars on the planet, with densities far exceeding those found in the Amazon rainforest. The hyacinth macaw, the world's largest flying parrot, also thrives here, with an estimated 5,000 individuals representing the majority of its global population. Consequently, the Pantanal serves as a critical refuge for numerous endangered species.

C. For centuries, the Pantanal has been home to indigenous communities, including the Guató and Bororo peoples, who developed sustainable fishing and hunting practices adapted to the region's seasonal rhythms. In the eighteenth century, European settlers introduced cattle ranching, which remains the predominant economic activity today. Approximately 2,500 ranches, known locally as fazendas, operate across the wetland, raising an estimated eight million head of cattle. More recently, ecotourism has emerged as a significant source of income, with visitors drawn by opportunities for wildlife observation and horseback excursions. However, population density remains remarkably low, at fewer than two inhabitants per square kilometre.

D. Despite its ecological significance, the Pantanal faces mounting environmental pressures. Large-scale soybean farming and sugarcane cultivation on surrounding plateaus have accelerated deforestation and increased pesticide runoff into the watershed. In 2020, unprecedented wildfires devastated roughly 30 percent of the entire wetland, destroying habitats and killing millions of animals. Scientists attributed the severity of these fires to prolonged drought conditions intensified by climate change. In response, the Brazilian government expanded the Pantanal Matogrossense National Park, established in 1981, and UNESCO designated a portion of the region as a World Heritage Site in 2000. Nevertheless, conservationists argue that current protections remain insufficient.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

### Questions & Answer Key

- 3. C

- 5. E

- **Explanation:** Paragraph D states that unprecedented wildfires devastated roughly 30 percent of the wetland and that scientists attributed their severity to drought conditions intensified by climate change. 'Shifting climate patterns' paraphrases 'climate change' and 'catastrophic ecological disaster' paraphrases the unprecedented wildfires.

## Exam 53 — Part 10: Urban Drive-Through Bans

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
When the city of Minneapolis enacted a sweeping prohibition on new drive-through construction in 2019, urban planners across North America took notice, and the debate surrounding the role of these automotive conveniences in contemporary cityscapes has only intensified since. A 2023 municipal survey conducted in Toronto revealed that drive-through establishments generate, on average, forty-two percent more vehicular idling emissions per customer transaction than their dine-in counterparts, a figure that has galvanized environmental advocates and prompted several Canadian municipalities to contemplate similar restrictions. The findings have reignited longstanding tensions between commercial viability and the liveability aspirations that many cities now profess to uphold.

Urban designer Priya Ramachandran, who consults for the City of Vancouver's planning department, contends that drive-throughs represent an anachronistic model of commercial design fundamentally incompatible with the densification goals most metropolitan areas currently espouse. "These facilities prioritize automobile throughput over pedestrian accessibility, effectively fragmenting walkable streetscapes with sprawling asphalt queuing lanes," Ramachandran argues. She further observes that the land footprint required for a single drive-through corridor could accommodate mixed-use infill development serving considerably more residents and enterprises, a trade-off she regards as increasingly untenable in cities grappling with acute housing shortages. In her estimation, the convenience such lanes afford does not warrant the broader urban costs they impose.

Not everyone, however, views the matter with such unequivocal disapproval. Restaurant franchise owner Derek Mulholland, who operates several quick-service locations across the Greater Toronto Area, insists that drive-throughs remain indispensable for working families and individuals with mobility constraints who cannot readily navigate traditional storefronts. "Eliminating drive-throughs does not eliminate demand — it merely displaces it, compelling customers to circle parking lots or double-park along residential streets, which arguably exacerbates the very congestion municipalities claim to be mitigating," Mulholland asserts. He notes that his drive-through locations employ roughly thirty percent more staff than his walk-in-only establishments, a distinction he believes policymakers consistently overlook.

Transportation researcher Dr. Yumiko Tanaka of McGill University occupies a decidedly more measured position, suggesting that outright bans constitute an overly blunt instrument for what is, at its core, a nuanced urban design challenge. Tanaka advocates instead for performance-based zoning regulations that would mandate electric vehicle queuing infrastructure, emissions thresholds, and landscaped pedestrian buffers around such facilities. "The question confronting planners is not whether drive-throughs belong in modern cities, but rather under what precise conditions they can coexist with the livability objectives those cities profess to champion," Tanaka maintains.

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. environmental consequences of vehicular emissions generated within urban centres
- 2. competing perspectives on whether drive-throughs are compatible with contemporary urban planning
- 3. economic advantages municipalities could derive from restricting automobile-dependent commercial establishments
- 4. regulatory powers that Canadian municipalities currently hold over commercial zoning decisions
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. environmental consequences of vehicular emissions generated within urban centres: While emissions are mentioned via the Toronto survey, they are only one supporting detail, not the article's central focus.
✅ 2. competing perspectives on whether drive-throughs are compatible with contemporary urban planning: The article presents Ramachandran's opposition, Mulholland's defence, and Tanaka's middle ground — three distinct viewpoints on drive-throughs' place in modern cities.
❌ 3. economic advantages municipalities could derive from restricting automobile-dependent commercial establishments: Economic aspects appear through Mulholland's employment figures, but the article does not argue for economic advantages of restrictions.
❌ 4. regulatory powers that Canadian municipalities currently hold over commercial zoning decisions: Regulation is discussed as a tool, but the article is about the broader debate, not the scope of municipal authority itself.

**Q2:** None
- 1. counterproductive because they shift traffic complications rather than resolve them
- 2. justified provided that alternative accessible service models are concurrently implemented
- 3. necessary to safeguard residential neighbourhoods from commercial encroachment
- 4. premature given insufficient evidence linking drive-throughs to broader congestion patterns
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. counterproductive because they shift traffic complications rather than resolve them: Mulholland states that eliminating drive-throughs 'merely displaces' demand, compelling customers to 'circle parking lots or double-park,' which 'exacerbates the very congestion municipalities claim to be mitigating.'
❌ 2. justified provided that alternative accessible service models are concurrently implemented: Mulholland does not concede the justification of restrictions under any conditions; he defends drive-throughs outright.
❌ 3. necessary to safeguard residential neighbourhoods from commercial encroachment: Mulholland never supports restrictions as necessary; he argues the opposite.
❌ 4. premature given insufficient evidence linking drive-throughs to broader congestion patterns: Mulholland does not question the evidence; he argues that bans themselves create congestion, not that the link is unproven.

**Q3:** None
- 1. fundamentally opposed but willing to consider phased elimination timelines
- 2. aligned with Ramachandran's view that they are architecturally obsolete
- 3. conditionally supportive provided they meet stringent operational standards
- 4. primarily concerned with their detrimental impact on electric vehicle adoption rates
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. fundamentally opposed but willing to consider phased elimination timelines: Tanaka explicitly rejects outright bans, calling them 'an overly blunt instrument,' so she is not fundamentally opposed in a way that seeks elimination.
❌ 2. aligned with Ramachandran's view that they are architecturally obsolete: Tanaka occupies a 'decidedly more measured position' and does not characterise drive-throughs as anachronistic the way Ramachandran does.
✅ 3. conditionally supportive provided they meet stringent operational standards: Tanaka advocates 'performance-based zoning regulations' with mandated EV infrastructure, emissions thresholds, and pedestrian buffers — acceptance conditional on strict standards.
❌ 4. primarily concerned with their detrimental impact on electric vehicle adoption rates: Tanaka mentions EV queuing infrastructure as one component of her proposal, not as her primary concern; her focus is broader urban livability.

**Q4:** None
- 1. Requiring drive-through operators to install emissions-reduction technology at existing sites
- 2. Conducting additional studies before implementing any substantive land-use changes
- 3. Permitting drive-throughs solely in areas with pre-existing low pedestrian traffic
- 4. Converting drive-through corridors into higher-density residential and commercial developments
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. Requiring drive-through operators to install emissions-reduction technology at existing sites: This incremental modification aligns more with Tanaka's performance-based approach; Ramachandran views the entire model as incompatible, not merely in need of upgrades.
❌ 2. Conducting additional studies before implementing any substantive land-use changes: Ramachandran already expresses firm opposition, calling the trade-off 'increasingly untenable'; she would not advocate delays.
❌ 3. Permitting drive-throughs solely in areas with pre-existing low pedestrian traffic: Ramachandran's criticism targets the model itself as fragmenting walkability, and she would not endorse their presence even in low-traffic areas given her densification priorities.
✅ 4. Converting drive-through corridors into higher-density residential and commercial developments: Ramachandran specifically states that the land footprint of a drive-through corridor 'could accommodate mixed-use infill development serving considerably more residents and enterprises.'

**Q5:** None
- 1. prompting policy deliberations in additional Canadian municipalities
- 2. convincing franchise operators to voluntarily redesign their facilities
- 3. accelerating Vancouver's existing drive-through prohibition measures
- 4. establishing nationally recognised emissions benchmarks for commercial properties
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. prompting policy deliberations in additional Canadian municipalities: The article states the findings 'prompted several Canadian municipalities to contemplate similar restrictions,' directly supporting this option.
❌ 2. convincing franchise operators to voluntarily redesign their facilities: The article never indicates that franchise operators were persuaded to alter their designs; Mulholland actively defends drive-throughs.
❌ 3. accelerating Vancouver's existing drive-through prohibition measures: No Vancouver prohibition is mentioned; Ramachandran consults for Vancouver's planning department, but no specific ban is described.
❌ 4. establishing nationally recognised emissions benchmarks for commercial properties: The survey's figures are cited locally; no national benchmarks are discussed in the article.

**Q6:** None
- 1. unsustainable
- 2. outdated
- 3. unregulated
- 4. inefficient
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. unsustainable: While Ramachandran calls the trade-off 'increasingly untenable,' she characterises the drive-through model itself as 'anachronistic,' meaning old-fashioned, not unsustainable.
✅ 2. outdated: Ramachandran explicitly describes drive-throughs as 'an anachronistic model of commercial design,' and 'outdated' is the most precise paraphrase of 'anachronistic.'
❌ 3. unregulated: The lack of regulation is more relevant to Tanaka's argument about performance-based standards, not Ramachandran's fundamental objection to the model itself.
❌ 4. inefficient: Ramachandran's critique is not about operational inefficiency but about the model being historically obsolete and incompatible with current urban goals.

**Q7:** None
- 1. pedestrians navigating congested streetscapes
- 2. families seeking affordable dining options
- 3. individuals who face physical barriers
- 4. commuters in densely populated neighbourhoods
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. pedestrians navigating congested streetscapes: Mulholland defends drive-throughs for people who cannot navigate storefronts, not for pedestrians in congested areas.
❌ 2. families seeking affordable dining options: While Mulholland mentions 'working families,' his accessibility argument centres on mobility, not affordability.
✅ 3. individuals who face physical barriers: Mulholland specifically cites 'individuals with mobility constraints who cannot readily navigate traditional storefronts,' meaning those with physical barriers to access.
❌ 4. commuters in densely populated neighbourhoods: Mulholland's argument is about mobility limitations, not commuters or neighbourhood density.

**Q8:** None
- 1. eliminating demand
- 2. imposing restrictions
- 3. regulating standards
- 4. increasing density
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. eliminating demand: Tanaka does not advocate eliminating demand; Mulholland actually warns that banning drive-throughs fails to do this.
❌ 2. imposing restrictions: Tanaka explicitly critiques 'outright bans' as too blunt and instead favours conditional rules — merely 'imposing restrictions' is too vague and negative.
✅ 3. regulating standards: Tanaka advocates 'performance-based zoning regulations' with emissions thresholds and infrastructure mandates — in essence, regulating standards rather than banning outright.
❌ 4. increasing density: While density is relevant to Ramachandran's argument, Tanaka's proposal centres on operational conditions, not densification.

**Q9:** None
- 1. weigh multiple perspectives and trade-offs
- 2. prioritise environmental objectives exclusively
- 3. defer to commercial stakeholders entirely
- 4. follow the Minneapolis prohibition model
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. weigh multiple perspectives and trade-offs: The commenter appreciates viewpoints from all three experts and concludes that 'this debate warrants considerably more nuance,' implying cities should balance competing considerations.
❌ 2. prioritise environmental objectives exclusively: The commenter acknowledges Mulholland's accessibility concerns, so an exclusively environmental focus contradicts the comment's balanced tone.
❌ 3. defer to commercial stakeholders entirely: The commenter also finds Ramachandran's urban design argument persuasive, so deferring entirely to business interests is inconsistent.
❌ 4. follow the Minneapolis prohibition model: The commenter explicitly agrees with Tanaka that blanket prohibitions are not the answer, ruling out Minneapolis-style bans.

**Q10:** None
- 1. banned outright in residential zones
- 2. replaced with pedestrian-only alternatives
- 3. studied more thoroughly before regulation
- 4. redesigned to meet contemporary standards
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. banned outright in residential zones: The commenter states drive-throughs are 'neither inherently beneficial nor detrimental' and criticises blanket prohibitions, making outright bans inconsistent.
❌ 2. replaced with pedestrian-only alternatives: Full replacement contradicts the commenter's moderate stance that drive-throughs can remain under the right conditions.
❌ 3. studied more thoroughly before regulation: The commenter supports action aligned with Tanaka's conditional approach, not further delay through additional study.
✅ 4. redesigned to meet contemporary standards: The commenter says drive-throughs need to 'reflect evolving urban priorities,' and redesigning them to meet modern standards aligns with the balanced, reform-oriented tone of the comment.

## Exam 54 — Part 7: Family Doctor Recommendation

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Nadia,

Welcome to Ottawa! I'm so glad you and Tariq finally made the move from Dubai. I remember how nervous you were about starting over in a new country, but I promise you'll love it here once you settle in.

I wanted to reach out because you mentioned during our call last week that you were having trouble finding a family doctor. I know how frustrating that can be, especially when you're new to the city and don't know where to start. The truth is, there's actually a shortage of family doctors across Ontario right now, so many clinics aren't accepting new patients. That's exactly why I wanted to help before you end up relying on walk-in clinics for everything.

I'd like to recommend my own family doctor, Dr. Samantha Beaulieu, at the Riverside Health Clinic on Bank Street. She's been our family doctor for about six years now, and we've always been impressed with her patience and thoroughness. When my daughter Lily had recurring ear infections two winters ago, Dr. Beaulieu took the time to investigate the underlying cause rather than just prescribing antibiotics each time. It turned out Lily had an allergy we didn't know about, and once we addressed that, the infections stopped completely. I was so grateful for her attention to detail.

Now, there's also another doctor at the same clinic, Dr. Michael Tran, who is accepting new patients as well. He joined the practice last September and has a great reputation, though I haven't seen him personally. My neighbour, Carol, switched to Dr. Tran from a clinic on Elgin Street and says he's wonderful with her elderly parents. So either doctor would be a solid choice, but I'd personally suggest Dr. Beaulieu since I can speak from experience.

The clinic is open Monday through Saturday, and I believe they require new patients to bring their health card and any medical records from their previous physician. Since you just arrived, you'll want to apply for your OHIP card first — there's a ServiceOntario office near Rideau Centre that processes applications fairly quickly.

Why don't we grab coffee at that little bakery on Sparks Street sometime next week? I can walk you through the registration process and even help you fill out the forms if you need. It would be lovely to catch up properly after all these months.

Take care,
Priya

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** What is the main purpose of Priya's email?
- 1. To welcome Nadia to a new neighbourhood.
- 2. To describe the healthcare system in Ontario.
- 3. To help Nadia find medical care.
- 4. To invite Nadia for coffee next week.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. To welcome Nadia to a new neighbourhood: While Priya does welcome Nadia to Ottawa, the bulk of the email is about recommending doctors, not welcoming her to a neighbourhood.
❌ 2. To describe the healthcare system in Ontario: Priya briefly mentions the doctor shortage, but the email's focus is on specific recommendations for Nadia, not a general description of the system.
✅ 3. To help Nadia find medical care: Priya writes specifically to recommend doctors at Riverside Health Clinic, explain the registration process, and offer to help with forms — all aimed at helping Nadia find a family doctor.
❌ 4. To invite Nadia for coffee next week: The coffee invitation is only mentioned at the end as a secondary point; the main purpose is the doctor recommendation.

**Q2:** Priya learned about Nadia's difficulty finding a doctor .....

- 2. when Nadia visited the clinic.
- 3. from Nadia's email last month.
- 4. when they met for coffee.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. during a recent phone conversation: Priya says 'you mentioned during our call last week that you were having trouble finding a family doctor.'
❌ 2. when Nadia visited the clinic: There is no mention of Nadia visiting any clinic; she has been struggling to find a doctor.
❌ 3. from Nadia's email last month: The text says 'our call last week,' not an email or last month.
❌ 4. when they met for coffee: They haven't met for coffee yet — Priya is proposing they meet next week.

**Q3:** Dr. Beaulieu helped Lily by .....
- 1. prescribing stronger antibiotics each visit.
- 2. referring her to an ear specialist.
- 3. treating her recurring winter colds.
- 4. identifying a hidden cause of her illness.

- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. prescribing stronger antibiotics each visit: Dr. Beaulieu did the opposite — instead of 'just prescribing antibiotics each time,' she investigated the underlying cause.
❌ 2. referring her to an ear specialist: No referral to a specialist is mentioned; Dr. Beaulieu herself investigated the cause.
❌ 3. treating her recurring winter colds: Lily had recurring ear infections, not colds; the underlying issue was an allergy.
✅ 4. identifying a hidden cause of her illness: Dr. Beaulieu 'took the time to investigate the underlying cause' and discovered Lily had an allergy they didn't know about.

❌ 4. He is highly recommended by Priya's daughter: Priya's daughter Lily is mentioned only in relation to Dr. Beaulieu; the recommendation for Dr. Tran came from Priya's neighbour Carol.

## Exam 54 — Part 9: Starling Murmuration Mechanics

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. A murmuration refers to the mesmerizing aerial phenomenon in which thousands—sometimes hundreds of thousands—of European starlings, known scientifically as Sturnus vulgaris, fly in coordinated, fluid formations across the evening sky. These spectacles occur predominantly during autumn and winter, as starlings gather at dusk before settling into communal roost sites for the night. Murmurations have been documented throughout Europe, particularly in the United Kingdom, Denmark, and the Netherlands. One of the largest recorded events took place over the Somerset Levels in England, where an estimated six million birds formed a single, continuously shifting mass visible from several kilometres away.

B. For decades, scientists struggled to explain how starlings achieve such remarkable synchronization without a designated leader. In 2010, researchers at the University of Rome analyzed three-dimensional footage of murmurations and discovered that each bird adjusts its movement based on its nearest seven neighbours, regardless of the flock's overall density. This principle, known as topological distance, enables information about directional changes to propagate through the group almost instantaneously, at speeds approaching 40 metres per second. The resulting collective behaviour closely resembles phase transitions in physics, which has attracted considerable interest from both physicists and mathematicians.

C. The primary function of murmurations is widely believed to be predator defence. Raptors such as peregrine falcons find it exceedingly difficult to isolate and capture a single bird within a rapidly shifting mass. Studies conducted in Denmark between 2014 and 2018 confirmed that starling flocks exhibiting tighter, more coordinated formations experienced significantly fewer predation events. In addition to safety, murmurations serve a thermoregulatory purpose; by roosting together in vast numbers after the aerial display, starlings conserve body heat during cold nights. Furthermore, some ornithologists suggest that murmurations function as information hubs where birds exchange knowledge about productive feeding sites.

D. Despite their spectacular nature, murmurations have become less frequent in certain regions due to declining starling populations. In the United Kingdom, starling numbers have fallen by approximately 66 percent since the mid-1970s, prompting the species to be placed on the Red List of conservation concern. Habitat loss, reduced insect availability, and changes in agricultural practices are considered the principal contributing factors. Conversely, the study of murmurations has yielded valuable insights beyond ornithology. Engineers have applied swarm algorithms derived from starling behaviour to improve drone coordination, traffic-flow management, and the modelling of crowd dynamics in densely populated urban environments.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

- 5. E

- **Explanation:** Paragraph D states that 'changes in agricultural practices' are among the principal factors behind declining starling populations, and that murmurations 'have become less frequent in certain regions' as a result. Combining these two facts yields the inference that modern farming has indirectly reduced murmuration occurrences.

## Exam 54 — Part 10: Overnight Park Camping

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
When Parks Canada reported a 38% surge in overnight camping permits over the past five years, the figure prompted renewed scrutiny of whether the nation's most treasured wilderness areas can sustainably accommodate the ever-growing influx of visitors seeking extended stays. The tension between preserving ecological integrity and democratizing access to nature has, in recent years, intensified considerably, with stakeholders on multiple sides articulating increasingly divergent positions on the matter.

Terrain ecologist Dr. Naomi Whitford, who has spent nearly two decades studying soil degradation in Banff and Jasper, contends that the cumulative impact of overnight campers far exceeds what casual day-use visitors inflict upon fragile alpine ecosystems. "Compacted root systems, fire-scarred undergrowth, and waste contamination in riparian zones—these are not problems that resolve themselves between camping seasons," Whitford asserts. She further notes that certain high-altitude meadows, having been subjected to relentless foot traffic and impromptu tent placements, now exhibit diminished vegetative regeneration that could require decades of remediation.

Conversely, outdoor recreation advocate and former park warden Julian Frey suggests that restricting overnight camping would disproportionately penalize lower-income families for whom multi-day excursions represent their sole viable vacation option. Frey, who now directs the Canadian Wilderness Access Coalition, maintains that the solution lies not in curtailing permits but in redistributing demand across underutilized backcountry corridors. "Eliminating overnight stays to protect ecosystems is akin to closing libraries to preserve books—it undermines the very purpose these spaces were established to fulfil," Frey remarks, advocating instead for enhanced trail infrastructure and rotational site closures.

Meanwhile, Lorraine Okafor, a campground operations supervisor in British Columbia's Pacific Rim National Park Reserve, observes that the practical challenges of managing overnight visitors have grown substantially more complex. Overcrowded disposal stations, unauthorized off-trail encampments, and escalating wildlife encounters have strained her already limited staff. Despite these operational burdens, Okafor remains cautiously optimistic that technological innovations—such as satellite-monitored site allocation and biodegradable waste systems—could mitigate much of the environmental toll. "We ought not to sever people's connection with these landscapes altogether," Okafor reflects. "Rather, we must fundamentally reimagine how that connection is structured, lest convenience become the adversary of conservation."

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. the financial strain placed on Parks Canada by rising visitor numbers
- 2. the environmental protocols governing backcountry camping in alpine regions
- 3. the ecological cost of increased campfire usage across national parks
- 4. whether surging overnight camping demand is reconcilable with wilderness preservation
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the financial strain placed on Parks Canada by rising visitor numbers: Financial strain on the agency itself is never discussed; the focus is on ecological and access tensions.
❌ 2. the environmental protocols governing backcountry camping in alpine regions: The article does not describe existing protocols; it debates the broader question of accommodation versus preservation.
❌ 3. the ecological cost of increased campfire usage across national parks: Campfire damage is mentioned only in passing ('fire-scarred undergrowth'); it is not the central issue.
✅ 4. whether surging overnight camping demand is reconcilable with wilderness preservation: The article frames the core tension as 'preserving ecological integrity and democratizing access to nature,' with all three experts addressing this question.

**Q2:** None
- 1. requiring prolonged recovery that current management has failed to address
- 2. relatively minor when compared to industrial pollution affecting parks
- 3. primarily attributable to day-use hikers rather than overnight campers
- 4. easily reversible with adequate seasonal maintenance programs
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. requiring prolonged recovery that current management has failed to address: Whitford states the damage 'could require decades of remediation' and that problems 'are not problems that resolve themselves between camping seasons,' indicating prolonged harm unaddressed by current practices.
❌ 2. relatively minor when compared to industrial pollution affecting parks: Whitford never references industrial pollution; she focuses exclusively on the severity of camper impact.
❌ 3. primarily attributable to day-use hikers rather than overnight campers: Whitford explicitly says overnight campers inflict damage that 'far exceeds what casual day-use visitors' cause.
❌ 4. easily reversible with adequate seasonal maintenance programs: Whitford says damage does not resolve itself between seasons and may need decades of remediation — the opposite of easily reversible.

**Q3:** None
- 1. parks and libraries serve fundamentally different societal functions
- 2. overnight visitors contribute more to park conservation than day users do
- 3. lower-income families rely on public parks as heavily as they do on libraries
- 4. prohibiting access contradicts the foundational purpose of national parks
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. parks and libraries serve fundamentally different societal functions: Frey's analogy equates the two, not differentiates them; both exist to provide public access.
❌ 2. overnight visitors contribute more to park conservation than day users do: Frey's analogy concerns access rights, not the relative conservation contributions of different visitor types.
❌ 3. lower-income families rely on public parks as heavily as they do on libraries: Although Frey mentions lower-income families, the analogy itself is about purpose, not the degree of reliance.
✅ 4. prohibiting access contradicts the foundational purpose of national parks: Frey says eliminating overnight stays 'undermines the very purpose these spaces were established to fulfil,' directly paralleling closing libraries to preserve books.

**Q4:** None
- 1. She believes environmental damage from camping has been greatly exaggerated by ecologists.
- 2. She endorses a reformed approach to visitor management rather than outright exclusion.
- 3. She advocates for immediate suspension of overnight permits until technology catches up.
- 4. She favours prioritizing staff expansion over investing in technological solutions.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. She believes environmental damage from camping has been greatly exaggerated by ecologists: Okafor acknowledges escalating wildlife encounters and disposal issues — she does not dismiss ecological damage.
✅ 2. She endorses a reformed approach to visitor management rather than outright exclusion: Okafor says 'we ought not to sever people's connection' but must 'fundamentally reimagine how that connection is structured' — reform, not removal.
❌ 3. She advocates for immediate suspension of overnight permits until technology catches up: Okafor never calls for suspending permits; she remains 'cautiously optimistic' about technological solutions while camping continues.
❌ 4. She favours prioritizing staff expansion over investing in technological solutions: Okafor specifically highlights satellite-monitored allocation and biodegradable waste systems, not staff hiring.

**Q5:** None
- 1. has prompted Parks Canada to impose a moratorium on new campground construction
- 2. is primarily concentrated in low-elevation forested areas near major highways
- 3. extends beyond surface-level disturbance to affect subsurface plant systems
- 4. has been systematically documented by Lorraine Okafor's operational staff
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. has prompted Parks Canada to impose a moratorium on new campground construction: No moratorium is mentioned; Parks Canada's response is limited to reporting permit figures.
❌ 2. is primarily concentrated in low-elevation forested areas near major highways: Whitford describes damage to 'high-altitude meadows,' not low-elevation zones.
✅ 3. extends beyond surface-level disturbance to affect subsurface plant systems: Whitford references 'compacted root systems' and 'diminished vegetative regeneration,' both indicating subsurface harm beyond visible surface damage.
❌ 4. has been systematically documented by Lorraine Okafor's operational staff: Okafor describes operational challenges but never claims her staff has documented ecological harm.

**Q6:** None
- 1. overstated
- 2. troubling
- 3. disheartening
- 4. unsurprising
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. overstated: The commenter calls Whitford's evidence 'sobering' and warns about 'unchecked expansion,' indicating genuine concern rather than a belief the problem is exaggerated.
✅ 2. troubling: The commenter treats the issue with gravity throughout — calling the evidence 'sobering' and cautioning about ecological risks — consistent with finding the predicament troubling.
❌ 3. disheartening: Although the commenter is concerned, they also identify 'a promising middle path' in Okafor's approach, suggesting they are not dispirited — merely worried.
❌ 4. unsurprising: The commenter's deeply engaged and reflective tone conveys active concern, not resigned familiarity with a predictable situation.

**Q7:** None
- 1. access ought to be reorganized rather than revoked
- 2. all overnight permits should be immediately revoked
- 3. ecological concerns are largely overstated by researchers
- 4. day visitors cause equivalent environmental harm
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. access ought to be reorganized rather than revoked: Frey advocates 'redistributing demand across underutilized backcountry corridors' instead of curtailing permits — reorganization over elimination.
❌ 2. all overnight permits should be immediately revoked: This is the opposite of Frey's position; he explicitly opposes restricting overnight camping.
❌ 3. ecological concerns are largely overstated by researchers: Frey does not dispute ecological damage; he simply argues restriction is the wrong remedy.
❌ 4. day visitors cause equivalent environmental harm: Frey never compares the impact of day visitors to overnight campers.

**Q8:** None
- 1. academically rigorous
- 2. commercially motivated
- 3. unduly pessimistic
- 4. level-headed
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. academically rigorous: Okafor speaks from operational experience, not academic research; Whitford is the academic voice.
❌ 2. commercially motivated: Okafor is a campground supervisor concerned with environmental and operational challenges, not commercial gain.
❌ 3. unduly pessimistic: The commenter describes Okafor as offering 'a promising middle path,' which contradicts characterizing her as pessimistic.
✅ 4. level-headed: The commenter says Okafor 'refuses to champion either extreme,' which aligns with being balanced and level-headed.

**Q9:** None
- 1. simply hiring additional wardens
- 2. completely banning overnight campfires
- 3. emerging technological interventions
- 4. reducing overall park operating hours
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. simply hiring additional wardens: Okafor mentions strained staff but does not propose hiring as her primary solution; she highlights technology.
❌ 2. completely banning overnight campfires: Campfire bans are never suggested by Okafor; her focus is on monitoring and waste innovation.
✅ 3. emerging technological interventions: Okafor expresses optimism about 'satellite-monitored site allocation and biodegradable waste systems' — technological solutions to environmental challenges.
❌ 4. reducing overall park operating hours: Shortened hours are never proposed by any figure in the article.

**Q10:** None
- 1. modestly affecting the biodiversity within
- 2. permanently undermining the resilience of
- 3. irreversibly transforming the character of
- 4. temporarily disrupting the seasonal cycles of
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. modestly affecting the biodiversity within: The commenter's warning tone and Whitford's evidence of damage requiring 'decades of remediation' rule out a characterization of modest impact.
✅ 2. permanently undermining the resilience of: The commenter warns that unchecked growth 'risks' harming the ecosystems parks exist to protect, aligning with Whitford's evidence of deep, lasting degradation to root systems and vegetative regeneration.
❌ 3. irreversibly transforming the character of: While plausible, Whitford's evidence points to degradation and diminished regeneration rather than transformation; the concern is about weakening ecosystems, not altering their fundamental nature.
❌ 4. temporarily disrupting the seasonal cycles of: The commenter and Whitford both stress long-term consequences; 'temporarily' contradicts the decades-long remediation timeline described in the article.

## Exam 55 — Part 7: High School Reunion

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Michelle,

I know this must seem out of the blue, but I found our old Westdale Secondary yearbook from 2006 while cleaning out my parents' garage in Hamilton last weekend. The moment I saw your photo next to mine on the volleyball team page, I felt a wave of nostalgia that caught me off guard. I tracked down your email through our class reunion page, and I just had to reach out.

It has been almost eighteen years since graduation, and so much has changed. After high school, I moved to Ottawa for university and ended up staying there for nearly a decade. I studied environmental science at Carleton, which surprised everyone because you probably remember I was terrible at biology in grade eleven. It was actually Mr. Patterson's grade twelve geography class that inspired me to pursue that path. He truly changed the way I saw the world.

About seven years ago, I relocated to Toronto for a position with a conservation organization. I am now working as a project coordinator for a wetland restoration initiative near Lake Simcoe. It is demanding but incredibly fulfilling. I got married in 2019 to someone I met through a mutual friend at a conference in Kingston. We have a three-year-old daughter named Lily who keeps us wonderfully exhausted.

I was flipping through the yearbook and noticed the message you wrote on the back cover. You mentioned wanting to open a bakery someday. Did you ever follow that dream? I found a photo of us at the grade twelve formal as well, you in that green dress and me in my borrowed suit that was two sizes too big. I laughed so hard when I saw it. I had forgotten how ridiculous I looked that night.

The reason I am writing, beyond nostalgia, is that I will be in Hamilton visiting my parents over the Thanksgiving weekend in October. My mother mentioned that your family still lives on Dundurn Street, so I am hoping you might still be in the area. I would love to grab coffee at that little cafe near Bayfront Park if you are free. It would mean so much to catch up after all these years.

I really hope to hear from you, Michelle. Finding that yearbook reminded me how important those friendships were.

Warmly,
Jessica

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** What is the main purpose of this email?
- 1. To return something that belongs to Michelle.
- 2. To share details about Jessica's wedding.
- 3. To reconnect and suggest meeting in person.
- 4. To invite Michelle to a class reunion event.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. To return something that belongs to Michelle: Jessica found her own yearbook; she is not returning anything to Michelle.
❌ 2. To share details about Jessica's wedding: While Jessica mentions her marriage briefly, the email's main purpose is reconnecting and arranging a visit.
✅ 3. To reconnect and suggest meeting in person: Jessica reaches out after finding the yearbook and proposes grabbing coffee in Hamilton over Thanksgiving weekend.
❌ 4. To invite Michelle to a class reunion event: Jessica found Michelle through the reunion page, but she is not organizing or inviting her to a reunion.

**Q2:** Jessica found Michelle's contact information through .....
- 1. a class reunion website.
- 2. her mother in Hamilton.
- 3. a social media post.
- 4. a mutual friend at a conference.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. a class reunion website: Jessica says 'I tracked down your email through our class reunion page.'
❌ 2. her mother in Hamilton: Jessica's mother mentioned Michelle's family still lives on Dundurn Street, but that is not how she found the email address.
❌ 3. a social media post: No social media is mentioned in the email; it was a class reunion page.
❌ 4. a mutual friend at a conference: Jessica met her spouse through a mutual friend at a conference, not Michelle.

**Q3:** Jessica chose to study environmental science because of .....
- 1. her success in grade eleven biology.
- 2. her experience at university in Ottawa.
- 3. her work with a conservation organization.
- 4. a class she took in her final year of high school.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. her success in grade eleven biology: Jessica says she was 'terrible at biology in grade eleven,' so it was not an inspiration.
❌ 2. her experience at university in Ottawa: She studied environmental science at Carleton; the inspiration came before university, not during it.
❌ 3. her work with a conservation organization: She joined the organization after her studies; it did not inspire her original career choice.
✅ 4. a class she took in her final year of high school: Jessica says 'Mr. Patterson's grade twelve geography class' inspired her to pursue environmental science.

**Q4:** In the yearbook, Michelle had written about .....
- 1. how much fun the volleyball team was.
- 2. a dream she had for her future career.
- 3. staying in touch with Jessica after school.
- 4. the grade twelve formal dance.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. how much fun the volleyball team was: The volleyball team photo is mentioned, but Michelle's written message was about something else.
✅ 2. a dream she had for her future career: Jessica says Michelle 'mentioned wanting to open a bakery someday' in her yearbook message.
❌ 3. staying in touch with Jessica after school: No such message is described; Jessica found the email through a reunion page, not from the yearbook.
❌ 4. the grade twelve formal dance: The formal photo is mentioned separately by Jessica; it was not part of Michelle's written message.

**Q5:** When Jessica saw the photo from the formal, she probably felt .....
- 1. amused by her own appearance.
- 2. embarrassed about Michelle's dress.
- 3. sad that high school was over.
- 4. proud of how well the suit fit.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. amused by her own appearance: Jessica says 'I laughed so hard when I saw it' and 'how ridiculous I looked that night' — she found her own look funny.
❌ 2. embarrassed about Michelle's dress: Jessica describes Michelle's green dress without any negative comment; her amusement was directed at herself.
❌ 3. sad that high school was over: The photo made her laugh, not feel sad; the nostalgia in the email is warm, not sorrowful.
❌ 4. proud of how well the suit fit: The suit was 'two sizes too big' and she called herself 'ridiculous' — the opposite of pride in the fit.

**Q6:** Jessica is planning to visit Hamilton .....
- 1. during the summer holidays.
- 2. for her daughter's birthday.
- 3. over a long weekend in October.
- 4. when she relocates from Toronto.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. during the summer holidays: Jessica specifies Thanksgiving weekend, which is in October, not summer.
❌ 2. for her daughter's birthday: Lily is mentioned but not her birthday; the visit is to see Jessica's parents.
✅ 3. over a long weekend in October: Jessica says she 'will be in Hamilton visiting my parents over the Thanksgiving weekend in October.'
❌ 4. when she relocates from Toronto: There is no mention of Jessica planning to leave Toronto; she is only visiting for the weekend.

**Q7:** None
- 1. pleasant
- 2. wonderful
- 3. unfortunate
- 4. confusing
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. pleasant: While positive, 'pleasant' is too mild for Michelle's enthusiastic tone — she says 'I cannot wait to see you,' suggesting strong excitement.
✅ 2. wonderful: Michelle is clearly thrilled to hear from Jessica — she responds with great enthusiasm and eagerly agrees to meet, making 'wonderful' the best fit.
❌ 3. unfortunate: Michelle's reply is enthusiastic, not regretful; she does not view the contact as unwelcome.
❌ 4. confusing: Michelle clearly remembers Jessica and their shared history; there is nothing confusing about the situation.

**Q8:** None
- 1. thoughts
- 2. moments
- 3. concerns
- 4. memories
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. thoughts: While 'thoughts' is neutral, the natural collocation is 'brought back memories,' not 'brought back thoughts.'
❌ 2. moments: 'Brought back moments' is not a standard English expression; 'memories' is the natural word paired with 'brought back.'
❌ 3. concerns: Michelle is reminiscing happily, not expressing worries about anything from Westdale.
✅ 4. memories: Jessica's email is full of fond recollections — the volleyball team, the formal, yearbook messages — which would naturally trigger Michelle's own memories, and 'brought back memories' is the standard collocation.

**Q9:** None
- 1. geography
- 2. biology
- 3. environmental science
- 4. conservation
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. geography: Mr. Patterson's geography class inspired Jessica, but she studied environmental science at university, not geography.
❌ 2. biology: Jessica was terrible at biology in grade eleven; she did not study it at Carleton.
✅ 3. environmental science: Jessica's main email states she 'studied environmental science at Carleton,' which is what Michelle is impressed by.
❌ 4. conservation: Jessica works in conservation now, but her field of study at university was environmental science.

**Q10:** None
- 1. meet up for coffee
- 2. visit your parents together
- 3. bring Lily to the bakery
- 4. tour around Westdale Secondary
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. meet up for coffee: Jessica specifically proposed grabbing coffee at the cafe near Bayfront Park, and Michelle is accepting this invitation.
❌ 2. visit your parents together: Jessica is visiting her parents, but she invited Michelle for coffee, not to visit her parents.
❌ 3. bring Lily to the bakery: While both the daughter and bakery are mentioned, Jessica never suggested bringing Lily to the bakery.
❌ 4. tour around Westdale Secondary: Neither email mentions visiting the old school; the proposed plan is coffee at Bayfront Park.

**Q11:** None
- 1. busier
- 2. lovelier
- 3. noisier
- 4. more expensive
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. busier: A busier cafe could mean no free seats. This does not support Michelle's happy agreement to meet there.
✅ 2. lovelier: Jessica asks to meet at "that little cafe near Bayfront Park". Michelle says the cafe "is still there" and adds "I cannot wait to see you!" A warm, positive word is needed to recommend the place, and "lovelier than ever" does this.
❌ 3. noisier: Jessica wants to "catch up after all these years". A noisier cafe would make talking harder, so it would not encourage the visit.
❌ 4. more expensive: Higher prices would discourage the meeting. This does not match Michelle's enthusiastic reply.

✅ 4. the staff there will care for Baxter professionally: 'In good hands' means being looked after competently and reliably — Karen is reassuring Tom that the salon's experienced groomers will take proper care of his nervous dog.

## Exam 55 — Part 9: Irish Bog Butter

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. Bog butter refers to ancient deposits of butter—or occasionally animal fat—that have been unearthed from peat bogs across Ireland and parts of Scotland and Scandinavia. To date, over 430 specimens have been recovered in Ireland alone, with some dating back more than 3,000 years. These deposits, often discovered by turf cutters during peat harvesting, typically appear as waxy, pale masses enclosed in wooden containers, animal skins, or woven baskets. The largest specimen ever found, discovered in County Kildare in 2009, weighed approximately 35 kilograms. Remarkably, many of these ancient deposits retain a recognisable, albeit pungent, buttery odour.

B. Scholars have proposed several explanations for the practice of burying butter in bogs. In early medieval Ireland, butter was a valuable commodity, frequently used to pay taxes and rents to landlords. Consequently, concealing it in a bog may have served as a means of safeguarding wealth from raiders or thieves. Other researchers contend that the burial was primarily a method of food preservation, as the cold, acidic, and oxygen-deprived conditions of peat bogs could keep dairy products edible for extended periods. A third hypothesis suggests that some deposits were votive offerings to deities, intended to ensure agricultural prosperity.

C. The remarkable preservation of bog butter is attributable to the unique chemistry of peat bogs. These wetlands are highly acidic, with pH levels comparable to vinegar, and contain negligible dissolved oxygen. Together, these conditions inhibit the growth of bacteria and microorganisms that would ordinarily cause organic matter to decompose. Chemical analyses conducted at University College Dublin revealed that ancient bog butter retains many of the same fatty acids found in modern dairy products, particularly palmitic and oleic acid. Interestingly, prolonged immersion in peat also imparts a distinctive flavour, transforming the butter into something resembling a strong, aged cheese.

D. The National Museum of Ireland in Dublin houses one of the world's most extensive collections of bog butter, exhibiting specimens alongside other organic artefacts recovered from Irish wetlands. New discoveries continue to emerge regularly; in 2016, a turf cutter in County Meath unearthed a deposit weighing approximately ten kilograms and estimated to be around 2,000 years old. Several adventurous food historians have sampled ancient bog butter, generally describing its taste as intensely pungent yet still recognisably dairy-based. These ongoing finds underscore the archaeological significance of Ireland's diminishing peatlands, which scientists increasingly regard as irreplaceable repositories of cultural heritage.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

### Questions & Answer Key

## Exam 55 — Part 10: Inclusive Restaurant Pricing

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
When Marisol Devereaux, proprietor of a mid-range bistro in downtown Vancouver, eliminated gratuities from her establishment last spring and folded a twenty-percent service surcharge into menu prices, she anticipated a modest degree of customer pushback—what she did not foresee was the polarizing debate her decision would ignite among restaurateurs, servers, and patrons across the country. A survey conducted by the Canadian Restaurant Association in 2023 revealed that roughly 62 percent of diners expressed discomfort with the ambiguity surrounding expected tip amounts, yet paradoxically, nearly half of those same respondents admitted they would be reluctant to patronize venues whose listed prices appeared substantially higher than competitors, even when informed that no additional gratuity was expected.

Devereaux, whose revenue has remained stable since the transition, contends that the conventional tipping model perpetuates an inequitable wage structure in which front-of-house staff reap the lion's share of customer generosity while kitchen personnel—whose contributions are no less indispensable—subsist on comparatively meagre compensation. "Embedding the service charge into the price has allowed me to distribute earnings more equitably among all team members, which in turn has reduced back-of-house turnover by nearly forty percent," Devereaux notes. She acknowledges, however, that certain veteran servers initially resisted the change, perceiving it as a curtailment of their earning potential.

Hospitality economist Ronan Whitfield, who has examined tipping practices across fourteen countries, cautions that the service-included model, while ostensibly more transparent, can inadvertently diminish the motivational link between exemplary service and financial reward. "When gratuities are absorbed into pricing, the incentive for servers to exceed baseline expectations becomes somewhat attenuated," Whitfield observes, adding that establishments adopting this approach must therefore invest more heavily in performance-based management systems to sustain service quality.

Conversely, Priya Ranganathan, a longtime server at a Toronto fine-dining establishment that recently transitioned to inclusive pricing, maintains that the predictability of her income has alleviated considerable financial anxiety. "Under the old system, a slow Tuesday could devastate my weekly budget, whereas now I can plan my expenses with a degree of certainty that was previously unimaginable," Ranganathan explains. She concedes that some colleagues lament the absence of those exceptionally lucrative Saturday evenings but insists the trade-off favours stability.

Whitfield, reflecting on the broader trajectory of the industry, remains measured in his assessment. "Whether this movement gains sufficient momentum to displace entrenched tipping norms hinges not merely on economic rationality but on deeply ingrained cultural expectations that no single pricing model can easily override."

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. financial consequences of eliminating restaurant service charges
- 2. contentious shift away from conventional tipping in Canadian restaurants
- 3. strategies restaurant owners employ to retain back-of-house staff
- 4. cultural resistance to elevated menu prices across the country
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. financial consequences of eliminating restaurant service charges: While finances are touched upon, the article is broader — it encompasses viewpoints from economists, servers, owners, and survey data on the tipping debate itself.
✅ 2. contentious shift away from conventional tipping in Canadian restaurants: The article centres on Devereaux's decision and the polarizing debate it sparked among various stakeholders regarding the move from tipping to inclusive pricing.
❌ 3. strategies restaurant owners employ to retain back-of-house staff: Staff retention is mentioned as one benefit Devereaux cites, but it is only a supporting detail, not the overarching topic.
❌ 4. cultural resistance to elevated menu prices across the country: Cultural resistance is raised by Whitfield at the end, but the article covers multiple dimensions — equity, motivation, income stability — not merely resistance.

**Q2:** None
- 1. is inherently superior to traditional tipping customs worldwide
- 2. has already proven unsuccessful in the majority of international markets
- 3. should be mandated across the entire Canadian hospitality sector
- 4. necessitates alternative mechanisms to uphold service standards
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. is inherently superior to traditional tipping customs worldwide: Whitfield is 'measured' and 'cautions' about the model — he never claims superiority.
❌ 2. has already proven unsuccessful in the majority of international markets: Whitfield studied fourteen countries but never states the model has broadly failed.
❌ 3. should be mandated across the entire Canadian hospitality sector: Whitfield does not advocate for mandates; he remains analytically cautious.
✅ 4. necessitates alternative mechanisms to uphold service standards: Whitfield explicitly states that establishments adopting inclusive pricing 'must invest more heavily in performance-based management systems to sustain service quality.'

**Q3:** None
- 1. It disproportionately rewards one segment of restaurant employees.
- 2. It guarantees higher overall compensation for all staff members.
- 3. It discourages loyal customers from dining out frequently.
- 4. It has been universally condemned by hospitality economists.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. It disproportionately rewards one segment of restaurant employees.: Devereaux states that tipping causes 'front-of-house staff to reap the lion's share' while kitchen personnel receive comparatively meagre pay — a clear imbalance.
❌ 2. It guarantees higher overall compensation for all staff members.: Devereaux argues the opposite — tipping creates inequity, not a guarantee of higher pay for everyone.
❌ 3. It discourages loyal customers from dining out frequently.: Devereaux does not discuss customer frequency; her focus is on internal wage equity.
❌ 4. It has been universally condemned by hospitality economists.: Only Whitfield is cited, and he offers a nuanced rather than condemnatory view.

**Q4:** None
- 1. unequivocal and dismissive of any potential drawbacks
- 2. reluctant and primarily influenced by peer pressure
- 3. pragmatic and informed by the value of financial consistency
- 4. idealistic and disconnected from real-world economic pressures
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. unequivocal and dismissive of any potential drawbacks: Ranganathan 'concedes that some colleagues lament' losing lucrative evenings, so she acknowledges downsides rather than dismissing them.
❌ 2. reluctant and primarily influenced by peer pressure: Nothing in the article suggests Ranganathan was pressured; she actively champions the change.
✅ 3. pragmatic and informed by the value of financial consistency: Ranganathan values being able to 'plan expenses with a degree of certainty' and weighs the trade-off practically, insisting stability wins — a pragmatic stance.
❌ 4. idealistic and disconnected from real-world economic pressures: Ranganathan speaks directly from lived experience with 'slow Tuesdays' devastating her budget, demonstrating deep awareness of economic realities.

**Q5:** None
- 1. firm convictions that gratuities should be legally abolished
- 2. mistrust toward restaurants that advertise transparent pricing models
- 3. expectations that service quality improves markedly without tipping
- 4. seemingly contradictory attitudes toward tipping and menu pricing
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. firm convictions that gratuities should be legally abolished: The survey shows discomfort with ambiguity but not advocacy for legal abolition of tips.
❌ 2. mistrust toward restaurants that advertise transparent pricing models: Reluctance to patronize higher-priced menus is about perceived cost, not mistrust of transparency.
❌ 3. expectations that service quality improves markedly without tipping: The survey addresses pricing and comfort with tipping norms, not expectations about service quality.
✅ 4. seemingly contradictory attitudes toward tipping and menu pricing: The survey reveals a paradox — diners dislike tipping ambiguity yet are reluctant to patronize restaurants with higher listed prices that include service, which is inherently contradictory.

**Q6:** None
- 1. reckless and poorly calculated
- 2. bold yet commercially sound
- 3. inevitable but ultimately harmful
- 4. generous solely to kitchen staff
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. reckless and poorly calculated: The commenter notes revenue 'hasn't suffered,' contradicting any suggestion of recklessness.
✅ 2. bold yet commercially sound: The commenter acknowledges the decision as a 'radical overhaul' (bold) while noting that revenue remained stable (commercially sound).
❌ 3. inevitable but ultimately harmful: The commenter's tone is favourable, not critical — there is no indication of perceived harm.
❌ 4. generous solely to kitchen staff: Devereaux distributes earnings 'equitably among all team members,' not exclusively to kitchen staff.

**Q7:** None
- 1. equity
- 2. efficiency
- 3. transparency
- 4. profitability
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. equity: Devereaux explicitly states her goal was to 'distribute earnings more equitably among all team members,' making equity her primary concern.
❌ 2. efficiency: While operational improvements may follow, Devereaux's stated motivation centres on fair wages, not efficiency.
❌ 3. transparency: Transparency is more associated with Whitfield's discussion of pricing models, not Devereaux's core motivation.
❌ 4. profitability: Devereaux's revenue remained 'stable,' and her focus was on wage distribution, not increasing profits.

**Q8:** None
- 1. attract more experienced servers
- 2. reduce overall menu prices
- 3. erode the quality of service
- 4. increase customer satisfaction considerably
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. attract more experienced servers: Whitfield warns of diminished motivation, not improved recruitment — experienced servers may actually resist the model.
❌ 2. reduce overall menu prices: The inclusive model raises listed prices; it does not reduce them.
✅ 3. erode the quality of service: Whitfield states that without tips, 'the incentive for servers to exceed baseline expectations becomes somewhat attenuated,' meaning service quality could deteriorate.
❌ 4. increase customer satisfaction considerably: Whitfield expresses concern about declining standards, which would likely reduce rather than increase satisfaction.

**Q9:** None
- 1. higher earnings
- 2. career advancement
- 3. flexible scheduling
- 4. reliable income
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. higher earnings: Ranganathan acknowledges losing exceptionally lucrative nights, so her preference is not for higher earnings but for consistency.
❌ 2. career advancement: Ranganathan discusses income predictability, not professional progression.
❌ 3. flexible scheduling: Scheduling is never mentioned in the article by any source.
✅ 4. reliable income: Ranganathan explicitly values being able to 'plan expenses with a degree of certainty,' and the commenter frames this as preferring reliability over occasional windfalls.

**Q10:** None
- 1. government regulations on wages
- 2. deeply entrenched cultural norms
- 3. widespread misconceptions among diners
- 4. rising operational costs nationwide
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. government regulations on wages: Neither the article nor the commenter identifies government regulation as the primary obstacle.
✅ 2. deeply entrenched cultural norms: Whitfield concludes that success hinges on 'deeply ingrained cultural expectations that no single pricing model can easily override,' which the commenter directly echoes.
❌ 3. widespread misconceptions among diners: The survey shows diners are aware of the issue and hold opinions — the problem is contradictory attitudes, not misconceptions or ignorance.
❌ 4. rising operational costs nationwide: Operational costs are never discussed as an obstacle in the article.
