## Exam 56 — Part 7: Parking Collision Apology

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Marcus,

I hope you're doing well. I wanted to follow up about the minor accident in the parking lot at Riverside Community Centre last Saturday. I know we exchanged insurance information at the scene, but I thought it would be good to write you a note since we've been neighbours on Elm Crescent for over three years now.

First of all, I want to apologize again. The whole thing was entirely my fault. I was backing out of the spot near the east entrance when I hit your rear bumper. I had been distracted because my daughter Sophie called to say she had missed her bus from school, and I was trying to figure out how to pick her up in time. I felt terrible the moment I heard that awful crunch.

I took my car to Brampton Auto Body on Tuesday, and they estimated the repair for my front bumper at around $650. I imagine your damage might be similar, since the dent on your rear panel did not look too deep. I also called my insurance company, Northland Mutual, on Monday morning and filed a claim. The adjuster, a woman named Patricia, told me that since I accepted full responsibility, your claim should be processed without any issues. She said it typically takes about two to three weeks for everything to be approved.

Patricia mentioned that she might need a few photos of your vehicle before the repairs are done. If she has not contacted you yet, she should reach out by the end of this week. Her office is the one on King Street, not the branch on Queen Street, so make sure you contact the right location if you need to call them directly.

On a brighter note, I was really touched by how calm and understanding you were about the whole situation. Most people would have been upset, and honestly, I was pretty shaken up myself. I am grateful we could handle it like good neighbours.

Would you and Linda be free this Saturday evening? I would love to have you both over for dinner. I still owe you for helping me clear the driveway after that big snowfall last February, and now I owe you even more. Consider it a small thank-you for being such a wonderful neighbour through all of this.

Warm regards,
Denise

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** What is the main purpose of this letter?
- 1. To file an insurance claim with Northland Mutual.
- 2. To request that Marcus pay for the car repairs.
- 3. To apologize and update Marcus on the situation.
- 4. To invite Marcus and Linda for a Saturday dinner.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. To file an insurance claim with Northland Mutual: Denise already filed the claim on Monday; the letter is not an insurance document.
❌ 2. To request that Marcus pay for the car repairs: Denise accepts full responsibility and never asks Marcus for money.
✅ 3. To apologize and update Marcus on the situation: Denise apologizes again for the accident and provides updates on the repair estimate, the insurance claim, and what to expect from the adjuster.
❌ 4. To invite Marcus and Linda for a Saturday dinner: The dinner invitation appears at the end as a secondary gesture; the letter's main purpose is the apology and update.

**Q2:** Denise hit Marcus's car because .....
- 1. she was dealing with her daughter's problem.
- 2. the parking spot near the east entrance was too narrow.
- 3. she was on the phone with Northland Mutual.
- 4. she could not see Marcus's car behind her.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. she was dealing with her daughter's problem: Denise says Sophie called to say she had missed her bus and Denise was trying to figure out how to pick her up.
❌ 2. the parking spot near the east entrance was too narrow: The east entrance is mentioned as the location but no mention is made of the spot being narrow.
❌ 3. she was on the phone with Northland Mutual: Denise called her insurance company on Monday morning, not during the Saturday accident.
❌ 4. she could not see Marcus's car behind her: Denise says she was distracted by Sophie's call, not that she couldn't see the car.

**Q3:** The $650 estimate was for .....
- 1. the dent on Marcus's rear panel.
- 2. both vehicles combined.
- 3. the insurance deductible amount.
- 4. Denise's front bumper repair.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the dent on Marcus's rear panel: Denise says the $650 is for her own front bumper; she only guesses Marcus's damage might be similar.
❌ 2. both vehicles combined: The estimate covers only Denise's car from Brampton Auto Body.
❌ 3. the insurance deductible amount: No deductible is mentioned; $650 is the repair estimate.
✅ 4. Denise's front bumper repair: Denise says 'they estimated the repair for my front bumper at around $650.'

**Q4:** Patricia needs to .....
- 1. visit Denise's home on Elm Crescent.
- 2. photograph Marcus's car before it is fixed.
- 3. inspect both vehicles at Brampton Auto Body.
- 4. contact Marcus from the Queen Street office.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. visit Denise's home on Elm Crescent: Patricia is not asked to visit anyone's home; she needs photos of the vehicle.
✅ 2. photograph Marcus's car before it is fixed: Denise says Patricia 'might need a few photos of your vehicle before the repairs are done.'
❌ 3. inspect both vehicles at Brampton Auto Body: There is no mention of inspecting both cars or visiting the auto body shop.
❌ 4. contact Marcus from the Queen Street office: Denise specifically warns that Patricia's office is on King Street, not Queen Street.

**Q5:** Denise was probably surprised that Marcus .....
- 1. stayed calm after the accident.
- 2. agreed to file a claim with her insurance.
- 3. helped her clean up the parking lot.
- 4. already knew about her insurance company.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. stayed calm after the accident: Denise says 'Most people would have been upset' and she was 'really touched by how calm and understanding' Marcus was, implying she expected a different reaction.
❌ 2. agreed to file a claim with her insurance: Filing a claim is routine after an accident; there is no indication of surprise about this.
❌ 3. helped her clean up the parking lot: There is no mention of cleaning up the parking lot.
❌ 4. already knew about her insurance company: There is no indication Marcus knew about Northland Mutual beforehand.

**Q6:** Denise says she owes Marcus for .....
- 1. driving Sophie to school last winter.
- 2. recommending Brampton Auto Body.
- 3. helping with snow removal.
- 4. lending her his car after the accident.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. driving Sophie to school last winter: There is no mention of Marcus helping Sophie; he helped clear the driveway.
❌ 2. recommending Brampton Auto Body: No one recommended the auto body shop to Denise; she found it herself.
✅ 3. helping with snow removal: Denise says 'I still owe you for helping me clear the driveway after that big snowfall last February.'
❌ 4. lending her his car after the accident: There is no mention of Marcus lending his car to Denise.

**Q7:** None
- 1. dinner plans
- 2. insurance paperwork
- 3. accident
- 4. repair estimate
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. dinner plans: Marcus wouldn't tell Denise not to worry about dinner — she offered the invitation cheerfully.
❌ 2. insurance paperwork: While insurance is mentioned, Denise's main concern and apology is about the accident itself, not paperwork.
✅ 3. accident: Denise repeatedly apologizes for the accident; Marcus is reassuring her not to worry about the incident overall.
❌ 4. repair estimate: The estimate is one detail; Marcus is addressing Denise's broader guilt about the entire situation.

**Q8:** None
- 1. stressful
- 2. exciting
- 3. embarrassing
- 4. confusing
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. stressful: Denise was distracted by Sophie's call about missing her bus while backing out and then had the accident — this is a stressful combination of events.
❌ 2. exciting: Nothing about the accident or Sophie missing her bus was exciting — the tone is clearly negative.
❌ 3. embarrassing: While Denise may have felt embarrassed, the sentence focuses on dealing with Sophie's situation, which was stressful rather than embarrassing.
❌ 4. confusing: The situation wasn't confusing — Denise knew what happened; she was overwhelmed by competing demands.

**Q9:** None
- 1. visit the Queen Street branch
- 2. contact the King Street office
- 3. call Northland Mutual's head office
- 4. send photos to Brampton Auto Body
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. visit the Queen Street branch: Denise specifically warned that Patricia's office is NOT on Queen Street — choosing this shows confusion between the two locations.
✅ 2. contact the King Street office: Denise wrote 'Her office is the one on King Street, not the branch on Queen Street, so make sure you contact the right location.'
❌ 3. call Northland Mutual's head office: No head office is mentioned; the specific instruction was about the King Street location.
❌ 4. send photos to Brampton Auto Body: Brampton Auto Body is where Denise got her estimate; Patricia needs photos, not the auto body shop.

**Q10:** None
- 1. much higher than
- 2. about the same as
- 3. lower than
- 4. double
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. much higher than: Denise did not guess this. She said the dent "did not look too deep", so "just as you guessed" cannot mean a much higher cost.
✅ 2. about the same as: Denise wrote "I imagine your damage might be similar" to her own $650 repair. Marcus says his estimate matches "just as you guessed", so it is about the same.
❌ 3. lower than: Denise never guessed Marcus's repair would cost less. She guessed it "might be similar", so this does not fit "just as you guessed".
❌ 4. double: Denise never said the cost might be twice as much. Double the cost would mean worse damage than she described.

**Q11:** None
- 1. visit the community centre
- 2. help clear your driveway
- 3. take you out to a restaurant
- 4. join you for dinner
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. visit the community centre: The community centre is where the accident happened — there's no reason to go back there together.
❌ 2. help clear your driveway: Marcus already helped with the driveway last February; this isn't what Denise invited them to do.
❌ 3. take you out to a restaurant: Denise invited them over to her home for dinner, not out to a restaurant.
✅ 4. join you for dinner: Denise asked 'Would you and Linda be free this Saturday evening? I would love to have you both over for dinner.'

- 1. the food trucks will have stopped serving by then

- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the food trucks will have stopped serving by then: Sunday hours are "11:00 a.m. – 7:00 p.m.", so the trucks are still serving at 4:00 p.m.
❌ 2. you'll miss the live music performances entirely: The poster says "LIVE MUSIC on the main stage both days!" but gives no show times, so nothing says she would miss all of it.
❌ 3. you'll still have a full five hours to explore: Five hours after 4:00 p.m. only works with Saturday's "9:00 p.m." closing. Nadia is not going on Saturday. Sunday closes at "7:00 p.m."
✅ 4. you'll only have around three hours before it closes: The festival is only two days, "August 17 & 18" (Saturday and Sunday). Greg went "today" and Nadia is going "tomorrow", so she goes on the last day, Sunday. Sunday ends at "7:00 p.m.", so arriving at 4:00 p.m. leaves 3 hours. That is why Greg says "try to leave a bit sooner".

**Q5:** None
- 1. they serve extremely spicy dishes not suitable for children
- 2. the truck doesn't offer any kid-friendly portions
- 3. their kitchen uses nuts, which could trigger his allergy
- 4. they don't list their ingredients on the menu
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. they serve extremely spicy dishes not suitable for children: The diagram never mentions spice levels for Thai One On or any other truck.
❌ 2. the truck doesn't offer any kid-friendly portions: No information about portion sizes is provided for any truck in the diagram.
✅ 3. their kitchen uses nuts, which could trigger his allergy: Greg calls it Jayden's 'condition' — a serious allergy. Thai One On's listing includes 'Allergy Alert: peanuts used in kitchen,' and peanuts are a type of nut that would trigger a nut-sensitive person's allergy.
❌ 4. they don't list their ingredients on the menu: Thai One On does list menu items (pad thai, mango sticky rice, coconut curry soup) and even provides an allergy alert about peanuts.

**Q6:** None
- 1. coworkers who recently started at the same company
- 2. friends whose children spend time together
- 3. neighbours who just introduced themselves
- 4. relatives who are visiting for the weekend
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. coworkers who recently started at the same company: The email is entirely personal and family-focused with no mention of a workplace, office, or job — nothing suggests a professional relationship.
✅ 2. friends whose children spend time together: Greg mentions 'Lily can't stop talking about their playdate last week,' showing their children (Lily and Ava) regularly socialize. Greg also knows Omar's dietary needs and Jayden's allergy intimately, indicating a close family friendship.
❌ 3. neighbours who just introduced themselves: Greg knows the names and specific needs of every family member — Ava's personality, Omar's faith-based diet, Jayden's medical condition — which indicates a well-established relationship, not a recent introduction.
❌ 4. relatives who are visiting for the weekend: There is no mention of travel, family ties, or visiting from elsewhere; Greg simply shares tips for the next day at a local festival.

**Q7:** None
- 1. buy their festival passes well in advance
- 2. arrive before the food trucks start serving
- 3. bring their own meals because of limited choices
- 4. plan around certain dietary and medical needs
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. buy their festival passes well in advance: Admission to the festival is FREE according to the diagram — there are no passes to purchase.
❌ 2. arrive before the food trucks start serving: The festival opens at 11:00 a.m. on both days, and Greg's concern is that Nadia might arrive too LATE, not about arriving before opening.
❌ 3. bring their own meals because of limited choices: The festival has six different food trucks with diverse cuisines — there are plenty of options, and Greg specifically recommends Yalla Falafel for Omar.
✅ 4. plan around certain dietary and medical needs: Omar requires halal-certified food (a religious dietary need), and Jayden has a serious nut allergy (a medical need). Both of these require careful planning about which trucks are safe to eat from.

**Q8:** None
- 1. frustrating because of the limited food truck selection
- 2. disappointing since several attractions were unavailable
- 3. enjoyable despite a small issue with payment
- 4. overwhelming due to the large weekend crowds
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. frustrating because of the limited food truck selection: Greg describes the festival as 'fantastic' and recommends multiple trucks — he clearly found the selection satisfying.
❌ 2. disappointing since several attractions were unavailable: Greg does not mention any closed or unavailable attractions; he enthusiastically recommends the festival to Nadia.
✅ 3. enjoyable despite a small issue with payment: Greg says the festival 'was fantastic' (enjoyable) but specifically warns 'Bring cash too — that same BBQ place doesn't take cards!' The pointed cash warning, combined with the fact that he ate there ('they were delicious'), suggests the cash-only policy was a notable hiccup he learned about firsthand during an otherwise great experience.
❌ 4. overwhelming due to the large weekend crowds: Greg never mentions crowds, wait times, or feeling overwhelmed — his tone is positive and enthusiastic throughout.

## Exam 56 — Part 9: Sable Island Horses

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. Sable Island, a narrow crescent-shaped sandbar approximately 300 kilometres southeast of Halifax, Nova Scotia, is home to one of Canada's most iconic populations of wild horses. The origins of these horses remain debated among historians. Some evidence suggests they were introduced by Boston merchant Thomas Hancock around 1760, while other accounts attribute their arrival to the forced deportation of Acadian settlers during the Great Expulsion of 1755, when confiscated livestock was reportedly transported to the remote island. Regardless of their precise origins, the horses are descendants of domesticated breeds that have since adapted to an extraordinarily harsh and isolated environment.

B. The island itself stretches roughly 42 kilometres in length yet measures only 1.5 kilometres at its widest point. Devoid of trees, it is characterized by rolling sand dunes, sparse grasslands, and frequent Atlantic storms that bring powerful winds and dense fog. Despite these inhospitable conditions, the horses have thrived for over two centuries by subsisting primarily on marram grass, which stabilizes the island's shifting dunes, along with beach pea and other wild vegetation. Their diet, though nutritionally limited, sustains a population that has become noticeably smaller in stature than typical domesticated horses, a physical consequence of generations of dietary restriction.

C. The Sable Island horses organize themselves into small social bands, typically comprising one stallion, several mares, and their young offspring. These groups establish loose territories across the island, though boundaries frequently shift depending on seasonal grazing availability. Notably, the horses receive no human intervention whatsoever—no supplemental feeding, veterinary treatment, or shelter is provided. Consequently, the population is entirely self-regulating, fluctuating in response to natural factors such as winter severity and forage abundance. Researchers from Dalhousie University have monitored the herd since the 1980s, documenting population numbers that have ranged between approximately 250 and 550 individuals over recent decades.

D. The legal protection of the Sable Island horses was prompted by public outcry in the early 1960s. Previously, the federal government had periodically authorized roundups to reduce the herd, with captured horses often sold for slaughter or labour. In 1961, following a national letter-writing campaign led predominantly by schoolchildren, Ottawa enacted regulations prohibiting any interference with the horses. This protected status was reinforced in 2013, when Sable Island was officially designated as Canada's forty-third national park reserve. Today, the horses are widely regarded as a cherished symbol of resilience, drawing researchers and limited visitors to this remote Atlantic outpost annually.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

### Questions & Answer Key

- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** Paragraph A states 'The origins of these horses remain debated among historians.' 'Scholars disagree' paraphrases 'debated among historians,' and 'how the horses first reached the island' paraphrases their 'origins.'

**Q2:** None
- 1. A

- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** Paragraph B states the horses have become 'noticeably smaller in stature than typical domesticated horses, a physical consequence of generations of dietary restriction.' 'Prolonged dietary limitations' paraphrases 'generations of dietary restriction,' and 'physically transformed' paraphrases the change in stature.

**Q3:** None
- 1. A

- 4. D
- 5. E

- **Explanation:** Paragraph D states 'a national letter-writing campaign led predominantly by schoolchildren' prompted Ottawa to enact protective regulations. 'Young citizens' paraphrases 'schoolchildren,' 'instrumental' paraphrases 'led predominantly,' and 'legal safeguards' paraphrases 'regulations prohibiting any interference.'

**Q4:** None
- 1. A

## Exam 56 — Part 10: Remote Learning Snow Days

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
When a severe February blizzard shuttered schools across Ontario for three consecutive days last winter, the Halton District School Board made the unprecedented decision to pivot immediately to virtual instruction, a move that reignited a simmering national debate over whether the traditional snow day—long cherished by children and parents alike—has become an anachronism in the age of ubiquitous digital connectivity. According to a 2023 survey conducted by the Canadian Teachers' Federation, approximately 41 percent of school districts in Canada have either adopted or are actively considering policies that would mandate remote learning in lieu of outright cancellations during inclement weather events. Proponents of such measures contend that the academic continuity afforded by online platforms far outweighs the nostalgic appeal of an unscheduled day off.

Vicente Almeida, superintendent of the Halton board, maintains that instructional hours lost to weather-related closures accumulate in ways that disproportionately disadvantage students already struggling with curriculum benchmarks. "Every forfeited day of teaching compounds existing inequities, and we now possess the technological infrastructure to ensure that meteorological disruptions do not translate into pedagogical ones," Almeida asserts. He further notes that the district's remote attendance rates during the February storm exceeded 87 percent, a figure he characterizes as remarkably encouraging.

Not everyone, however, regards this shift as wholly beneficial. Ramona Chartrand, a child psychologist practising in Ottawa, cautions that the elimination of spontaneous breaks may erode the psychological respite that unstructured free time provides developing minds. "Snow days function as rare, serendipitous interruptions that foster creativity and familial bonding," Chartrand explains, adding that the relentless digitization of childhood leaves increasingly fewer spaces for genuine leisure devoid of screen-mediated obligations.

Parent and software developer Kwame Osei, whose two children attend schools in the Halton district, offers a more pragmatic perspective, acknowledging that while remote learning days are logistically convenient for working families who would otherwise scramble for childcare, the arrangement does impose an additional burden on households lacking reliable internet access. "We are essentially transferring a systemic responsibility onto individual families and calling it progress," Osei remarks. "Until equitable access is guaranteed, this policy risks widening the very gap it purports to close."

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. technological preparedness of Canadian school boards for emergency remote instruction
- 2. psychological toll of excessive screen exposure on elementary-aged students
- 3. contentious question of whether virtual learning ought to supplant traditional snow days
- 4. inequitable distribution of digital infrastructure across Ontario's school districts
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. technological preparedness of Canadian school boards for emergency remote instruction: While technology is mentioned, the article's scope extends well beyond readiness assessments to include psychological, social, and equity dimensions of the debate.
❌ 2. psychological toll of excessive screen exposure on elementary-aged students: Screen time is raised only by Chartrand as one perspective; the article covers multiple competing viewpoints, not solely psychological harm.
✅ 3. contentious question of whether virtual learning ought to supplant traditional snow days: The article presents a superintendent defending virtual snow days, a psychologist opposing the shift, and a parent raising equity concerns — all revolving around this central debate.
❌ 4. inequitable distribution of digital infrastructure across Ontario's school districts: Digital equity is one concern raised by Osei, but it is not the overarching topic of the entire article.

**Q2:** None
- 1. the academic consequences of weather cancellations warrant the adoption of remote learning alternatives
- 2. remote instruction should be federally mandated across all Canadian provinces and territories
- 3. meteorological disruptions primarily affect students enrolled in affluent suburban school districts
- 4. traditional snow days are pedagogically inconsequential for the majority of learners
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. the academic consequences of weather cancellations warrant the adoption of remote learning alternatives: Almeida argues that lost instructional hours 'compound existing inequities' and that the district's technological infrastructure can prevent 'pedagogical disruptions' — he clearly sees academic costs as justifying the shift.
❌ 2. remote instruction should be federally mandated across all Canadian provinces and territories: Almeida speaks only about his own district's decision; he never advocates for a federal mandate.
❌ 3. meteorological disruptions primarily affect students enrolled in affluent suburban school districts: Almeida states that struggling students are disproportionately disadvantaged, not students in affluent districts specifically.
❌ 4. traditional snow days are pedagogically inconsequential for the majority of learners: The opposite — Almeida emphasizes that every forfeited day compounds inequities, implying snow days are indeed consequential.

**Q3:** None
- 1. unreservedly critical of the district's technological capabilities and implementation strategy
- 2. conditionally supportive, tempered by serious reservations about equitable access
- 3. predominantly optimistic about the long-term logistical benefits for dual-income families
- 4. largely indifferent to the broader implications for student achievement and well-being
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. unreservedly critical of the district's technological capabilities and implementation strategy: Osei actually acknowledges logistical convenience for working families, so his criticism is not unreserved.
✅ 2. conditionally supportive, tempered by serious reservations about equitable access: Osei concedes that remote days are 'logistically convenient' but warns the policy 'risks widening the very gap it purports to close' due to unequal internet access — a balanced, conditional stance.
❌ 3. predominantly optimistic about the long-term logistical benefits for dual-income families: While Osei notes convenience, his overall tone emphasizes concern about equity, not optimism.
❌ 4. largely indifferent to the broader implications for student achievement and well-being: Osei explicitly raises concerns about systemic responsibility and access, demonstrating significant engagement, not indifference.

**Q4:** None
- 1. abrupt transitions to online instruction during storms cause undue psychological strain on young children
- 2. replacing cancellations with virtual sessions disproportionately disadvantages children from lower-income households
- 3. the pervasive digitization of children's daily lives is progressively eliminating spaces for authentic, screen-free leisure
- 4. virtual instruction platforms are pedagogically inferior to traditional face-to-face classroom delivery
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. abrupt transitions to online instruction during storms cause undue psychological strain on young children: Chartrand's concern is not about acute storm-related stress but about the broader erosion of unstructured free time through ongoing digitization.
❌ 2. replacing cancellations with virtual sessions disproportionately disadvantages children from lower-income households: This reflects Osei's equity-based concern, not Chartrand's psychological perspective.
✅ 3. the pervasive digitization of children's daily lives is progressively eliminating spaces for authentic, screen-free leisure: Chartrand warns that snow days provide 'psychological respite' and 'foster creativity and familial bonding,' and that digitization leaves 'increasingly fewer spaces for genuine leisure devoid of screen-mediated obligations.'
❌ 4. virtual instruction platforms are pedagogically inferior to traditional face-to-face classroom delivery: Chartrand does not compare instructional quality; her concern is psychological, focused on the loss of unstructured free time and the overreach of digital demands.

**Q5:** None
- 1. begun exploring or implementing policies to replace weather cancellations with online instruction
- 2. formally rejected proposals to introduce compulsory virtual learning during inclement weather
- 3. partnered with private telecommunications firms to ensure broadband access for all enrolled students
- 4. retained traditional cancellation protocols due to opposition from child welfare organizations
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. begun exploring or implementing policies to replace weather cancellations with online instruction: The article states that approximately 41 percent of districts 'have either adopted or are actively considering policies that would mandate remote learning in lieu of outright cancellations.'
❌ 2. formally rejected proposals to introduce compulsory virtual learning during inclement weather: The article reports adoption and consideration, not rejection.
❌ 3. partnered with private telecommunications firms to ensure broadband access for all enrolled students: No such partnerships are mentioned anywhere in the article.
❌ 4. retained traditional cancellation protocols due to opposition from child welfare organizations: While opposition exists, the article does not mention child welfare organizations or claim districts have retained old protocols because of them.

**Q6:** None
- 1. frustration with outdated classroom technology
- 2. apprehension regarding instructional losses
- 3. enthusiasm for district-wide digital innovation
- 4. criticism of parental disengagement from schooling
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. frustration with outdated classroom technology: Almeida praises the district's technological infrastructure rather than expressing frustration with it.
✅ 2. apprehension regarding instructional losses: Almeida warns that 'every forfeited day of teaching compounds existing inequities,' demonstrating clear concern about lost instructional time.
❌ 3. enthusiasm for district-wide digital innovation: While Almeida supports virtual learning, his argument centres on equity and lost hours, not enthusiasm for innovation per se.
❌ 4. criticism of parental disengagement from schooling: Almeida never discusses parental involvement or lack thereof.

**Q7:** None
- 1. saturated
- 2. unfamiliar
- 3. comfortable
- 4. disillusioned
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. saturated: Chartrand argues that 'relentless digitization of childhood leaves increasingly fewer spaces for genuine leisure,' implying children are already overwhelmed or saturated with digital demands.
❌ 2. unfamiliar: The article suggests the opposite — children are deeply immersed in digital environments, not unfamiliar with them.
❌ 3. comfortable: While children may be accustomed to screens, the commenter's tone is critical, implying excess rather than comfort.
❌ 4. disillusioned: Nothing in the article suggests children are disillusioned with technology; the concern is about overexposure.

**Q8:** None
- 1. improve academic outcomes for all learners
- 2. strengthen community ties among families
- 3. deepen existing socioeconomic disparities
- 4. discourage parental engagement in education
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. improve academic outcomes for all learners: Osei warns the policy may widen gaps, not improve outcomes universally.
❌ 2. strengthen community ties among families: Community ties are not discussed by Osei; his focus is on access inequality.
✅ 3. deepen existing socioeconomic disparities: Osei states the policy 'risks widening the very gap it purports to close' due to unequal internet access — directly pointing to deepening disparities.
❌ 4. discourage parental engagement in education: Osei discusses systemic access, not parental willingness to participate.

**Q9:** None
- 1. upgrade curricula
- 2. eliminate closures
- 3. survey educators
- 4. address connectivity
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. upgrade curricula: The commenter's concern aligns with Osei's point about infrastructure, not curriculum quality.
❌ 2. eliminate closures: The commenter sympathizes with Chartrand's pro-snow-day stance, making 'eliminate closures' contradictory.
❌ 3. survey educators: Teacher perspectives are not discussed in the article, and the commenter focuses on household access.
✅ 4. address connectivity: The commenter states 'boards should not assume every household enjoys the same digital infrastructure,' echoing Osei's call for equitable access before implementation.

**Q10:** None
- 1. has long since become irrelevant to modern families
- 2. only benefits students in well-resourced school districts
- 3. teachers have consistently failed to appreciate
- 4. virtual platforms cannot adequately replicate
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. has long since become irrelevant to modern families: The commenter explicitly defends snow days, so calling them irrelevant contradicts the entire comment.
❌ 2. only benefits students in well-resourced school districts: The commenter implies snow days benefit all children psychologically, not just those in affluent areas.
❌ 3. teachers have consistently failed to appreciate: Teacher attitudes toward snow days are never mentioned in the article or the comment.
✅ 4. virtual platforms cannot adequately replicate: The commenter sides with Chartrand's view that snow days provide unique psychological and developmental benefits — creativity, bonding, unstructured leisure — that online instruction cannot reproduce.

## Exam 57 — Part 7: Rental Listing Feedback

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Kenji,

I finally had a chance to look over the Airbnb listing draft you sent me on Tuesday. First of all, I have to say that your basement suite looks amazing. You and Laura have done such a great job renovating it since you bought the place last spring. I remember visiting your house on Birchwood Drive back in August, and the basement was still unfinished concrete. The transformation is honestly incredible, and I think you should feel really proud of what you have accomplished.

The photos you included are mostly great, especially the ones of the living area and the kitchenette. However, I noticed that the two bedroom photos are quite dark, probably because you took them in the evening without enough lighting. Natural light makes a huge difference in listing photos, so I would suggest retaking those on a bright morning when the sun comes through the east-facing windows. Also, I noticed you did not include any pictures of the private entrance on the side of the house. That is actually a big selling point because most guests prefer not walking through the host's main living space.

Regarding the written description, you mention that the suite is a ten-minute walk from the Southgate LRT station, but I think you are thinking of Century Park station. Southgate is actually the shopping centre nearby, not the train stop. You might want to double-check that so guests do not get confused when they arrive. I also think you should highlight that the neighbourhood is close to Whyte Avenue, since visitors to Edmonton always love exploring the restaurants and shops there.

One thing that concerned me a little is your pricing. You have listed the suite at $135 per night, which seems high for a basement unit in that area. I did some research, and similar places nearby are going for around $95 to $110. Because this is your first listing and you will not have any reviews yet, setting the price a bit lower at the beginning could help you attract those important first guests. Once you build up some positive ratings, you can always raise it.

I am free this Saturday afternoon if you want me to come over and help you retake those bedroom photos. I can bring my camera, which handles low light much better than a phone. Let me know if that works for you.

Best regards,
Danielle

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** Danielle is writing to Kenji in order to .....
- 1. invite him to photograph her home
- 2. recommend a vacation rental for his trip
- 3. provide feedback on his listing draft
- 4. apologize for a delayed visit
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. invite him to photograph her home: Danielle offers to bring her camera to Kenji's home, not the other way around.
❌ 2. recommend a vacation rental for his trip: Kenji is creating his own Airbnb listing, not searching for a rental.
✅ 3. provide feedback on his listing draft: Danielle says 'I finally had a chance to look over the Airbnb listing draft you sent me' and proceeds to give detailed suggestions.
❌ 4. apologize for a delayed visit: Danielle mentions visiting in August but does not apologize for any delay.

**Q2:** The bedroom photos need to be retaken because .....
- 1. they were taken with insufficient lighting.
- 2. the rooms had not been fully renovated.
- 3. Danielle's camera was unavailable at the time.
- 4. the kitchenette was accidentally in the frame.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. they were taken with insufficient lighting: Danielle says the bedroom photos 'are quite dark, probably because you took them in the evening without enough lighting.'
❌ 2. the rooms had not been fully renovated: The basement was unfinished in August, but the renovation is now complete — the photos are poor due to lighting, not unfinished work.
❌ 3. Danielle's camera was unavailable at the time: Danielle offers her camera for Saturday; this is not the reason the original photos are dark.
❌ 4. the kitchenette was accidentally in the frame: The kitchenette photos are described as 'great' — they are a separate set of photos.

**Q3:** Danielle points out that Kenji forgot to include photos of .....
- 1. the kitchenette and its appliances.
- 2. the private side entrance.
- 3. the east-facing bedroom windows.
- 4. the living area and furniture.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the kitchenette and its appliances: Danielle says the kitchenette photos are already 'great,' so they are included.
✅ 2. the private side entrance: Danielle says 'I noticed you did not include any pictures of the private entrance on the side of the house' and calls it 'a big selling point.'
❌ 3. the east-facing bedroom windows: The windows are mentioned in the context of when to retake bedroom photos, not as missing from the listing.
❌ 4. the living area and furniture: Danielle says the living area photos are 'mostly great,' so they are already included.

**Q4:** Danielle suggests correcting the name of .....
- 1. the street where the suite is located.
- 2. the neighbourhood's popular shopping district.
- 3. the avenue with restaurants and shops.
- 4. the nearby transit station.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the street where the suite is located: Birchwood Drive is mentioned correctly and Danielle does not suggest any correction to it.
❌ 2. the neighbourhood's popular shopping district: Southgate is the shopping centre, but the error is that Kenji called the train station 'Southgate' instead of 'Century Park.'
❌ 3. the avenue with restaurants and shops: Whyte Avenue is mentioned as something to add, not as a name that needs correcting.
✅ 4. the nearby transit station: Danielle says Kenji wrote 'Southgate LRT station' but 'I think you are thinking of Century Park station. Southgate is actually the shopping centre.'

**Q5:** Danielle thinks the listed price is too high because .....
- 1. the basement is still partially unfinished.
- 2. comparable listings in the area charge less.
- 3. Kenji has not included enough photos.
- 4. the suite is far from public transit.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the basement is still partially unfinished: The renovation is complete; the unfinished concrete was the state back in August.
✅ 2. comparable listings in the area charge less: Danielle says 'similar places nearby are going for around $95 to $110' compared to Kenji's $135.
❌ 3. Kenji has not included enough photos: Missing photos are discussed separately from pricing concerns.
❌ 4. the suite is far from public transit: The suite is described as a ten-minute walk from a transit station, which is relatively close.

**Q6:** Danielle's overall attitude toward Kenji's project can best be described as .....
- 1. doubtful but polite
- 2. impressed but unhelpful
- 3. supportive and constructive
- 4. overwhelmed and critical
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. doubtful but polite: Danielle is not doubtful — she calls the renovation 'incredible' and offers specific help.
❌ 2. impressed but unhelpful: Danielle is impressed, but she also offers to come over Saturday with her camera, which is clearly helpful.
✅ 3. supportive and constructive: Danielle praises the renovation, gives specific actionable suggestions about photos, description, and pricing, and offers hands-on help.
❌ 4. overwhelmed and critical: Danielle is organized and balanced in her feedback, not overwhelmed, and her suggestions are constructive rather than harshly critical.

**Q7:** None
- 1. questioned
- 2. expected
- 3. appreciate
- 4. overlooked
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. questioned: Kenji would not question kind words about a renovation he is proud of.
❌ 2. expected: Saying he 'expected' praise sounds presumptuous and does not match the grateful tone of the reply.
✅ 3. appreciate: Kenji is thanking Danielle, so 'appreciate your kind words' fits both the grateful tone and the context of receiving positive feedback.
❌ 4. overlooked: 'Overlooked your kind words' means he ignored them, which contradicts the thankful tone of the sentence.

**Q8:** None
- 1. getting dark
- 2. raining outside
- 3. quite cold
- 4. fully furnished
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. getting dark: Danielle's email says the bedroom photos are dark because they were taken 'in the evening without enough lighting' — after dinner it would be getting dark.
❌ 2. raining outside: Rain is not mentioned anywhere in the main email; the issue was evening darkness, not weather.
❌ 3. quite cold: Temperature is irrelevant to why the photos turned out poorly; the problem was lighting.
❌ 4. fully furnished: This does not logically complete the sentence about why natural light was lacking.

**Q9:** None
- 1. laptop
- 2. camera
- 3. toolkit
- 4. extra lighting
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. laptop: Danielle did not offer to bring a laptop; the issue is retaking photos, not editing them.
✅ 2. camera: Danielle says 'I can bring my camera, which handles low light much better than a phone' in the main email.
❌ 3. toolkit: No toolkit is mentioned; the Saturday visit is about photography, not renovation work.
❌ 4. extra lighting: Danielle's solution was to use natural morning light and her camera, not to bring additional lighting equipment.

## Exam 57 — Part 9: Head-Smashed-in Buffalo Jump

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. Head-Smashed-In Buffalo Jump is a remarkable archaeological site located in southwestern Alberta, Canada, near the town of Fort Macleod. Designated a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1981, it is among the oldest, largest, and best-preserved buffalo jump sites in North America. The sandstone cliff rises approximately 10 metres above the prairie below. For nearly 6,000 years, Indigenous peoples of the Northern Plains utilized this precipice to hunt bison by driving herds over the edge. The deposit of bones at the base of the cliff extends roughly 12 metres in depth, attesting to millennia of sustained use.

B. The communal hunt required meticulous planning and coordination among the Blackfoot people, who were the principal users of the site. Weeks before a hunt, specially trained runners would position themselves along carefully constructed drive lanes made of stone cairns and brush piles. These lanes, stretching over 14 kilometres across the surrounding grasslands, funnelled the bison toward the cliff. When conditions were favourable, the runners would startle the herd, gradually guiding the animals into the narrowing lanes. The momentum of the stampeding bison at the rear made it virtually impossible for those at the front to stop.

C. Following the fall, animals that survived the plunge were dispatched by hunters waiting below. The processing camp at the base of the cliff was where women and elders undertook the task of butchering and preserving the meat. Virtually every part of the bison was utilized: hides were fashioned into clothing and shelter, bones were carved into tools and weapons, and sinew served as thread for sewing. The hunt was not merely a practical endeavour but held profound spiritual significance for the Blackfoot. Ceremonies and rituals were performed before and after each hunt to honour the animals that sustained their communities.

D. The site's use as a buffalo jump declined in the nineteenth century following the introduction of horses and firearms, which fundamentally altered traditional hunting methods. In 1987, the Head-Smashed-In Buffalo Jump Interpretive Centre was opened, built directly into the sandstone cliff to minimize visual disruption to the landscape. The centre, designed by the Blackfoot people in collaboration with archaeologists, houses seven levels of exhibits featuring artifacts, multimedia displays, and Blackfoot oral histories. Consequently, the site serves as both an educational institution and a place of cultural preservation. It attracts approximately 100,000 visitors annually, contributing significantly to regional tourism.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

## Exam 57 — Part 10: Digital Diploma Transition

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
When Marcus Pellegrini, director of admissions at Whitmore Polytechnic Institute, announced last September that the school would phase out paper diplomas in favour of blockchain-verified digital credentials, the decision ignited a spirited debate among educators, employers, and graduates alike. A 2023 survey conducted by the Canadian Council on Learning Innovation revealed that approximately 41 percent of post-secondary institutions across the country were either piloting or actively considering some form of digital credentialing—a figure that has nearly doubled since 2020, underscoring the accelerating momentum behind this paradigm shift.

Pellegrini contends that traditional diplomas, which can be forged, lost, or damaged, represent an antiquated verification mechanism ill-suited to the demands of a digitally interconnected labour market. "A blockchain-secured credential is tamper-proof, instantly verifiable, and grants employers real-time access to a granular record of competencies rather than a single, monolithic document," Pellegrini explains. He further asserts that digital credentials could embed metadata detailing specific skills, project outcomes, and micro-certifications, thereby offering a far more nuanced portrait of a graduate's capabilities than any parchment certificate ever could.

Not all stakeholders, however, share Pellegrini's unequivocal enthusiasm. Rosalind Chartier, a professor of higher education policy at the University of Ottawa, cautions that the rush toward digitization risks marginalizing populations who lack consistent access to technology or who remain unfamiliar with digital platforms. "Equity must not be sacrificed on the altar of innovation," Chartier maintains, adding that institutions ought to consider hybrid models preserving the tangible legitimacy of a printed diploma while augmenting it with digital supplements. She also raises the philosophical concern that reducing educational achievement to a series of machine-readable data points may inadvertently diminish the broader, humanistic value of a university education.

Meanwhile, Jerome Adetola, a human resources manager at a mid-sized engineering firm in Calgary, has already begun requesting digital credentials from applicants and reports that the streamlined verification process has reduced onboarding delays by nearly three weeks. "We no longer waste time contacting registrars or waiting for sealed transcripts," Adetola notes. Yet he concedes that many of his industry peers remain sceptical, viewing digital credentials as an unproven novelty rather than a dependable standard. "Until there is a universally recognized framework governing these credentials, adoption will remain uneven, and that hesitation is not entirely unreasonable," Adetola acknowledges.

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. technological infrastructure required for blockchain-based education systems
- 2. growing popularity of micro-certifications in Canadian post-secondary institutions
- 3. contentious shift from conventional diplomas to digitally verified credentials
- 4. declining relevance of university education in the modern labour market
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. technological infrastructure required for blockchain-based education systems: The article discusses the debate around digital credentials, not the technical requirements of blockchain infrastructure.
❌ 2. growing popularity of micro-certifications in Canadian post-secondary institutions: Micro-certifications are mentioned only briefly as one feature Pellegrini describes; they are not the article's central topic.
✅ 3. contentious shift from conventional diplomas to digitally verified credentials: The article explores multiple perspectives — Pellegrini's advocacy, Chartier's caution, and Adetola's qualified support — around replacing paper diplomas with digital credentials.
❌ 4. declining relevance of university education in the modern labour market: No one in the article argues that university education itself is becoming irrelevant; the debate concerns the format of credentials.

**Q2:** None
- 1. fundamentally inadequate for conveying a graduate's full range of competencies
- 2. serviceable documents that merely require supplementary digital features
- 3. deliberately misleading representations of academic achievement
- 4. culturally significant artefacts that nonetheless lack practical utility
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. fundamentally inadequate for conveying a graduate's full range of competencies: Pellegrini calls traditional diplomas 'antiquated' and contrasts them with digital credentials that offer 'a granular record of competencies rather than a single, monolithic document.'
❌ 2. serviceable documents that merely require supplementary digital features: This aligns with Chartier's hybrid model, not Pellegrini's view; he advocates phasing out paper diplomas entirely.
❌ 3. deliberately misleading representations of academic achievement: Pellegrini says paper diplomas can be forged and are limited, but never suggests they are designed to mislead.
❌ 4. culturally significant artefacts that nonetheless lack practical utility: Pellegrini makes no acknowledgement of cultural significance; he dismisses them as outdated without sentiment.

**Q3:** None
- 1. Postponing all digitization efforts until universal technology access is guaranteed
- 2. Prioritizing machine-readable data to enhance employer decision-making
- 3. Eliminating paper diplomas to accelerate institutional modernization
- 4. Implementing a dual system that retains physical documents alongside digital versions
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. Postponing all digitization efforts until universal technology access is guaranteed: Chartier does not advocate halting progress entirely; she suggests hybrid models that balance innovation with equity.
❌ 2. Prioritizing machine-readable data to enhance employer decision-making: Chartier explicitly warns against 'reducing educational achievement to a series of machine-readable data points,' making this contrary to her view.
❌ 3. Eliminating paper diplomas to accelerate institutional modernization: This reflects Pellegrini's position, which Chartier opposes by stressing the 'tangible legitimacy of a printed diploma.'
✅ 4. Implementing a dual system that retains physical documents alongside digital versions: Chartier advocates 'hybrid models preserving the tangible legitimacy of a printed diploma while augmenting it with digital supplements.'

**Q4:** None
- 1. dismissive of their practical merits in a recruitment context
- 2. cautiously favourable while recognizing legitimate barriers to widespread uptake
- 3. wholly enthusiastic about their potential to transform hiring practices
- 4. primarily concerned with the cost implications for smaller firms
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. dismissive of their practical merits in a recruitment context: Adetola actively uses digital credentials and reports reduced onboarding delays, so he clearly values their practical merits.
✅ 2. cautiously favourable while recognizing legitimate barriers to widespread uptake: Adetola praises digital credentials for streamlining verification but concedes that industry scepticism exists and calls the hesitation 'not entirely unreasonable' without a universal framework.
❌ 3. wholly enthusiastic about their potential to transform hiring practices: Adetola tempers his support by acknowledging that peers view digital credentials as 'an unproven novelty,' so his enthusiasm is qualified, not unconditional.
❌ 4. primarily concerned with the cost implications for smaller firms: The article never mentions cost as a concern for Adetola; his reservations centre on the lack of a universally recognized framework.

**Q5:** None
- 1. concerns about the security vulnerabilities inherent in blockchain technology
- 2. a preference for maintaining personal relationships with institutional registrars
- 3. the absence of a standardized governance structure for such credentials
- 4. evidence that digital credentials have led to fraudulent hiring outcomes
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. concerns about the security vulnerabilities inherent in blockchain technology: Pellegrini describes blockchain credentials as 'tamper-proof,' and no one in the article raises security vulnerabilities.
❌ 2. a preference for maintaining personal relationships with institutional registrars: While Adetola mentions contacting registrars as a time-consuming step, no employer is described as preferring that relationship.
✅ 3. the absence of a standardized governance structure for such credentials: Adetola states that 'until there is a universally recognized framework governing these credentials, adoption will remain uneven,' directly linking employer hesitation to the lack of standards.
❌ 4. evidence that digital credentials have led to fraudulent hiring outcomes: The article does not mention any instances of fraud associated with digital credentials.

**Q6:** None
- 1. too costly to maintain
- 2. no longer fit for purpose
- 3. still valuable to graduates
- 4. primarily a symbolic gesture
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. too costly to maintain: Pellegrini never discusses the cost of paper diplomas; his argument centres on their functional limitations.
✅ 2. no longer fit for purpose: Pellegrini calls traditional diplomas 'an antiquated verification mechanism ill-suited to the demands of a digitally interconnected labour market,' meaning they are no longer fit for purpose.
❌ 3. still valuable to graduates: This contradicts Pellegrini's stance; he advocates phasing them out entirely.
❌ 4. primarily a symbolic gesture: While one might argue diplomas are symbolic, Pellegrini's critique focuses on their inadequacy for verification, not their symbolism.

**Q7:** None
- 1. persuasive
- 2. exaggerated
- 3. irrelevant
- 4. premature
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. persuasive: The commenter says they find Chartier's equity warning compelling and immediately supports it by noting unequal access, indicating agreement.
❌ 2. exaggerated: The commenter agrees with Chartier's concern rather than dismissing it as overblown.
❌ 3. irrelevant: The commenter explicitly engages with the equity argument and reinforces it, showing they consider it very relevant.
❌ 4. premature: The commenter treats the concern as timely and important, not as something raised too early.

**Q8:** None
- 1. how technology can alienate workers
- 2. clear merit in verification speed
- 3. growing demand for digital literacy
- 4. practical gains in recruitment efficiency
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. how technology can alienate workers: Adetola's experience is positive, reporting reduced delays, not worker alienation.
❌ 2. clear merit in verification speed: While close, this is too narrow; Adetola's comment about eliminating registrar contacts and reducing onboarding time speaks to broader recruitment efficiency.
❌ 3. growing demand for digital literacy: Adetola discusses hiring benefits, not digital literacy demands on employees.
✅ 4. practical gains in recruitment efficiency: Adetola reports that digital credentials 'reduced onboarding delays by nearly three weeks' and eliminated time-consuming transcript requests — demonstrating practical recruitment efficiency.

**Q9:** None
- 1. better technology
- 2. employer coalitions
- 3. uniform standards
- 4. legislative mandates
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. better technology: The commenter's concern is about governance and trust, not technological improvement.
❌ 2. employer coalitions: While employer agreement matters, the commenter references all stakeholders needing consensus, aligning with standardized frameworks rather than employer-only coalitions.
✅ 3. uniform standards: Adetola calls for 'a universally recognized framework governing these credentials,' and the commenter echoes this by seeking something 'all institutions and employers can trust.'
❌ 4. legislative mandates: The commenter mentions consensus and trust rather than government-imposed laws; no one in the article advocates legislation.

**Q10:** None
- 1. a source of ongoing confusion
- 2. an unnecessary burden on graduates
- 3. a tool for institutional prestige
- 4. yet another passing technological fad
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. a source of ongoing confusion: Without consensus or uniform standards, digital credentials would create inconsistency and confusion rather than clarity — the commenter contrasts this with being 'a transformative solution.'
❌ 2. an unnecessary burden on graduates: The commenter's concern is about systemic trust, not personal burden on individuals.
❌ 3. a tool for institutional prestige: Institutional prestige is never discussed in the article or implied by the commenter.
❌ 4. yet another passing technological fad: While Adetola notes some view credentials as a 'novelty,' the commenter does not dismiss them as a fad — they advocate for proper development, suggesting belief in their potential.

## Exam 58 — Part 7: First Ice Fishing Trip

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Marcus,

I hope you're doing well up in Thunder Bay. I've been meaning to write to you since last weekend because I finally tried ice fishing for the first time. I know you've been telling me to try it for years. Well, you were absolutely right — I loved it.

My coworker Daniel invited me to join him and his brother-in-law, Steve, on Lake Simcoe this past Saturday. We left Toronto around five in the morning because Daniel insisted we be on the ice before sunrise. The drive took about an hour and a half, and when we arrived near Beaverton, the sky was just turning pink along the horizon. It was one of the most peaceful mornings I can remember.

Steve had rented a heated ice hut for the day, which cost $150 for the three of us. I had been dreading the cold — it was around minus eighteen — but the hut was surprisingly warm inside. Daniel brought his own portable shelter and set up thirty metres away on the ice. He prefers fishing alone, so Steve and I shared the rented hut.

Steve showed me how to use a jig and read the fish finder. I didn't catch anything for the first couple of hours and was starting to feel discouraged. Then, just before noon, I felt a strong tug on my line. My hands were shaking, partly from the cold but mostly from excitement. I pulled up a lake trout that Steve estimated was about four pounds. I couldn't stop grinning. Daniel, on the other hand, only managed a couple of small perch all day, which frustrated him since he is usually the most successful angler in the group.

We packed up around three because the wind picked up and the temperature was dropping fast. On the drive home, we stopped in Orillia for dinner at a small pub where I proudly told our server about my catch. She laughed and said beginners always have the best luck on Simcoe.

I'm already planning another trip in February, and I think you should come down and join us. Steve mentioned that Lake Couchiching, right next to Simcoe, is even better for walleye. It would be great to see you again. Let me know if you can make it work.

Your friend,
Kevin

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** What is the main purpose of Kevin's letter?
- 1. To recommend a restaurant he visited in Orillia.
- 2. To complain about the cold weather in Toronto.
- 3. To share an experience and invite Marcus to visit.
- 4. To ask Marcus for advice about fishing equipment.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. To recommend a restaurant he visited in Orillia: The pub stop is a minor detail; the letter is about the ice fishing trip and an invitation to Marcus.
❌ 2. To complain about the cold weather in Toronto: Kevin mentions the cold but does not complain about it; he focuses on how much he enjoyed the trip.
✅ 3. To share an experience and invite Marcus to visit: Kevin describes his first ice fishing trip in detail and then invites Marcus to come down in February.
❌ 4. To ask Marcus for advice about fishing equipment: Kevin does not ask for any advice; he is telling Marcus about the trip and proposing they fish together.

**Q2:** Daniel preferred to .....
- 1. fish by himself away from the hut.
- 2. share the rented hut with Kevin.
- 3. stay warm in the car during the trip.
- 4. teach Kevin how to use the fish finder.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. fish by himself away from the hut: Kevin says Daniel 'brought his own portable shelter and set up thirty metres away on the ice' because 'He prefers fishing alone.'
❌ 2. share the rented hut with Kevin: Steve and Kevin shared the rented hut, not Daniel; Daniel set up his own shelter separately.
❌ 3. stay warm in the car during the trip: There is no mention of anyone staying in the car; Daniel fished from his own portable shelter.
❌ 4. teach Kevin how to use the fish finder: It was Steve who showed Kevin how to use the jig and fish finder, not Daniel.

**Q3:** Kevin caught his first fish .....
- 1. within minutes of arriving on the ice.
- 2. after Steve lent him better equipment.
- 3. early in the morning before sunrise.
- 4. after waiting for several hours.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. within minutes of arriving on the ice: Kevin says he 'didn't catch anything for the first couple of hours,' so it was far from immediate.
❌ 2. after Steve lent him better equipment: No equipment lending is mentioned; Kevin simply felt a tug on his line before noon.
❌ 3. early in the morning before sunrise: They arrived around sunrise, and Kevin caught the fish 'just before noon' — hours later.
✅ 4. after waiting for several hours: Kevin says he caught nothing for the first couple of hours and then landed the trout 'just before noon,' meaning he waited several hours.

**Q4:** The group stopped in Orillia to .....
- 1. rent the ice hut for the day.
- 2. have a meal on the way home.
- 3. pick up Steve before the trip.
- 4. buy supplies for the next outing.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. rent the ice hut for the day: The hut was rented near Beaverton on Lake Simcoe, not in Orillia.
✅ 2. have a meal on the way home: Kevin says 'On the drive home, we stopped in Orillia for dinner at a small pub.'
❌ 3. pick up Steve before the trip: They all left Toronto together at five in the morning; Orillia was a stop on the way back, not before the trip.
❌ 4. buy supplies for the next outing: Kevin mentions no shopping in Orillia; they stopped only for dinner.

**Q5:** The server at the pub probably thought Kevin was .....
- 1. an inexperienced fisherman.
- 2. exaggerating about the size of his catch.
- 3. a regular customer at the restaurant.
- 4. visiting from Thunder Bay.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. an inexperienced fisherman: The server 'laughed and said beginners always have the best luck on Simcoe,' implying she could tell Kevin was new to ice fishing.
❌ 2. exaggerating about the size of his catch: The server's comment about beginner's luck suggests she believed him, not that she doubted the story.
❌ 3. a regular customer at the restaurant: There is no indication they had been to this pub before; they stopped on the drive home.
❌ 4. visiting from Thunder Bay: Kevin lives in Toronto; Marcus lives in Thunder Bay. The server would have no reason to think Kevin was from Thunder Bay.

**Q6:** Steve mentioned that Lake Couchiching .....
- 1. has better heated ice huts for rent.
- 2. is much farther from Toronto than Simcoe.
- 3. is a good spot for a certain type of fish.
- 4. is where Daniel usually goes fishing.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. has better heated ice huts for rent: Hut rentals are mentioned only in relation to Lake Simcoe; Steve says nothing about huts on Couchiching.
❌ 2. is much farther from Toronto than Simcoe: Kevin says Couchiching is 'right next to Simcoe,' so it would not be much farther.
✅ 3. is a good spot for a certain type of fish: Steve mentioned that Lake Couchiching 'is even better for walleye,' a specific type of fish.
❌ 4. is where Daniel usually goes fishing: There is no mention of Daniel fishing on Couchiching; Steve simply recommended it.

**Q7:** None
- 1. lengthy
- 2. disappointing
- 3. fantastic
- 4. entertaining
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. lengthy: While Kevin's email is detailed, calling someone's exciting story 'lengthy' would be dismissive, not the warm, enthusiastic tone Marcus uses throughout his reply.
❌ 2. disappointing: Kevin loved the experience and caught a big fish — there was nothing disappointing about it, and Marcus expresses happiness, not sympathy.
✅ 3. fantastic: Kevin described the trip with great enthusiasm and caught a four-pound trout on his first outing — 'fantastic' matches the genuinely excited tone of Marcus's reply.
❌ 4. entertaining: While the story is enjoyable, 'entertaining' is too mild for Marcus's enthusiastic reaction; he goes on to express genuine excitement and eagerness to join, suggesting stronger praise than mere entertainment.

**Q8:** None
- 1. thorough
- 2. patient
- 3. reluctant
- 4. skillful
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. thorough: While Steve covered both the jig and the fish finder, 'thorough' focuses on completeness of instruction rather than the key detail that Kevin caught nothing for hours and Steve stayed with him throughout.
✅ 2. patient: Steve taught Kevin how to use a jig and read the fish finder, and Kevin didn't catch anything for the first couple of hours — Steve stuck with the beginner through a long wait, showing patience that ultimately paid off.
❌ 3. reluctant: There is no indication Steve was reluctant; he willingly shared the hut and actively taught Kevin fishing techniques.
❌ 4. skillful: While Steve clearly knows how to fish, the context — 'especially since you managed to land a lake trout' — highlights the teaching process and the long wait before success, pointing to patience rather than technical skill.

**Q9:** None
- 1. aware
- 2. concerned
- 3. surprised
- 4. thrilled
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. aware: 'Wasn't too aware' is grammatically awkward in this context and doesn't reflect Daniel's emotional reaction described in the email.
❌ 2. concerned: 'Wasn't too concerned' implies indifference, but Daniel's reaction was active frustration at being outfished, not a lack of concern.
❌ 3. surprised: While Daniel may have been surprised, the email emphasizes his frustration, and 'wasn't too surprised' would imply he expected it — the opposite of his reaction.
✅ 4. thrilled: Daniel was frustrated because he only caught small perch while Kevin, a complete beginner, landed a large trout — he clearly would not have been thrilled about being outfished.

**Q10:** None
- 1. join you for the trip
- 2. rent a bigger hut
- 3. move down to Toronto
- 4. try fishing alone
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. join you for the trip: Kevin invited Marcus to come down for a February fishing trip, and Marcus is accepting the invitation.
❌ 2. rent a bigger hut: While a hut was rented on the first trip, Marcus is responding to the invitation to join, not discussing hut logistics.
❌ 3. move down to Toronto: Kevin invited Marcus for a fishing trip, not to relocate — this is far too extreme.
❌ 4. try fishing alone: Daniel is the one who prefers fishing alone; Marcus is expressing excitement about joining the group.

**Q11:** None
- 1. perch
- 2. lake trout
- 3. bass
- 4. walleye
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. perch: Perch were caught by Daniel on Lake Simcoe; Steve did not mention perch in connection with Lake Couchiching.
❌ 2. lake trout: Kevin caught lake trout on Simcoe; Couchiching was recommended for a different species.
❌ 3. bass: Bass is never mentioned anywhere in Kevin's email.
✅ 4. walleye: Kevin wrote that 'Steve mentioned that Lake Couchiching, right next to Simcoe, is even better for walleye.'

Sunday: 11:00 a.m. – 7:00 p.m.

## Exam 58 — Part 9: Apollo Moon Trees

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. In 1971, approximately 500 tree seeds were carried into space aboard the Apollo 14 mission, which launched on January 31 from Kennedy Space Center in Florida. The seeds were the initiative of Stuart Roosa, the Command Module Pilot, who had worked as a smokejumper for the United States Forest Service before joining NASA. In partnership with the Forest Service, Roosa selected seeds from five species—loblolly pine, sycamore, sweetgum, redwood, and Douglas fir—and stored them in small canisters. These seeds orbited the Moon 34 times inside the command module Kitty Hawk while his crewmates explored the lunar surface below.

B. The primary objective of the experiment was to determine whether prolonged exposure to the conditions of space—including microgravity and cosmic radiation—would have any measurable effect on seed germination and growth. Upon returning to Earth, however, the seed canisters accidentally burst open during the decontamination process, scattering and mixing the contents. Scientists initially feared the seeds had been ruined. Remarkably, when the Forest Service germinated them at research stations in Mississippi and California, nearly all sprouted successfully. Control seeds that had remained on Earth were also planted, providing researchers with a basis for scientific comparison between the two groups.

C. The resulting seedlings, known as Moon Trees, were distributed across the United States beginning in 1975, with many planted during the nation's bicentennial celebrations the following year. They were gifted to universities, state capitals, national parks, and other prominent institutions as living symbols of scientific achievement. A sycamore Moon Tree was notably planted on the grounds of the White House, while others were sent to locations in Brazil, Switzerland, and Japan. Significantly, no observable differences in appearance, growth rate, or longevity were detected between the Moon Trees and their Earth-bound counterparts, suggesting that space travel had not altered their biology.

D. For decades, the locations of most Moon Trees went largely unrecorded, and public awareness of their existence faded considerably. In 1996, a NASA scientist named David Williams began compiling a detailed database after receiving an inquiry from a schoolteacher in Indiana who wanted to learn more about a Moon Tree in her local park. Through Williams's research, dozens of surviving specimens were identified across the country, though some had been lost to storms or disease. More recently, second-generation Moon Trees—grown from the seeds of the originals—have been planted at various sites, ensuring that Roosa's remarkable legacy endures for future generations.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** Paragraph D states that David Williams began compiling a database 'after receiving an inquiry from a schoolteacher in Indiana.' 'An educator's curiosity' paraphrases 'a schoolteacher's inquiry,' and 'catalogue remaining trees' paraphrases 'compiling a detailed database' of surviving specimens.

**Q2:** None

- **Explanation:** Paragraph B states 'the primary objective of the experiment was to determine whether prolonged exposure to the conditions of space... would have any measurable effect on seed germination and growth.' 'Cosmic conditions' paraphrases 'conditions of space,' and 'plant development' paraphrases 'seed germination and growth.'

- 5. E

This is a plausible claim given his role in initiating the project, but it is never stated or implied.
