## Exam 58 — Part 10: Suburban Pocket Forests

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
When the municipality of Thorndale, Ontario, converted a disused parking lot into a dense pocket forest last spring, it unwittingly ignited a polarizing conversation about the role of managed wilderness in residential areas. The initiative, which drew on the Miyawaki method of rapid reforestation—a technique involving the close planting of indigenous species to accelerate canopy growth—transformed 1,200 square metres of cracked asphalt into a thriving woodland within months. According to a regional environmental survey, neighbourhoods situated within 500 metres of such urban forests experienced a 17% reduction in ambient summer temperatures, a figure that proponents argue renders the practice indispensable in an era of intensifying heat events.

Landscape ecologist Dr. Priya Narayanan, who consulted on the Thorndale project, contends that rewilding suburban corridors is not merely an aesthetic endeavour but a systemic intervention against biodiversity collapse. "What people fail to appreciate is that these miniature ecosystems function as refugia for pollinators and migratory birds whose habitats have been incrementally obliterated by conventional development," Narayanan explains. She further posits that the psychological dividends of proximity to forested spaces—diminished cortisol levels and heightened cognitive restoration—remain woefully undervalued by municipal planners preoccupied with immediate fiscal returns.

Not all residents, however, regard the proliferation of rewilded plots with equanimity. Gerald Foss, a retired property appraiser who has lived in Thorndale for over two decades, voices apprehension that unmanaged vegetation may inadvertently depreciate adjacent home values and harbour vermin. "There is a meaningful distinction between a manicured park and what essentially amounts to orchestrated neglect," Foss remarks, adding that several homeowners on his street have already lodged complaints about encroaching root systems and increased mosquito activity.

Urban planning consultant Marguerite Talbot occupies a more conciliatory position, suggesting that the discord stems largely from insufficient community engagement rather than from any inherent flaw in the rewilding concept itself. Talbot advocates for participatory design frameworks wherein residents collaborate with ecologists to delineate boundaries, select species, and establish maintenance protocols. "Rewilding need not be synonymous with abandonment," Talbot insists. "When communities are granted genuine agency in shaping these green spaces, resistance tends to dissolve—because stewardship, ultimately, is a deeply human impulse."

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. mechanics of the Miyawaki reforestation method in Canadian suburban climates.
- 2. competing perspectives on integrating managed wilderness into residential settings.
- 3. environmental advantages of converting disused infrastructure into green spaces.
- 4. dispute between ecological consultants and property appraisers over land use priorities.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. mechanics of the Miyawaki reforestation method in Canadian suburban climates: The Miyawaki method is briefly described but is not the article's focus; the article centres on differing opinions about rewilding.
✅ 2. competing perspectives on integrating managed wilderness into residential settings: The article presents Narayanan's ecological argument, Foss's residential concerns, and Talbot's conciliatory approach—multiple competing viewpoints on suburban rewilding.
❌ 3. environmental advantages of converting disused infrastructure into green spaces: Environmental benefits are discussed, but the article equally addresses criticism and community engagement, making this too narrow.
❌ 4. dispute between ecological consultants and property appraisers over land use priorities: While Narayanan and Foss disagree, the article also includes Talbot's mediating view and broader context, so framing it as a two-sided dispute is reductive.

**Q2:** None
- 1. views the rewilding initiative as fundamentally incompatible with residential neighbourhood standards.
- 2. supports the concept of urban greening but would prefer conventional manicured park designs.
- 3. believes the municipality should allocate significantly more funding to maintain the forested area.
- 4. objects primarily to the rapidity with which the vegetation was permitted to proliferate.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. views the rewilding initiative as fundamentally incompatible with residential neighbourhood standards: Foss draws a 'meaningful distinction between a manicured park and orchestrated neglect,' suggesting the forest itself is the problem, not just its management.
❌ 2. supports the concept of urban greening but would prefer conventional manicured park designs: While Foss mentions manicured parks approvingly, calling the project 'orchestrated neglect' signals opposition to the concept, not mere preference for another style.
❌ 3. believes the municipality should allocate significantly more funding to maintain the forested area: Foss's criticism targets the nature of the project itself—depreciated home values, vermin—not a lack of funding.
❌ 4. objects primarily to the rapidity with which the vegetation was permitted to proliferate: Foss's concerns centre on property values, vermin, root encroachment, and mosquitoes—not the speed of growth specifically.

**Q3:** None
- 1. reflects legitimate ecological concerns that project designers have consistently failed to address.
- 2. is an inevitable consequence of introducing dense, unmanicured vegetation near residential properties.
- 3. could be substantially mitigated through more inclusive and participatory planning processes.
- 4. stems from residents' fundamental misunderstanding of contemporary ecological principles.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. reflects legitimate ecological concerns that project designers have consistently failed to address: Talbot attributes opposition to 'insufficient community engagement,' not to designers' ecological oversights.
❌ 2. is an inevitable consequence of introducing dense, unmanicured vegetation near residential properties: Talbot explicitly argues that 'resistance tends to dissolve' when communities are given agency, meaning opposition is not inevitable.
✅ 3. could be substantially mitigated through more inclusive and participatory planning processes: Talbot advocates 'participatory design frameworks' and states that when communities have 'genuine agency,' resistance dissipates.
❌ 4. stems from residents' fundamental misunderstanding of ecological principles: Talbot never suggests residents are ignorant; she locates the problem in process, not comprehension.

**Q4:** None
- 1. failing to consult qualified landscape ecologists before approving suburban development projects.
- 2. prioritizing aesthetic considerations over ecological functionality in urban green space design.
- 3. neglecting to address the legitimate grievances raised by long-term residents such as Gerald Foss.
- 4. underestimating the broader ecological and psychological dividends of proximity to forested spaces.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. failing to consult qualified landscape ecologists before approving suburban development projects: Narayanan was herself consulted on the project; her criticism targets planners' priorities, not their consultation practices.
❌ 2. prioritizing aesthetic considerations over ecological functionality in urban green space design: Narayanan says rewilding is 'not merely an aesthetic endeavour,' but she criticizes planners for focusing on fiscal returns, not aesthetics.
❌ 3. neglecting to address the legitimate grievances raised by long-term residents such as Gerald Foss: Narayanan never references resident complaints; her critique targets planners' fixation on immediate financial considerations.
✅ 4. underestimating the broader ecological and psychological dividends of proximity to forested spaces: Narayanan explicitly states that the psychological benefits 'remain woefully undervalued by municipal planners preoccupied with immediate fiscal returns.'

**Q5:** None
- 1. the selective removal of invasive species to facilitate the natural regrowth of indigenous flora.
- 2. the dense planting of native species to hasten the development of overhead tree cover.
- 3. the gradual replacement of impervious paved surfaces with drought-tolerant vegetation.
- 4. the strategic introduction of pollinator habitats designed to restore declining migratory bird corridors.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the selective removal of invasive species to facilitate the natural regrowth of indigenous flora: The article describes active planting, not removal of invasive species.
✅ 2. the dense planting of native species to hasten the development of overhead tree cover: The Miyawaki method is described as 'close planting of indigenous species to accelerate canopy growth,' which this option paraphrases accurately.
❌ 3. the gradual replacement of impervious paved surfaces with drought-tolerant vegetation: The transformation was rapid ('within months'), not gradual, and drought tolerance is never mentioned.
❌ 4. the strategic introduction of pollinator habitats designed to restore declining migratory bird corridors: Pollinators and migratory birds are mentioned by Narayanan as beneficiaries, but this is not a description of the Miyawaki technique itself.

**Q6:** None
- 1. an unfortunate reminder
- 2. a compelling illustration
- 3. a controversial experiment
- 4. an incomplete solution
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. an unfortunate reminder: The commenter says the article 'resonated deeply' and uses positive framing, so 'unfortunate' contradicts the tone.
✅ 2. a compelling illustration: The commenter views the Thorndale project positively, calling it a demonstration of how municipalities can reimagine spaces—'compelling illustration' fits this supportive stance.
❌ 3. a controversial experiment: While the project is debated, the commenter clearly supports it, so labelling it 'controversial' conflicts with their affirming tone.
❌ 4. an incomplete solution: The commenter endorses the initiative and does not suggest it falls short as a solution.

**Q7:** None
- 1. temporary shelters
- 2. aesthetic attractions
- 3. indispensable sanctuaries
- 4. experimental zones
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. temporary shelters: Narayanan describes these ecosystems as lasting refugia, not temporary arrangements.
❌ 2. aesthetic attractions: The commenter explicitly contrasts ecological function with being 'merely decorative amenities,' ruling out an aesthetic framing.
✅ 3. indispensable sanctuaries: Narayanan calls these forests 'refugia for pollinators and migratory birds,' and the commenter echoes this by pairing the term with 'endangered species'—'indispensable sanctuaries' captures this essential protective function.
❌ 4. experimental zones: Neither Narayanan nor the commenter frames these forests as experiments; they present them as functioning ecosystems.

**Q8:** None
- 1. overlooked
- 2. vindicated
- 3. misrepresented
- 4. endangered
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. overlooked: Foss and other residents lodged complaints about practical consequences, and Talbot confirms the discord stems from 'insufficient community engagement'—residents feel their concerns are being ignored.
❌ 2. vindicated: Foss's complaints have not been validated by any outcome described in the article; he expresses frustration, not triumph.
❌ 3. misrepresented: Foss never claims his views are being distorted—his grievance is that concerns are not being heard.
❌ 4. endangered: While Foss worries about property depreciation, the commenter's empathy is about feeling unheard, not physically or financially threatened.

**Q9:** None
- 1. property values remain demonstrably unaffected
- 2. residents are meaningfully involved
- 3. ecologists override community objections
- 4. vegetation is meticulously controlled
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. property values remain demonstrably unaffected: Talbot focuses on engagement processes, not on guaranteeing property values.
✅ 2. residents are meaningfully involved: Talbot advocates 'participatory design frameworks' where residents 'collaborate with ecologists,' and the commenter immediately references 'that collaborative dimension.'
❌ 3. ecologists override community objections: This contradicts Talbot's emphasis on granting communities 'genuine agency' in the process.
❌ 4. vegetation is meticulously controlled: Talbot's focus is on community participation in decision-making, not on strict vegetation management.

**Q10:** None
- 1. passing trend
- 2. political strategy
- 3. financial burden
- 4. pressing necessity
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. passing trend: The commenter's emphatic phrasing ('not a luxury') and call for 'genuine public dialogue' indicate they view rewilding as enduringly important, not transient.
❌ 2. political strategy: The commenter frames rewilding as an ecological and community imperative, not a political manoeuvre.
❌ 3. financial burden: The commenter's supportive tone throughout contradicts framing rewilding as a cost problem.
✅ 4. pressing necessity: The commenter contrasts 'luxury' with urgency, asserting rewilding 'demands thoughtful execution'—'pressing necessity' captures this sense of imperative importance.

## Exam 59 — Part 7: Farmers Market Invitation

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Natalie,

Welcome to the neighbourhood! It was lovely meeting you and your husband at the community barbecue on Saturday. I remember how overwhelming it felt when Daniel and I first moved to Kitchener from Hamilton three years ago, so I wanted to share something that helped us feel at home.

Every Saturday morning from May through October, there is a farmers' market called the Bridgeport Market in the parking lot behind St. Andrew's Church on Lancaster Street. I discovered it by accident during our first week here. Daniel and I were driving to the hardware store for paint when we noticed the colourful tents and crowds. We pulled over out of curiosity, and I have been going almost every weekend since.

The market has around thirty vendors, but my two favourites are Henderson Farm and Maple Lane Organics. Henderson Farm sells incredible strawberries in June and the sweetest corn in August. Maple Lane Organics is known for their preserves and honey. I once bought a jar of their wildflower honey for eight dollars, thinking it was expensive, but after tasting it, I realized it was worth every penny. They sell lavender honey as well, which Daniel prefers, though I find it too floral for my taste.

What surprised me was how the market became our social life. Because we did not know anyone when we arrived, those Saturday visits turned into our way of connecting with the community. The vendors remember your name, ask about your week, and set aside items they know you like. I was genuinely touched the first time Mr. Henderson saved me a basket of his early strawberries without my even asking.

There is also a smaller Wednesday evening market at Victoria Park from June to September, but the Saturday one has a much better selection. The Wednesday market is fine for picking up a few things after work, but it only has about a dozen vendors.

I would love to take you this Saturday if you are free. It starts at seven in the morning, but I usually arrive around eight thirty because the rush has died down but everything is still well stocked. My treat for coffee at the little stand near the entrance.

Looking forward to getting to know you better!

Warm regards,
Sophie

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** Sophie is writing to Natalie mainly to .....
- 1. invite her to a community barbecue.
- 2. recommend a hardware store in the area.
- 3. suggest a way to settle into the neighbourhood.
- 4. ask for a favour at the farmers' market.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. invite her to a community barbecue: The barbecue already took place on Saturday; Sophie is not organizing another one.
❌ 2. recommend a hardware store in the area: The hardware store is only mentioned as where Sophie was heading when she accidentally found the market.
✅ 3. suggest a way to settle into the neighbourhood: Sophie says she wants to 'share something that helped us feel at home' and describes how the market became their way of connecting with the community.
❌ 4. ask for a favour at the farmers' market: Sophie is offering to take Natalie to the market, not requesting any help.

**Q2:** Sophie first found the Bridgeport Market when .....
- 1. she and Daniel noticed it on the way to another errand.
- 2. a neighbour mentioned it at a community event.
- 3. she was searching for fresh strawberries.
- 4. Mr. Henderson invited her to his stall.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. she and Daniel noticed it on the way to another errand: Sophie says they were driving to the hardware store for paint when they 'noticed the colourful tents and crowds' and pulled over.
❌ 2. a neighbour mentioned it at a community event: Sophie says she 'discovered it by accident' — no one told her about it.
❌ 3. she was searching for fresh strawberries: Sophie was on her way to buy paint, not looking for produce.
❌ 4. Mr. Henderson invited her to his stall: Mr. Henderson is a vendor she got to know later; he did not invite her initially.

**Q3:** Daniel's preference for honey is .....
- 1. wildflower honey from Henderson Farm.
- 2. the least expensive option available.
- 3. the same as Sophie's.
- 4. lavender honey from Maple Lane Organics.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. wildflower honey from Henderson Farm: The honey comes from Maple Lane Organics, not Henderson Farm; and wildflower is what Sophie bought, not Daniel's preference.
❌ 2. the least expensive option available: No price comparison between honey types is given; only the wildflower honey price is mentioned.
❌ 3. the same as Sophie's: Sophie finds the lavender honey 'too floral,' so their preferences are different.
✅ 4. lavender honey from Maple Lane Organics: Sophie states 'Daniel prefers' the lavender honey, which is sold by Maple Lane Organics.

**Q4:** The Wednesday evening market .....
- 1. runs from May through October.
- 2. has fewer vendors than the Saturday market.
- 3. is located behind St. Andrew's Church.
- 4. offers a better selection than the Saturday one.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. runs from May through October: The Wednesday market runs from June to September; the May-through-October schedule belongs to the Saturday market.
✅ 2. has fewer vendors than the Saturday market: Sophie says the Wednesday market 'only has about a dozen vendors' compared to 'around thirty' on Saturday.
❌ 3. is located behind St. Andrew's Church: St. Andrew's Church is where the Saturday market is held; the Wednesday market is at Victoria Park.
❌ 4. offers a better selection than the Saturday one: Sophie says 'the Saturday one has a much better selection' — the opposite.

**Q5:** Sophie felt "genuinely touched" because Mr. Henderson .....
- 1. anticipated what she wanted without being asked.
- 2. offered her a special discount on produce.
- 3. introduced her to other people in the community.
- 4. invited her family to visit his farm.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. anticipated what she wanted without being asked: Mr. Henderson 'saved me a basket of his early strawberries without my even asking' — he remembered her preferences on his own.
❌ 2. offered her a special discount on produce: There is no mention of any discount; he set aside strawberries, not reduced prices.
❌ 3. introduced her to other people in the community: Sophie describes the market generally as her social life, but does not say Mr. Henderson made introductions.
❌ 4. invited her family to visit his farm: There is no mention of visiting Henderson Farm; their interactions take place at the market.

**Q6:** Sophie usually arrives at the market at eight thirty because .....
- 1. that is when the best items become available.
- 2. she needs time to drive from Hamilton.
- 3. it is less crowded but still has good stock.
- 4. the coffee stand does not open until then.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. that is when the best items become available: The items are 'still well stocked' at 8:30, meaning they were available earlier too — they don't first appear at that time.
❌ 2. she needs time to drive from Hamilton: Sophie moved from Hamilton to Kitchener three years ago; she now lives in Kitchener.
✅ 3. it is less crowded but still has good stock: Sophie says 'the rush has died down but everything is still well stocked' at 8:30.
❌ 4. the coffee stand does not open until then: Sophie mentions buying coffee but gives no information about when the stand opens.

**Q7:** None
- 1. thoughtful
- 2. lengthy
- 3. surprising
- 4. formal
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. thoughtful: Sophie went out of her way to share helpful local information with a new neighbour, making 'thoughtful' the most fitting description of her email.
❌ 2. lengthy: While the email contains detail, calling it 'lengthy' doesn't match the grateful and warm tone of Natalie's reply.
❌ 3. surprising: There is nothing unexpected about a neighbour writing after meeting at a barbecue; Natalie shows appreciation, not surprise.
❌ 4. formal: Sophie's email uses a friendly, personal tone with phrases like 'My treat for coffee' — it is not formal.

**Q8:** None
- 1. local strawberries
- 2. imported spices
- 3. fresh honey
- 4. organic butter
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. local strawberries: Strawberries are sold by Henderson Farm, not Maple Lane Organics — the sentence says he'd want to check out Maple Lane specifically.
❌ 2. imported spices: No vendor at the market sells imported spices; this is not mentioned in Sophie's email.
✅ 3. fresh honey: Maple Lane Organics is 'known for their preserves and honey' according to Sophie's email, so Natalie's husband wanting honey would lead him to that vendor.
❌ 4. organic butter: Butter is not mentioned among Maple Lane Organics' products; Sophie lists only preserves and honey.

**Q9:** None
- 1. typical
- 2. heartwarming
- 3. remarkable
- 4. inconvenient
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. typical: Natalie says 'that kind of personal connection is hard to find,' indicating it is unusual, not typical.
✅ 2. heartwarming: Natalie is emotionally moved by the idea that vendors remember names and set aside items — 'heartwarming' captures the warm, personal tone that connects to 'personal connection is hard to find these days.'
❌ 3. remarkable: While this is positive, it expresses intellectual admiration rather than the emotional warmth conveyed by Natalie's focus on 'personal connection' — 'heartwarming' better fits the sentiment.
❌ 4. inconvenient: Being remembered by name is presented as a positive thing, not a source of inconvenience.

**Q10:** None
- 1. drive to Lancaster Street together
- 2. visit Henderson Farm with you
- 3. try the Wednesday market first
- 4. come along with you
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. drive to Lancaster Street together: While the market is on Lancaster Street, this phrasing is awkward as a response to Sophie's invitation and sounds like a destination rather than an activity.
❌ 2. visit Henderson Farm with you: Sophie invited Natalie to the market, not to a farm visit; Henderson Farm is a vendor at the market.
❌ 3. try the Wednesday market first: Sophie specifically invited Natalie to the Saturday market and noted the Wednesday one has a weaker selection.
✅ 4. come along with you: Sophie offered to take Natalie 'this Saturday,' and 'I'd love to come along with you' is the natural, direct acceptance of that invitation.

**Q11:** None
- 1. treat you
- 2. bring my own cup
- 3. skip the coffee stand
- 4. try the lavender honey instead
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. treat you: Sophie offered 'My treat for coffee,' so Natalie is reciprocating by saying she'll treat Sophie next time — a natural, polite response.
❌ 2. bring my own cup: This doesn't logically respond to Sophie's offer to buy coffee; it sidesteps the gesture.
❌ 3. skip the coffee stand: Natalie is accepting the invitation warmly, not rejecting the coffee offer.
❌ 4. try the lavender honey instead: Lavender honey is sold by Maple Lane Organics and has nothing to do with the coffee offer Sophie made.

**Q3:** None
- 1. you'd need to call ahead and book a time slot

## Exam 59 — Part 9: Peat Bog Mummies

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. Bog bodies are remarkably well-preserved human remains discovered in the peat bogs of Northern Europe. These waterlogged environments, found predominantly in Denmark, Germany, the Netherlands, the United Kingdom, and Ireland, have yielded hundreds of such specimens over the past several centuries. The earliest recorded discovery dates to 1640, when a body was unearthed in Holstein, Germany. While some bog bodies are thousands of years old, the majority date from the Iron Age, roughly between 800 BCE and 200 CE. Despite their antiquity, many retain intact skin, hair, and internal organs, offering researchers an extraordinary glimpse into prehistoric life.

B. The exceptional preservation of bog bodies is attributable to the unique chemical properties of peat bogs. These acidic, oxygen-deprived wetlands contain high concentrations of sphagnum moss, which releases a substance called sphagnan. This compound effectively halts bacterial decomposition by binding to nitrogen and calcium, two elements essential for microbial activity. Consequently, soft tissues such as skin and organs are preserved in a manner comparable to the tanning of leather. However, the same acidic conditions that protect soft tissue simultaneously dissolve calcium phosphate in bones, often leaving skeletons severely weakened or entirely absent.

C. Among the most celebrated bog bodies is Tollund Man, discovered in 1950 near Silkeborg, Denmark. Dating to approximately 400 BCE, his remarkably serene facial expression and well-preserved features captivated the scientific community. A braided leather noose found tightly fastened around his neck indicated he had been hanged. Similarly, Grauballe Man, unearthed just two years later and only 18 kilometres away, exhibited evidence of a throat wound inflicted from ear to ear. Analysis of his stomach contents revealed a final meal of porridge made from over 60 plant species, including grains and seeds, consumed 12 to 24 hours before death.

D. The circumstances surrounding many bog body deaths have sparked considerable scholarly debate. A prevailing theory, advanced by Danish archaeologist P.V. Glob in his influential 1965 work The Bog People, suggests these individuals were sacrificial victims offered to fertility gods during religious ceremonies. Evidence of ritualistic treatment—such as deliberate positioning of limbs and placement of stakes to pin bodies beneath the water—supports this interpretation. Other researchers, however, contend that some bog bodies may have been executed criminals or outcasts. Regardless of these disagreements, the ancient remains continue to provide invaluable insights into the spiritual beliefs, dietary habits, and social hierarchies of Iron Age societies.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** Paragraph A lists Denmark, Germany, the Netherlands, the United Kingdom, and Ireland as locations where bog bodies have been found. 'Multiple European nations' paraphrases this enumeration and 'ancient remains' paraphrases 'remarkably well-preserved human remains.'

**Q2:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** Paragraph B states sphagnum moss releases sphagnan, which 'halts bacterial decomposition by binding to nitrogen and calcium, two elements essential for microbial activity.' 'Moss-derived compounds' paraphrases sphagnan from sphagnum moss, and 'deprive microorganisms of nutrients' captures the effect of binding those elements.

**Q3:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 5
- **Explanation:** The passage provides archaeological dates for certain bog bodies (e.g., Tollund Man circa 400 BCE) but never discusses forensic determination of how old these individuals were biologically at the time of death. This information is entirely absent.

**Q4:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** Paragraph D states P.V. Glob's 'influential 1965 work The Bog People suggests these individuals were sacrificial victims offered to fertility gods.' 'Mid-twentieth-century publication' paraphrases '1965 work,' 'killed to honor deities' paraphrases 'sacrificial victims offered to fertility gods.'

## Exam 59 — Part 10: Farmland Rewilding Projects

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
When the municipality of Greystone, Ontario, sanctioned a proposal to convert 1,200 hectares of disused agricultural land into a naturalized wildlife corridor last spring, the decision ignited a vigorous debate that has since reverberated across rural communities nationwide. According to a 2023 federal land-use survey, approximately fourteen percent of Canada's previously cultivated farmland now lies fallow, a figure that rewilding proponents regard as an unprecedented ecological opportunity. Conservation biologist Dr. Nadia Ferencz, who spearheaded the Greystone initiative, contends that restoring native ecosystems on such parcels yields measurable environmental dividends. "Abandoned cropland that is permitted to regenerate naturally can sequester up to three times more carbon than it did under conventional tillage," Ferencz notes. She further argues that the reintroduction of indigenous flora and fauna fosters biodiversity in regions where monoculture farming had long suppressed it, thereby fortifying the resilience of surrounding landscapes against climate volatility.
Not everyone, however, regards these tracts of idle soil as suitable candidates for ecological restoration. Duncan Thibault, a fourth-generation grain farmer whose family holdings border the Greystone corridor, characterizes the rewilding scheme as a squandering of productive capacity that the nation can scarcely afford. "We are witnessing arable land—land that could sustain communities—being surrendered to thistles and coyotes at precisely the moment when global food security is under mounting strain," Thibault asserts. He points out that many rural economies already grapple with dwindling populations and diminished tax revenues, conditions that the permanent withdrawal of agricultural land from production would only deepen. For Thibault, the initiative represents an urban idealism fundamentally disconnected from the pragmatic realities of those who have worked the soil for generations.
Environmental economist Professor Lina Marchetti of the University of Guelph occupies a decidedly more nuanced position, suggesting that the dichotomy between rewilding and food production is, in numerous instances, a false one. Her research indicates that strategically rewilded buffer zones adjacent to active farms can enhance pollinator activity and mitigate soil erosion, ultimately boosting yields on neighbouring plots. "The assumption that every hectare must be either entirely cultivated or entirely wild overlooks the considerable synergies that integrated land management can generate," Marchetti observes. She cautions, nonetheless, that poorly planned rewilding efforts risk introducing invasive species, which could undermine the very agricultural operations they are meant to complement.
Ferencz, for her part, remains resolute in the face of such criticisms. "Restoring ecological function to degraded land is not an abdication of agricultural responsibility—it is an investment in the very natural infrastructure upon which all farming ultimately depends."

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. rural communities should receive greater federal assistance to maintain cultivated acreage.
- 2. converting dormant farmland to wildlife habitat represents sound environmental policy.
- 3. Canadian farmers are adequately equipped to confront the consequences of climate volatility.
- 4. municipal governments possess the legal authority to rezone privately held agricultural land.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. rural communities should receive greater federal assistance to maintain cultivated acreage: Federal assistance or subsidies for farmers are never discussed in the article.
✅ 2. converting dormant farmland to wildlife habitat represents sound environmental policy: The article debates whether repurposing disused agricultural land for ecological restoration is wise, with Ferencz supporting it, Thibault opposing it, and Marchetti proposing a middle ground.
❌ 3. Canadian farmers are adequately equipped to confront the consequences of climate volatility: While climate resilience is mentioned, the central debate concerns land-use policy, not farmer preparedness.
❌ 4. municipal governments possess the legal authority to rezone privately held agricultural land: The municipality's decision is a background detail; the article focuses on the merits and risks of rewilding, not jurisdictional authority.

**Q2:** None
- 1. agricultural scientists pursuing new avenues for research funding.
- 2. conservation biologists with negligible understanding of farming economics.
- 3. municipal officials hoping to generate higher property tax revenues.
- 4. individuals removed from the day-to-day demands of working the land.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. agricultural scientists pursuing new avenues for research funding: Thibault never attributes the initiative to researchers seeking grant money.
❌ 2. conservation biologists with negligible understanding of farming economics: Although Ferencz is a biologist, Thibault's criticism is broader—he targets 'urban idealism,' not a specific profession's ignorance.
❌ 3. municipal officials hoping to generate higher property tax revenues: Thibault argues the opposite—that the initiative will diminish tax revenues, not increase them.
✅ 4. individuals removed from the day-to-day demands of working the land: Thibault explicitly describes the initiative as 'an urban idealism fundamentally disconnected from the pragmatic realities of those who have worked the soil for generations.'

**Q3:** None
- 1. require deliberate planning to avoid inflicting unintended ecological harm.
- 2. must invariably prioritise biodiversity conservation over crop productivity.
- 3. should be confined exclusively to land that has never been cultivated.
- 4. will inevitably diminish the profitability of adjacent farming operations.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. require deliberate planning to avoid inflicting unintended ecological harm: Marchetti cautions that 'poorly planned rewilding efforts risk introducing invasive species,' directly supporting the need for careful planning.
❌ 2. must invariably prioritise biodiversity conservation over crop productivity: Marchetti rejects this dichotomy, arguing that rewilding and farming can produce synergies rather than requiring one to override the other.
❌ 3. should be confined exclusively to land that has never been cultivated: Marchetti discusses buffer zones adjacent to active farms, not untouched land, and never proposes such a restriction.
❌ 4. will inevitably diminish the profitability of adjacent farming operations: Marchetti's research indicates the opposite—that rewilded buffer zones can enhance pollinator activity and boost yields on neighbouring plots.

**Q4:** None
- 1. deliberately dismissing the economic dividends generated by ecological corridors.
- 2. misrepresenting the proportion of Canadian land that currently lies unproductive.
- 3. overlooking the extent to which agriculture relies on intact natural systems.
- 4. underestimating the practical difficulty of restoring biodiversity to depleted soils.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. deliberately dismissing the economic dividends generated by ecological corridors: Ferencz frames her argument in ecological rather than economic terms in her concluding remark, focusing on 'natural infrastructure,' not financial returns.
❌ 2. misrepresenting the proportion of Canadian land that currently lies unproductive: The federal survey statistic is cited by the article's author, not disputed by Ferencz or attributed to Thibault.
✅ 3. overlooking the extent to which agriculture relies on intact natural systems: Ferencz states that restoration 'is an investment in the very natural infrastructure upon which all farming ultimately depends,' implying critics fail to recognise farming's reliance on healthy ecosystems.
❌ 4. underestimating the practical difficulty of restoring biodiversity to depleted soils: Ferencz expresses confidence in restoration and never raises concerns about its difficulty.

**Q5:** None
- 1. enhancing pollination and curbing soil degradation on neighbouring plots.
- 2. providing diversified revenue streams through ecotourism ventures.
- 3. absorbing excess precipitation that would otherwise inundate cultivated fields.
- 4. establishing natural windbreaks that shield crops from extreme weather events.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. enhancing pollination and curbing soil degradation on neighbouring plots: Marchetti's research indicates that rewilded buffer zones 'can enhance pollinator activity and mitigate soil erosion, ultimately boosting yields on neighbouring plots.'
❌ 2. providing diversified revenue streams through ecotourism ventures: Ecotourism is never mentioned in the article.
❌ 3. absorbing excess precipitation that would otherwise inundate cultivated fields: Water management and flood prevention are not discussed anywhere in the article.
❌ 4. establishing natural windbreaks that shield crops from extreme weather events: While climate resilience is mentioned by Ferencz, windbreaks are never cited as a specific mechanism by any expert.

**Q6:** None
- 1. trivial
- 2. contentious
- 3. resolved
- 4. theoretical
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. trivial: The article presents a vigorous, multi-sided debate—the commenter clearly finds the issue significant, not trivial.
✅ 2. contentious: The article describes a 'vigorous debate' with sharply opposing viewpoints, making 'contentious' the best fit for the commenter's acknowledgment of the issue's divisive nature.
❌ 3. resolved: The debate is ongoing with no resolution; the commenter's own deliberation confirms the issue remains unsettled.
❌ 4. theoretical: The Greystone initiative is an actual, sanctioned proposal with real-world implications, not merely a hypothetical scenario.

**Q7:** None
- 1. reverse the decline of
- 2. permanently reshape the trajectory of
- 3. profit commercially from improving
- 4. conduct further research into
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. reverse the decline of: Ferencz argues that restoring native ecosystems fosters biodiversity and fortifies landscape resilience—directly aiming to reverse ecological decline on degraded land.
❌ 2. permanently reshape the trajectory of: This overstates Ferencz's claims; she discusses measurable benefits but does not assert permanent, trajectory-altering transformation.
❌ 3. profit commercially from improving: Ferencz's argument centres on environmental dividends, not commercial profit from ecological restoration.
❌ 4. conduct further research into: Ferencz advocates for active restoration, not merely further study of ecological health.

**Q8:** None
- 1. attract unwanted federal scrutiny toward
- 2. generate unforeseen opportunities within
- 3. further undermine the viability of
- 4. improve long-term infrastructure within
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. attract unwanted federal scrutiny toward: Thibault's concern is economic decline, not regulatory attention from the federal government.
❌ 2. generate unforeseen opportunities within: This contradicts Thibault's argument; he warns of harm, not hidden benefits.
✅ 3. further undermine the viability of: Thibault says rural economies 'already grapple with dwindling populations and diminished tax revenues' and that permanent land withdrawal would 'deepen' these conditions—undermining their viability.
❌ 4. improve long-term infrastructure within: Thibault sees the initiative as harmful to rural communities, not beneficial to their infrastructure.

**Q9:** None
- 1. equally productive
- 2. scientifically comparable
- 3. economically balanced
- 4. mutually exclusive
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. equally productive: The commenter's point is about coexistence, not about whether rewilding and farming yield equivalent outputs.
❌ 2. scientifically comparable: Marchetti does not compare the two activities on a scientific basis; she discusses their potential synergy.
❌ 3. economically balanced: Marchetti's observation concerns integration of land use, not economic parity between the two approaches.
✅ 4. mutually exclusive: Marchetti explicitly argues that 'the dichotomy between rewilding and food production is, in numerous instances, a false one,' meaning they need not be mutually exclusive.

**Q10:** None
- 1. minimal bureaucratic interference
- 2. considerable political compromise
- 3. rigorous scientific oversight and planning
- 4. substantial financial backing from taxpayers
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. minimal bureaucratic interference: The commenter emphasises caution, not deregulation; reducing oversight contradicts the need for responsible implementation.
❌ 2. considerable political compromise: Marchetti's warning is ecological (invasive species), not political; the commenter echoes this scientific concern.
✅ 3. rigorous scientific oversight and planning: Marchetti warns that 'poorly planned rewilding efforts risk introducing invasive species,' and the commenter argues the initiative 'demands' careful preparation—aligning with scientific oversight.
❌ 4. substantial financial backing from taxpayers: Funding sources are never discussed in the article, and the commenter's concern is about planning quality, not financial investment.

## Exam 6 — Part 7: Street Art Festival

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Hi Alex,

Thanks again for taking on the kids' stencil table! Here's where the festival stands.

After two months of waiting, the borough has said yes to five walls, one more than we asked for. The extra one is the library wall facing the metro exit, so it'll be the first thing people see. There's one catch. The owner of the dry cleaner's on Galt Avenue loved our idea in May, but he never signed the borough's form for his wall. The borough won't accept his son's signature, and the owner is away in Quebec City until Monday.

All four local artists have said yes, too. Mireille Gendron, who painted the giant heron on the pharmacy, will do the library wall on the festival's first morning so people can watch her work. As for the open call for new artists, we got almost 500 portfolios, five times as many as last year, and I was touched by how many came from teenagers. We can't go through them all by June 30, so we've pushed the decision back two weeks, to July 14.

Your table has moved. Last year it was in the park, but the borough no longer allows spray paint near the splash pad, so this year you'll be in the schoolyard across the street.

Now the bad news. All three food trucks we'd booked cancelled last week, because a bigger event in downtown Montreal offered them more money for that weekend. The only truck still free wants $900 just to show up. The other choice is having the bakery and the Haitian restaurant on Wellington Street sell food from sidewalk tables. They're both keen, but we'd need a permit, and the borough takes ten days to issue one.

Finally, the teaser murals. I'd love artists to paint small pieces on plywood boards in a few shop windows for the month before the festival, each showing just a corner of a bigger design. The boards and paint would cost about $600, though, and the rest of the committee thinks posters would do the job for a fraction of that. What would you do about the food and the teasers? The committee meets on Thursday to vote on both, so let me know before then.

Cheers,
Jamie

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** Mireille Gendron is going to .....
- 1. run the stencil table for the kids
- 2. paint the wall at the dry cleaner's
- 3. paint a wall while visitors look on
- 4. repaint her giant heron on the pharmacy
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. run the stencil table for the kids: That is Alex's job. Jamie thanks him "for taking on the kids' stencil table".
❌ 2. paint the wall at the dry cleaner's: Mireille will paint "the library wall". The dry cleaner's wall on Galt Avenue is a different wall, and it is still waiting for the owner's signature.
✅ 3. paint a wall while visitors look on: Jamie writes that Mireille "will do the library wall on the festival's first morning so people can watch her work."
❌ 4. repaint her giant heron on the pharmacy: The heron is work she has already done: she is the artist "who painted the giant heron on the pharmacy". At the festival she will paint the library wall.

**Q2:** The decision on the new artists has been put off because .....
- 1. the four local artists have not replied yet
- 2. so many of the portfolios came from teenagers
- 3. the committee has to vote on the food first
- 4. there are too many portfolios to read in time
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the four local artists have not replied yet: The local artists have already answered: "All four local artists have said yes, too."
❌ 2. so many of the portfolios came from teenagers: Jamie "was touched by how many came from teenagers", but that is his feeling about the portfolios, not the reason for the delay.
❌ 3. the committee has to vote on the food first: The Thursday vote is about the food and the teaser murals ("The committee meets on Thursday to vote on both"). Nothing links it to choosing the new artists.
✅ 4. there are too many portfolios to read in time: There are "almost 500 portfolios", and Jamie says, "We can't go through them all by June 30, so we've pushed the decision back two weeks".

**Q3:** Unlike last year, the stencil table will be .....
- 1. in the park next to the splash pad
- 2. on the sidewalk along Wellington Street
- 3. at the school across from the park
- 4. beside the library near the metro exit
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. in the park next to the splash pad: That is where it was last year: "Last year it was in the park, but the borough no longer allows spray paint near the splash pad".
❌ 2. on the sidewalk along Wellington Street: The sidewalk tables are for food, if the bakery and the Haitian restaurant "sell food from sidewalk tables". They are not for the stencil table.
✅ 3. at the school across from the park: Jamie tells Alex, "this year you'll be in the schoolyard across the street", that is, across the street from the park where the table was last year.
❌ 4. beside the library near the metro exit: The library is where Mireille will paint ("the library wall facing the metro exit"). It is not where the stencil table will be.

**Q4:** To keep the Galt Avenue wall in the festival, Jamie will most likely .....
- 1. have the owner's son sign the form instead
- 2. tell the borough the owner liked the idea
- 3. drive to Quebec City to find the owner
- 4. ask the owner to sign when he is back
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. have the owner's son sign the form instead: This cannot work: "The borough won't accept his son's signature".
❌ 2. tell the borough the owner liked the idea: The owner "loved our idea in May", but the problem is that "he never signed the borough's form for his wall". The borough needs his signature, not just his support.
❌ 3. drive to Quebec City to find the owner: There is no need to travel. The owner is "away in Quebec City until Monday", so he will soon be home.
✅ 4. ask the owner to sign when he is back: Only the owner's own signature will do, and he is "away in Quebec City until Monday". Jamie does not say what he will do, but the natural next step is to get the owner to sign once he returns.

**Q5:** The three food trucks cancelled because .....
- 1. the borough would not give them a permit
- 2. the festival could not pay the fee they asked
- 3. they got a better offer from another event
- 4. local shops had already taken their places
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the borough would not give them a permit: The permit is for the bakery and the restaurant: "we'd need a permit, and the borough takes ten days to issue one." It has nothing to do with the trucks.
❌ 2. the festival could not pay the fee they asked: The trucks were already booked and never asked the festival for more. They left because another event "offered them more money for that weekend." The $900 fee belongs to a different truck: "The only truck still free wants $900 just to show up."
✅ 3. they got a better offer from another event: The trucks cancelled "because a bigger event in downtown Montreal offered them more money for that weekend."
❌ 4. local shops had already taken their places: The bakery and the Haitian restaurant are only a possible replacement now that the trucks are gone ("The other choice is having the bakery and the Haitian restaurant"). They did not cause the cancellation.

**Q6:** Why is Jamie writing to Alex?
- 1. To ask him to look after the stencil table
- 2. To ask what he thinks about two decisions
- 3. To invite him to Thursday's committee vote
- 4. To tell him the festival has been pushed back
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. To ask him to look after the stencil table: Alex has already agreed to do this. Jamie begins, "Thanks again for taking on the kids' stencil table!"
✅ 2. To ask what he thinks about two decisions: After giving his news, Jamie asks, "What would you do about the food and the teasers?" He wants Alex's opinion before the committee votes on both on Thursday.
❌ 3. To invite him to Thursday's committee vote: Jamie mentions the vote but does not invite Alex to it. He wants Alex's answer first: "so let me know before then."
❌ 4. To tell him the festival has been pushed back: Only the choice of new artists has been moved: "we've pushed the decision back two weeks, to July 14." Nothing in the email says the festival itself has moved.

**Q7:** None
- 1. pharmacy
- 2. bakery
- 3. metro exit
- 4. dry cleaner's
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. pharmacy: The pharmacy is where Mireille painted her giant heron. The wall the borough added is "the library wall facing the metro exit".
❌ 2. bakery: The bakery may sell food from tables on Wellington Street. The email never connects it to the wall the borough added.
✅ 3. metro exit: The borough gave the festival a wall more than it asked for ("one more than we asked for"), and "The extra one is the library wall facing the metro exit".
❌ 4. dry cleaner's: The dry cleaner's on Galt Avenue has its own wall, which is still waiting for the owner's signature. The extra wall is the library wall "facing the metro exit".

**Q8:** None
- 1. get the dry cleaner's form signed
- 2. order plywood for the teaser boards
- 3. ask about using the library wall
- 4. get the sidewalk tables approved
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. get the dry cleaner's form signed: The form has to wait for the owner, who is "away in Quebec City until Monday". The ten days are for a different paper from the borough, the permit for the food tables.
❌ 2. order plywood for the teaser boards: The plywood is not a borough matter, and the committee has not voted on the teasers yet. The borough's ten days are for the food permit.
❌ 3. ask about using the library wall: There is nothing left to ask. The borough has already given the library wall: "The extra one is the library wall facing the metro exit".
✅ 4. get the sidewalk tables approved: For the bakery and the restaurant to "sell food from sidewalk tables", Jamie says "we'd need a permit, and the borough takes ten days to issue one."

**Q9:** None
- 1. about as much as
- 2. a little less than
- 3. far less than
- 4. twice as much as
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. about as much as: The costs are not close. The boards "would cost about $600", and posters would cost only "a fraction of that."
❌ 2. a little less than: Posters are cheaper, but not by a little. The committee thinks they "would do the job for a fraction of that", that is, for a small part of the $600 the boards would cost.
✅ 3. far less than: The boards "would cost about $600, though, and the rest of the committee thinks posters would do the job for a fraction of that." A small part of $600 is far less.
❌ 4. twice as much as: This turns the facts around. The boards are the expensive choice at "about $600", and posters cost "a fraction of that."

**Q10:** None
- 1. two months
- 2. ten days
- 3. two weeks
- 4. a month
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. two months: Two months is how long the festival waited for the borough: "After two months of waiting, the borough has said yes to five walls".
❌ 2. ten days: Ten days is how long the borough takes to issue the food permit: "the borough takes ten days to issue one."
❌ 3. two weeks: Two weeks is how far the decision on new artists was moved: "we've pushed the decision back two weeks".
✅ 4. a month: Jamie wants the boards in shop windows "for the month before the festival".

**Q11:** None
- 1. committee
- 2. borough
- 3. artists
- 4. restaurant
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. committee: "The committee meets on Thursday to vote on both", and "the rest of the committee thinks posters would do the job", so Jamie has to change their minds about the teasers.
❌ 2. borough: The borough has already "said yes to five walls", and it has no part in Thursday's vote.
❌ 3. artists: The artists are already on board ("All four local artists have said yes, too"), and they do not vote on Thursday.
❌ 4. restaurant: The Haitian restaurant is already willing ("They're both keen"). The vote on Thursday belongs to the committee.

## Exam 6 — Part 8: Meal Kit Comparison

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
DINNER, DELIVERED
Four meal-kit services that deliver in Calgary
All prices are per week for two people, before GST.

FOOTHILLS FRESH
• Fresh ingredients with step-by-step recipe cards
• Dinners ready in 30 to 40 minutes
• 3 or 4 dinners a week
• Regular menu plus 6 vegetarian dinners every week: mix both in one box
• $78 for 3 dinners, $99 for 4 dinners
• Delivered Tuesday or Friday evening (you choose), 5 to 9 p.m.
• Choose or skip a week online until Sunday at midnight
• foothillsfresh.ca

CHINOOK KITCHEN
• Fully cooked dinners: heat and eat in 5 minutes
• 5 or 7 dinners a week
• Halal and gluten-free choices
• $109 for 5 dinners
• Delivered Mondays, 8 a.m. to noon
• Dinners keep up to 6 days in the fridge
• Phone orders only: 403-282-4471, by Thursday at noon for the next Monday

PRAIRIE SPOON
• Chef-designed recipes with wine or tea pairing suggestions
• Dinners ready in 45 to 60 minutes
• 2 or 3 dinners a week
• Meatless swap for one or both portions of most recipes
• From $92 for 2 dinners
• New chef's menu every week
• Delivered Wednesday or Saturday afternoon (you choose), 1 to 5 p.m.
• prairiespoon.ca

HARVEST CRATE
• Vegetables, grains and cheese from farms near Red Deer
• Dinners ready in 20 to 30 minutes
• 4 vegetarian dinners a week
• $84 a week
• Free delivery anywhere in Calgary
• Reusable insulated crate: leave it out and the driver picks it up at your next delivery
• Questions? Call 403-719-5530

★ Menus and prices change often, so check before you order.

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. set the empty crate out for the driver
- 2. choose recipes from the chef's new menu
- 3. call my order in before midday Thursday
- 4. place my order online by Sunday night
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. set the empty crate out for the driver: The crate ("leave it out and the driver picks it up") belongs to Harvest Crate. Chidi's first service was "the heat-and-eat one", which is Chinook Kitchen ("Fully cooked dinners: heat and eat in 5 minutes"), and it has no crate.
❌ 2. choose recipes from the chef's new menu: "New chef's menu every week" is a Prairie Spoon line. Chinook Kitchen sends dinners that are already cooked, so there are no recipes to choose.
✅ 3. call my order in before midday Thursday: The "heat-and-eat one" is Chinook Kitchen, which takes "Phone orders only", "by Thursday at noon for the next Monday". Having to phone in every week is the "hassle".
❌ 4. place my order online by Sunday night: Ordering online "until Sunday at midnight" belongs to Foothills Fresh, the recipe-card service. Chinook Kitchen takes "Phone orders only".

**Q2:** None
- 1. phoned them to put my order on hold
- 2. cancelled that week's box online in advance
- 3. kept the dinners in my fridge until I got back
- 4. left the crate out for them to collect
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. phoned them to put my order on hold: The "recipe-card one" is Foothills Fresh ("step-by-step recipe cards"), and the only contact the sheet gives for it is "foothillsfresh.ca". There is no phone number to call.
✅ 2. cancelled that week's box online in advance: Foothills Fresh lets you "Choose or skip a week online until Sunday at midnight". Skipping the week of the course is why "nothing went to waste".
❌ 3. kept the dinners in my fridge until I got back: Only Chinook Kitchen says its dinners "keep up to 6 days in the fridge", and Chidi had already left that service for the recipe-card one.
❌ 4. left the crate out for them to collect: The crate belongs to Harvest Crate ("leave it out and the driver picks it up"), which Chidi has not tried. Leaving a crate out would not stop food from going to waste either.

**Q3:** None
- 1. you can make one portion of most dinners meatless
- 2. every dinner in the box is meat-free
- 3. it has halal and gluten-free dinners to choose from
- 4. you can each pick your own kind of dinner
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. you can make one portion of most dinners meatless: "Meatless swap for one or both portions of most recipes" is a Prairie Spoon line. Chidi's service, Foothills Fresh, mixes whole regular and vegetarian dinners instead.
❌ 2. every dinner in the box is meat-free: "4 vegetarian dinners a week" is Harvest Crate. Foothills Fresh also has a "Regular menu", and an all-vegetarian box would not suit Emeka, who would "have steak every night".
❌ 3. it has halal and gluten-free dinners to choose from: "Halal and gluten-free choices" are listed for Chinook Kitchen, not Foothills Fresh, and neither one means a dinner has no meat.
✅ 4. you can each pick your own kind of dinner: Foothills Fresh offers a "Regular menu plus 6 vegetarian dinners every week: mix both in one box". Odette "doesn't eat meat" and Emeka loves steak, so each of them can have their own dinners in the same order.

**Q4:** None
- 1. Tuesday evenings
- 2. Wednesday afternoons
- 3. Friday evenings
- 4. Saturday afternoons
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. Tuesday evenings: Foothills Fresh does deliver on "Tuesday or Friday evening", but Tuesday is one of Emeka's "twelve-hour shifts from Saturday to Tuesday", so he cannot be sure to be home when the box comes.
❌ 2. Wednesday afternoons: Emeka is off on Wednesday, but "Wednesday or Saturday afternoon" is when Prairie Spoon delivers. Foothills Fresh, Chidi's service, only comes on Tuesday or Friday.
✅ 3. Friday evenings: Foothills Fresh is "Delivered Tuesday or Friday evening (you choose)". Emeka works "from Saturday to Tuesday", so he is off from Wednesday to Friday, and Friday is the only one of its two days when he will be home.
❌ 4. Saturday afternoons: Saturday is a Prairie Spoon delivery day, not a Foothills Fresh one, and Emeka's shifts run "from Saturday to Tuesday", so he would be on shift that day.

**Q5:** None
- 1. the sheet doesn't say which days they deliver
- 2. the sheet doesn't give a weekly price for it
- 3. the sheet doesn't say how long its dinners take to make
- 4. the sheet doesn't say whether they take the crate back
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. the sheet doesn't say which days they deliver: The farm crate is Harvest Crate ("from farms near Red Deer"). It only says "Free delivery anywhere in Calgary" and gives no delivery days or times, while Emeka needs to be home "when the box comes".
❌ 2. the sheet doesn't give a weekly price for it: Harvest Crate costs "$84 a week", and the sheet says "All prices are per week for two people".
❌ 3. the sheet doesn't say how long its dinners take to make: Harvest Crate's dinners are "ready in 20 to 30 minutes".
❌ 4. the sheet doesn't say whether they take the crate back: Harvest Crate says "leave it out and the driver picks it up at your next delivery".

**Q6:** None
- 1. at his mother's house on Sunday
- 2. after a health talk at his workplace
- 3. inside his first heat-and-eat delivery
- 4. during a visit to his own dietitian
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. at his mother's house on Sunday: Chidi "promised" Emeka the sheet "at Mum's on Sunday", so he already had it by then.
✅ 2. after a health talk at his workplace: Chidi writes "A dietitian who came in to talk to our department left a stack of them in the staff room". Both "our department" and "the staff room" show that this happened where he works.
❌ 3. inside his first heat-and-eat delivery: The sheet came from "a stack" in "the staff room", and Chidi tried the services only after that ("I've tried two of the four since then").
❌ 4. during a visit to his own dietitian: The dietitian "came in to talk to our department". Chidi did not go to see her on his own.

**Q7:** None
- 1. work in the same department
- 2. took the same course in Saskatoon
- 3. grew up in the same family
- 4. live together with Odette
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. work in the same department: The "department" is Chidi's, at "the office". Emeka works "twelve-hour shifts" on a "rotation", and nothing says the two work together.
❌ 2. took the same course in Saskatoon: Chidi writes "When I was away on that week-long course", so only he went. He and Emeka have known each other "since we were kids".
✅ 3. grew up in the same family: They both go to "Mum's" on Sunday, and Chidi writes "You've been the family cook since we were kids". They are brothers, so Chidi has known Emeka's cooking since childhood.
❌ 4. live together with Odette: Chidi writes "Now for you two" about Emeka and Odette, and his own box sat on "my front step", so he lives in his own home.

**Q8:** None
- 1. the food was still safe to eat once it thawed
- 2. he was glad to stop paying for meal kits
- 3. he had never liked the heat-and-eat dinners
- 4. it pushed him into a habit he now enjoys
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the food was still safe to eat once it thawed: Chidi says "I had to throw the whole lot out", so the food in the box was lost.
❌ 2. he was glad to stop paying for meal kits: He did not stop. He "switched to the recipe-card one" and still uses it.
❌ 3. he had never liked the heat-and-eat dinners: He says "The food was good". The trouble was the weekly ordering and the Monday morning delivery.
✅ 4. it pushed him into a habit he now enjoys: A blessing in disguise is a bad thing that turns out well. Losing the frozen box made him switch to cooking from recipe cards, and now "I actually enjoy cooking".

## Exam 6 — Part 9: Calligraphy Across Cultures

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. The word calligraphy comes from two Greek words, kallos, meaning beauty, and graphein, meaning to write, but the art it names grew up separately in several parts of the world. In China, brush writing was already prized as a fine art by the fourth century, when Wang Xizhi wrote his most admired piece, a short preface to poems composed at the Orchid Pavilion in 353. It is known today only from copies, because, according to tradition, a Tang emperor had the original buried with him in 649. In medieval Europe and the Islamic world, skilled scribes copied sacred texts by hand, and rulers depended on them for laws, decrees and official letters.

B. Each tradition relies on its own instruments, and these determine the look of its letters. Medieval European scribes wrote with quills made from goose feathers, while Arabic calligraphers use a qalam, a pen made from a length of reed whose end is sliced at a slant. Both kinds of pen are cut to a flat edge and held at a steady angle, so extra force does little to thicken a line; instead, the edge leaves a broad band when it travels one way and a hairline when it travels another. In China, Japan and Korea, the writer holds a soft brush of animal hair upright, making a line swell by pressing the tip down and narrowing it by lifting.

C. Mastery comes slowly, whatever the script. In the tenth century, Ibn Muqla, a high-ranking official in Baghdad, devised a method in which the height and width of each Arabic letter were measured in dots, small diamonds as wide as the pen's tip, so that a letter kept the same proportions at any size. Later, Ottoman students spent years copying their teachers' models and could sign finished pieces only after receiving a written licence called an icazet. In China and Japan, learners imitate the works of earlier masters before developing a style of their own. Many calligraphers also treat writing as an exercise for the mind, and some grind their ink slowly beforehand to settle their thoughts.

D. After printing spread across Europe in the late fifteenth century, books no longer had to be copied by hand, and by 1900 the pen methods of medieval scribes were barely remembered. The British calligrapher Edward Johnston recovered them by studying old manuscripts in museum collections, and his 1906 manual on writing and lettering launched a revival. In 1916 he designed the plain lettering for the signs of London's underground railway, and an updated form of it is still used across the network. Today, calligraphers work with graphic designers on logos, book covers and invitations, often on tablets that respond to pressure. UNESCO added Chinese calligraphy to its list of intangible cultural heritage in 2009, and Arabic calligraphy in 2021.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** Paragraph A says that Wang Xizhi's 'most admired piece', a preface written in 353, 'is known today only from copies, because, according to tradition, a Tang emperor had the original buried with him in 649'. 'A famous early masterpiece' paraphrases his most admired piece, 'is said to' paraphrases 'according to tradition', and 'lie in a monarch's tomb' paraphrases the emperor having the original buried with him. Paragraph C is the tempting wrong choice, because it mentions the works of earlier masters that learners imitate, but it says nothing about where any original is kept; paragraph D mentions old manuscripts in museum collections, not a work buried with a ruler.

**Q2:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** Paragraph B says that 'Arabic calligraphers use a qalam, a pen made from a length of reed whose end is sliced at a slant'. 'The traditional pen for Arabic script' paraphrases the qalam that Arabic calligraphers use, and 'fashioned from a plant stem' paraphrases 'made from a length of reed', since a reed is a tall plant with a hollow stem. Paragraph C also describes Arabic letters and the pen's tip, and paragraph D mentions Arabic calligraphy, but neither says what the pen is made of.

**Q3:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** Paragraph C says that in Ibn Muqla's method 'the height and width of each Arabic letter were measured in dots, small diamonds as wide as the pen's tip, so that a letter kept the same proportions at any size'. Because each dot is as wide as the tip, a broader nib makes bigger dots, and letters measured in bigger dots come out larger while keeping the same shape. 'Under one scheme' points to this method, and 'identically shaped' paraphrases 'the same proportions'. Paragraph B also talks about the flat edge of a pen, but it explains how thick a single line is, not how large the letters are.

**Q4:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** Paragraph D says that in 1916 Edward Johnston 'designed the plain lettering for the signs of London's underground railway, and an updated form of it is still used across the network'. 'A calligrapher's alphabet' paraphrases the lettering designed by 'the British calligrapher Edward Johnston', 'continues to' paraphrases 'is still used', and 'guide riders on one city's subway' paraphrases the signs of London's underground railway. Paragraph C is the tempting wrong choice, because it describes Ibn Muqla's system for measuring Arabic letters, but it says nothing about signs, public transport or use today.

**Q5:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** Paragraph A says that 'skilled scribes copied sacred texts by hand, and rulers depended on them for laws, decrees and official letters'. 'Trained writers' paraphrases skilled scribes, 'holy books' paraphrases sacred texts, and 'state documents' paraphrases the laws, decrees and official letters written for rulers. Paragraph D is the tempting wrong choice, because it says that after printing spread 'books no longer had to be copied by hand', but it does not say what kind of books they were or mention any work for rulers. Paragraph C calls Ibn Muqla a high-ranking official, but it never says that he wrote holy books or state documents.

**Q6:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 5
- **Explanation:** Paragraph C says that Ottoman students 'could sign finished pieces only after receiving a written licence called an icazet', so it gives this rule for one tradition only. C also says that learners in China and Japan imitate earlier masters, but not that they needed permission to sign anything, and no paragraph says this about European calligraphy. The statement turns one example into a claim about most traditions, so the information is not given.

**Q7:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 5
- **Explanation:** Paragraph D says that Edward Johnston's 1906 manual on writing and lettering 'launched a revival', and that an updated form of the lettering he designed in 1916 'is still used across the network'. The word 'still' there refers to the lettering on the railway signs, not to the book. No paragraph says whether the manual is still published or sold today, so the information is not given.

**Q8:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** Paragraph B says that the European quill and the Arabic qalam 'are cut to a flat edge and held at a steady angle, so extra force does little to thicken a line', while the East Asian brush makes 'a line swell by pressing the tip down'. Putting the two facts together shows that pushing harder changes the width of a brush stroke much more than that of a pen stroke. Paragraph D mentions tablets 'that respond to pressure', but it does not compare brushes and pens.

**Q9:** None
- 1. A
- 2. B
- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** Paragraph C says that many calligraphers treat writing as an exercise for the mind, 'and some grind their ink slowly beforehand to settle their thoughts'. 'Getting their materials ready' paraphrases grinding the ink before writing, and 'calm themselves' paraphrases settling their thoughts. Paragraph B is the tempting wrong choice, because it also describes preparing tools, with the reed 'sliced at a slant' and both kinds of pen 'cut to a flat edge', but it says nothing about writers calming their minds.
