## Exam 6 — Part 10: Lab-Grown Beef

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
The first samples came off the grill at ten on a cold Saturday morning: thumb-sized pieces of beef grown in a steel tank rather than on a steer. Sixty volunteers who had signed consent forms attended the first public tasting at a research centre outside Lethbridge. Until then, nobody outside Dr. Anjali Mathur's eleven-person team had eaten the beef, which cannot be sold in Canada until federal regulators approve it.

Dr. Mathur, the food scientist leading the project, said the beef began as a pea-sized piece of muscle from a heifer that still grazes on the centre's pasture; a fresh piece is taken every few months. The cells multiply in a broth of sugars and amino acids. "In a sealed tank, the meat never touches manure, the main source of E. coli," she said. Each sample cost about forty dollars, she admitted, and prices will drop only once tanks are a hundred times larger, perhaps a decade from now. To guests who called the product unnatural, she replied: "Yogurt, cheese and soy sauce are all made by living cells in steel vats, and nobody calls those unnatural."

Lorne Bakker, a third-generation rancher with four hundred cows on native prairie west of the city, cleaned his plate. "It tastes like a decent burger, but taste was never the question," he said. "My herd is the reason that land has never been ploughed, and the reason burrowing owls still nest on it. A tank will not keep a single hectare in grass."

Ruth Kanashiro, a registered dietitian at a Lethbridge family clinic, was more guarded. "We know its protein content, and the team can set the fat wherever it likes," she said. "Nobody has measured the iron yet. And nobody has eaten this for more than a few months, so until we know what a lifetime of it does, I will not call it better or worse than a steak."

In a recent poll, only 12 per cent of Albertans said they would buy such beef regularly. Nabil Darwish, a bus driver whose daughter was hospitalized after eating an undercooked burger, asked for a second sample. "After what happened to her, meat from a sealed tank sounds good to me," he said. "If it ever costs what ground beef does, it will be on our table every week." Marlene Wiebe, a Coaldale grandmother, finished her sample but will not serve it at home. "In the 1980s, I fed my children margarine because experts called it healthier," she said. "I want to see people eat this for twenty years before my grandchildren do."

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. a lab near Lethbridge won approval to sell its beef
- 2. lab-grown beef drew a divided response at its public debut
- 3. the first tasting turned most sceptical guests into supporters
- 4. scientists showed that tank-grown beef is safe to eat
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. a lab near Lethbridge won approval to sell its beef: The beef has not been approved; it "cannot be sold in Canada until federal regulators approve it."
✅ 2. lab-grown beef drew a divided response at its public debut: The article follows the first public tasting and the different reactions to it, from Dr. Mathur's defence of the beef to Bakker's doubts, Kanashiro's caution and two guests who reach opposite decisions.
❌ 3. the first tasting turned most sceptical guests into supporters: The doubters were not won over. Bakker says "taste was never the question", and Wiebe "finished her sample but will not serve it at home."
❌ 4. scientists showed that tank-grown beef is safe to eat: Nothing has been proven safe. Kanashiro says "nobody has eaten this for more than a few months", and regulators have not approved the beef.

**Q2:** None
- 1. worries about its long-term effects on people's health
- 2. the high cost of producing each tasting sample
- 3. the role his cattle play in protecting grassland
- 4. his disappointment with the way the sample tasted
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. worries about its long-term effects on people's health: Long-term health is what worries Kanashiro and Wiebe; Bakker never mentions health.
❌ 2. the high cost of producing each tasting sample: Dr. Mathur is the one who admits that "Each sample cost about forty dollars"; Bakker says nothing about price.
✅ 3. the role his cattle play in protecting grassland: Bakker says "My herd is the reason that land has never been ploughed" and "A tank will not keep a single hectare in grass."
❌ 4. his disappointment with the way the sample tasted: He was not disappointed. He says "It tastes like a decent burger, but taste was never the question."

**Q3:** None
- 1. Dr. Anjali Mathur
- 2. Lorne Bakker
- 3. Ruth Kanashiro
- 4. Nabil Darwish
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. Dr. Anjali Mathur: Dr. Mathur leads the project and defends the beef, while Wiebe will not serve it at home until she can "see people eat this for twenty years."
❌ 2. Lorne Bakker: Like Wiebe, Bakker ate his sample and is not won over, but his reason is the land ("My herd is the reason that land has never been ploughed"), not what the beef might do to people over time.
✅ 3. Ruth Kanashiro: Both want long-term evidence first. Kanashiro will not call it "better or worse than a steak" until "we know what a lifetime of it does", and Wiebe wants to "see people eat this for twenty years."
❌ 4. Nabil Darwish: Darwish is ready to put it "on our table every week" if the price is right and finds a sealed tank reassuring, which is the opposite of Wiebe's caution.

**Q4:** None
- 1. admitting that its price is still far too high
- 2. likening it to everyday foods made the same way
- 3. insisting that it tastes exactly like ranch beef
- 4. noting that it still needs federal approval first
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. admitting that its price is still far too high: She does admit that "Each sample cost about forty dollars", but that is about cost, not her reply to the guests who called the product unnatural.
✅ 2. likening it to everyday foods made the same way: Her reply to those guests is "Yogurt, cheese and soy sauce are all made by living cells in steel vats, and nobody calls those unnatural."
❌ 3. insisting that it tastes exactly like ranch beef: Dr. Mathur never talks about the taste; it is Bakker who says "It tastes like a decent burger."
❌ 4. noting that it still needs federal approval first: The article says the beef "cannot be sold in Canada until federal regulators approve it", but Dr. Mathur does not use this to answer the charge.

**Q5:** None
- 1. had already gone on sale in a few local shops
- 2. had been eaten only by the people who made it
- 3. had been tried by several ranchers from the area
- 4. had been tested on patients at a Lethbridge clinic
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. had already gone on sale in a few local shops: The beef "cannot be sold in Canada until federal regulators approve it", so no shop has sold it.
✅ 2. had been eaten only by the people who made it: "Until then, nobody outside Dr. Anjali Mathur's eleven-person team had eaten the beef", and her team is the one growing it.
❌ 3. had been tried by several ranchers from the area: Bakker ate his sample at the tasting itself; before that morning, "nobody outside Dr. Anjali Mathur's eleven-person team had eaten the beef."
❌ 4. had been tested on patients at a Lethbridge clinic: Kanashiro works at "a Lethbridge family clinic", but before the tasting nobody outside Dr. Mathur's team had eaten the beef.

**Q6:** None
- 1. slaughtered steers
- 2. living cattle
- 3. Bakker's herd
- 4. frozen samples
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. slaughtered steers: No animal was killed. The cells came from "a heifer that still grazes on the centre's pasture."
✅ 2. living cattle: The beef "began as a pea-sized piece of muscle from a heifer that still grazes on the centre's pasture", and "a fresh piece is taken every few months."
❌ 3. Bakker's herd: The heifer grazes "on the centre's pasture", not on Bakker's ranch.
❌ 4. frozen samples: The cells come from a living heifer, and "a fresh piece is taken every few months."

**Q7:** None
- 1. regulators approve its sale
- 2. more shoppers start buying it
- 3. production is scaled up greatly
- 4. the feeding broth becomes cheaper
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. regulators approve its sale: Approval decides when the beef can be sold ("cannot be sold in Canada until federal regulators approve it"); Dr. Mathur links the price to the size of the tanks.
❌ 2. more shoppers start buying it: Dr. Mathur does not tie the price to demand; the poll simply reports that "only 12 per cent of Albertans" would buy such beef regularly.
✅ 3. production is scaled up greatly: She says that "prices will drop only once tanks are a hundred times larger, perhaps a decade from now."
❌ 4. the feeding broth becomes cheaper: The cells grow "in a broth of sugars and amino acids", but Dr. Mathur ties lower prices only to larger tanks.

**Q8:** None
- 1. its price matches regular beef
- 2. a dietitian has approved it
- 3. long-term studies are finished
- 4. it tastes more like steak
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. its price matches regular beef: Darwish says "If it ever costs what ground beef does, it will be on our table every week."
❌ 2. a dietitian has approved it: Kanashiro is the dietitian in the article, and Darwish never says he is waiting for anyone's approval.
❌ 3. long-term studies are finished: Waiting for years of evidence is Wiebe's condition ("eat this for twenty years"), not Darwish's.
❌ 4. it tastes more like steak: Darwish liked the taste enough to ask "for a second sample"; the only condition he gives is the price.

**Q9:** None
- 1. stays free of animal waste
- 2. has passed federal inspection
- 3. contains less fat than steak
- 4. comes from a single animal
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. stays free of animal waste: Dr. Mathur says "In a sealed tank, the meat never touches manure, the main source of E. coli."
❌ 2. has passed federal inspection: The beef has not been approved yet; it "cannot be sold in Canada until federal regulators approve it."
❌ 3. contains less fat than steak: Dr. Mathur makes no claim about fat. Kanashiro only says "the team can set the fat wherever it likes."
❌ 4. comes from a single animal: The cells did come from one heifer, but Dr. Mathur's safety claim is about manure, not about the number of animals.

**Q10:** None
- 1. iron
- 2. protein
- 3. fat
- 4. sugar
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. iron: Kanashiro says "Nobody has measured the iron yet."
❌ 2. protein: The protein is known. Kanashiro says "We know its protein content."
❌ 3. fat: The fat is under the team's control, since Kanashiro says "the team can set the fat wherever it likes."
❌ 4. sugar: Sugars are part of the broth the cells grow in, but Kanashiro never says the sugar content is unknown; the only thing she says nobody has measured is the iron.

## Exam 60 — Part 7: Parental Leave Advice

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Priya,

Congratulations again on the wonderful news! Marcus and I are so thrilled for you and Dev. I know you mentioned at brunch last Saturday that you're feeling a bit overwhelmed about your parental leave options, so I thought I'd share what I learned from my own experience.

When I had Olivia two years ago, I had to choose between the standard 12-month leave and the extended 18-month option. I ended up going with the 18-month plan, and honestly, I'm glad I did, even though it meant receiving smaller monthly payments from Employment Insurance. The total amount you receive is the same either way—it's just spread over a longer period. For us, it worked out because Marcus had started his new position at the hospital in Barrie by then, so his income covered most of our expenses.

The one thing I wasn't prepared for was how tight things got around month ten. Because the payments are smaller with the extended plan, we had to dip into our savings more than expected, especially after our car needed major repairs that winter. If your budget is already stretched, the standard option might feel less stressful financially. That said, those extra six months at home with Olivia before daycare started in Orillia were priceless. I got emotional dropping her off on her first day and kept thinking how much harder it would have been six months earlier.

Also, Dev should look into his employer's top-up policy. Marcus's hospital offered a top-up for the first fifteen weeks, which made a real difference for us early on. Some employers only offer it if you commit to returning for a minimum period afterward, so it's worth reading the fine print now rather than being surprised later.

One more thing—if you decide on the extended leave, I'd strongly suggest applying for EI as soon as possible after the baby arrives in March. I waited almost three weeks after Olivia was born and there was a frustrating delay in receiving my first payment.

Why don't the four of us get together for dinner sometime next week? We could talk it all through, and Marcus can share the partner's perspective with Dev. Let me know what evening works for you.

Take care,
Nadia

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** Nadia is writing to Priya in order to .....
- 1. congratulate her on recent news.
- 2. invite her and Dev to a celebration.
- 3. share advice based on her own experience.
- 4. explain how Employment Insurance payments work.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. congratulate her on recent news: While Nadia opens with congratulations, the main purpose of the email is to share detailed advice about parental leave options, not simply to congratulate.
❌ 2. invite her and Dev to a celebration: Nadia does suggest dinner at the end, but this is a minor point — the bulk of the email is about parental leave advice.
✅ 3. share advice based on her own experience: Nadia says 'I thought I'd share what I learned from my own experience' and proceeds to offer detailed guidance about leave options, finances, top-ups, and EI timing.
❌ 4. explain how Employment Insurance payments work: EI is mentioned, but the email is broader personal advice about the whole parental leave decision, not a technical explanation of EI.

**Q2:** Around month ten of her leave, Nadia .....
- 1. had to use savings due to unexpected car costs.
- 2. decided to switch to the standard plan.
- 3. started looking for daycare in Orillia.
- 4. asked Marcus to find a better-paying position.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. had to use savings due to unexpected car costs: Nadia says 'we had to dip into our savings more than expected, especially after our car needed major repairs that winter.'
❌ 2. decided to switch to the standard plan: There is no mention of switching plans; Nadia stayed on the 18-month extended plan throughout.
❌ 3. started looking for daycare in Orillia: Daycare in Orillia is mentioned later when Olivia's leave ended, not at month ten.
❌ 4. asked Marcus to find a better-paying position: Marcus had already started his new position at the hospital in Barrie; there is no mention of seeking a different job.

**Q3:** Nadia suggests that Dev should .....
- 1. take parental leave at the same time as Priya.
- 2. check whether his employer offers a top-up.
- 3. apply for Employment Insurance early.
- 4. start saving more money before March.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. take parental leave at the same time as Priya: Nadia doesn't suggest Dev take leave simultaneously; she says he should look into his employer's top-up policy.
✅ 2. check whether his employer offers a top-up: Nadia says 'Dev should look into his employer's top-up policy' and warns about reading the fine print.
❌ 3. apply for Employment Insurance early: The EI application advice is directed at Priya, not Dev specifically.
❌ 4. start saving more money before March: There is no suggestion to start saving; Nadia advises checking the top-up policy.

**Q4:** Nadia delayed applying for EI, which resulted in .....
- 1. smaller payments over the full leave period.
- 2. a reduction in the total amount she received.
- 3. her missing the application deadline entirely.
- 4. a delayed start to her benefit payments.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. smaller payments over the full leave period: The smaller payments were a result of choosing the extended plan, not of the delay in applying.
❌ 2. a reduction in the total amount she received: Nadia says the total amount is the same either way; the delay didn't reduce the total.
❌ 3. her missing the application deadline entirely: Nadia didn't miss the deadline; she waited almost three weeks and experienced a delay, but still received payments.
✅ 4. a delayed start to her benefit payments: Nadia says she waited almost three weeks after Olivia was born and experienced 'a frustrating delay in receiving my first payment,' meaning her benefits were slow to begin.

**Q5:** Nadia most likely feels that her decision to take extended leave was .....
- 1. something she would not recommend to others.
- 2. a poor choice given the financial pressure.
- 3. only possible because they had large savings.
- 4. worthwhile despite the financial challenges.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. something she would not recommend to others: Nadia shares her experience positively and says 'I'm glad I did,' suggesting she would not discourage others.
❌ 2. a poor choice given the financial pressure: While finances were tight, Nadia says the extra time was 'priceless' and she's glad she chose it.
❌ 3. only possible because they had large savings: Nadia mentions dipping into savings more than expected, implying their savings weren't especially large; Marcus's income was the main factor.
✅ 4. worthwhile despite the financial challenges: Nadia says 'I'm glad I did' and calls the extra six months at home 'priceless,' even though she experienced financial strain around month ten.

**Q6:** Priya shared her worries with Nadia .....
- 1. by sending her an email after the weekend.
- 2. during a meal they had on the weekend.
- 3. at a dinner with their partners.
- 4. over a phone call earlier in the week.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. by sending her an email after the weekend: Nadia says Priya 'mentioned at brunch last Saturday,' not in a follow-up email.
✅ 2. during a meal they had on the weekend: Nadia says 'you mentioned at brunch last Saturday' — brunch is a meal and Saturday is the weekend.
❌ 3. at a dinner with their partners: The conversation happened at brunch, not dinner; it is not stated that Marcus and Dev were present.
❌ 4. over a phone call earlier in the week: Nadia specifically says the conversation happened 'at brunch,' which is an in-person meal, not a phone call.

**Q7:** None
- 1. stressful
- 2. confusing
- 3. helpful
- 4. surprising
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. stressful: Priya is thanking Nadia, so the tone is appreciative — 'stressful' is negative and doesn't fit a grateful response.
❌ 2. confusing: Nadia's advice was clear and detailed; Priya says it was 'exactly what I needed to hear,' not that it confused her.
✅ 3. helpful: Priya is expressing gratitude for practical advice from someone with relevant experience — 'helpful' fits the appreciative tone.
❌ 4. surprising: There is nothing unexpected about a friend sharing her experience; Nadia said she'd thought she'd share what she learned.

**Q8:** None
- 1. twelve-month
- 2. standard
- 3. fifteen-week
- 4. extended
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. twelve-month: The twelve-month option is the standard plan, which Nadia did NOT choose — she chose the 18-month extended plan.
❌ 2. standard: Nadia chose the extended 18-month plan, not the standard 12-month one; she says 'I ended up going with the 18-month plan.'
❌ 3. fifteen-week: Fifteen weeks refers to the duration of Marcus's employer top-up, not a leave plan.
✅ 4. extended: Nadia chose 'the extended 18-month option' and shared her experience with that plan, which Priya is referencing here.

**Q9:** None
- 1. savings
- 2. experience
- 3. time
- 4. income
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. savings: Nadia mentioned having to 'dip into our savings' with the extended plan; Priya is saying she doesn't have much in savings, so smaller payments concern her.
❌ 2. experience: The sentence is about financial concerns ('smaller monthly payments does worry me'), not about experience with parenting.
❌ 3. time: While the baby arrives in March, the sentence is clearly discussing finances, not time constraints.
❌ 4. income: 'We don't have much in income' is grammatically awkward and doesn't connect as naturally to the worry about smaller EI payments.

**Q10:** None
- 1. applied for Employment Insurance
- 2. looked into his top-up policy
- 3. committed to returning afterward
- 4. spoken to Marcus about it
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. applied for Employment Insurance: Nadia's EI advice was directed at Priya applying after the baby arrives, not at Dev applying now.
✅ 2. looked into his top-up policy: Nadia specifically advised that 'Dev should look into his employer's top-up policy' — Priya is confirming he hasn't done this yet.
❌ 3. committed to returning afterward: Returning for a minimum period is a condition some employers require, but Nadia's advice was to look into the policy first, not to commit.
❌ 4. spoken to Marcus about it: While dinner is planned so Marcus can share his perspective, Priya's sentence is about Dev checking with his own employer.

**Q11:** None
- 1. before the baby is born
- 2. within three weeks of delivery
- 3. right after the baby arrives
- 4. once we've decided on a plan
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. before the baby is born: Nadia said to apply 'as soon as possible after the baby arrives,' not before the birth.
❌ 2. within three weeks of delivery: Three weeks is how long Nadia waited, which caused a delay — she advises NOT waiting that long.
✅ 3. right after the baby arrives: Nadia said 'I'd strongly suggest applying for EI as soon as possible after the baby arrives' — 'right after' matches this advice.
❌ 4. once we've decided on a plan: Nadia's timing advice is about applying promptly after birth, not about waiting until the plan is chosen.

## Exam 60 — Part 9: Japanese Kintsugi Repair

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. Kintsugi, meaning "golden joinery," is the centuries-old Japanese art of repairing broken pottery using lacquer mixed with powdered gold, silver, or platinum. The practice is believed to have originated in the late 15th century when the Japanese shogun Ashikaga Yoshimasa sent a cracked Chinese tea bowl back to China for repair. The bowl was returned held together with unsightly metal staples, which displeased the shogun and prompted Japanese craftsmen to devise a more aesthetically pleasing restoration method. By incorporating precious metals into the mended seams, artisans transformed damaged ceramics into objects frequently considered more beautiful than the unbroken originals.

B. Kintsugi is deeply rooted in several Japanese philosophical traditions. It aligns closely with wabi-sabi, a worldview that embraces imperfection and transience as inherent qualities of existence. Rather than concealing damage, kintsugi celebrates it, treating breakage and repair as meaningful chapters in an object's history instead of something to disguise. The practice also reflects the concept of mushin, or "no mind," which encourages acceptance of change without attachment. Consequently, a kintsugi-repaired vessel is not regarded as diminished but rather as enriched by its fractures. This philosophical dimension distinguishes kintsugi from ordinary restoration techniques practiced elsewhere in the world.

C. The kintsugi restoration process is remarkably intricate and time-consuming. A craftsman first collects and reassembles the broken fragments, bonding them with urushi, a natural lacquer derived from the sap of the Toxicodendron vernicifluum tree native to East Asia. Each application of lacquer must cure in a humid environment for several weeks before the next layer can be applied. Once the structure is stable, the artisan dusts the lacquered seams with fine metallic powder, typically gold. A single repair can take anywhere from one to three months to complete, depending on the severity of the damage and the desired aesthetic outcome.

D. In recent decades, kintsugi has experienced a significant revival beyond Japan's borders. Contemporary artists and designers worldwide have adopted the technique, incorporating it into modern sculpture, jewellery, and interior design. Moreover, the philosophy underlying kintsugi has resonated deeply within psychology and wellness communities, where it is frequently used as a metaphor for resilience and emotional healing. Workshops teaching the craft have proliferated across Europe and North America since the early 2010s. Notably, auction houses such as Christie's and Sotheby's have sold kintsugi-repaired antiques for considerably higher prices than their undamaged counterparts, demonstrating the art's growing cultural and commercial value.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

- **Explanation:** Paragraph D states auction houses have sold kintsugi-repaired pieces for considerably higher prices than undamaged counterparts. 'Fetched premiums' paraphrases 'sold for considerably higher prices' and 'intact equivalents' paraphrases 'undamaged counterparts.' 'Artefacts' replaces the passage's 'antiques' to avoid direct copying.

## Exam 60 — Part 10: Performance Vacation Bonuses

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
When Toronto-based software firm Cranefield Technologies introduced a policy granting top performers an additional ten paid vacation days annually, the initiative was met with both enthusiasm and quiet skepticism from within the organization. CEO Margaux Elliston, who championed the program, maintains that compensating high-achieving employees with supplementary leisure time, rather than exclusively monetary bonuses, cultivates a workforce that is simultaneously more motivated and more resilient. "Exceptional output deserves recognition that transcends a paycheck," Elliston remarks, noting that internal surveys revealed a 27% increase in reported job satisfaction within the first year of implementation.

The premise underlying such policies is not without its detractors, however. Organizational psychologist Desmond Otieno cautions that tying vacation entitlements to measurable performance metrics may inadvertently foster resentment among colleagues who, despite diligent effort, fall marginally short of established benchmarks. Otieno, whose research at the University of Calgary examines incentive structures in mid-sized enterprises, contends that perceived inequity in non-monetary rewards can erode the collaborative spirit upon which most organizations depend. "When rest becomes a prize awarded to the few rather than a fundamental entitlement extended to all, the implicit message communicated to remaining staff is that their well-being is of lesser organizational consequence," he explains.

Nevertheless, proponents of performance-linked vacation schemes argue that such measures address a pervasive reluctance among Canadian workers to utilize their existing leave allocations. Human resources director Priya Venkatesh of consulting firm Alcourt Partners observes that employees who earn additional days off tend to feel less guilt about actually disconnecting from professional obligations, a psychological barrier she identifies as endemic in highly competitive workplaces. Venkatesh further suggests that the tangible nature of vacation days, unlike abstract praise or modest financial incentives, creates a reward employees can anticipate and plan around, thereby reinforcing sustained engagement throughout the fiscal year.

Critics and advocates alike acknowledge that the broader cultural attitude toward time away from work remains fraught with contradiction in many Canadian industries. Elliston concedes that the policy required careful calibration to prevent perceptions of favouritism, yet she remains resolute in her conviction that the experiment has yielded dividends. "Organizations that treat recuperation as a luxury rather than a strategic imperative will find themselves haemorrhaging precisely the talent they most wish to retain," Elliston asserts.

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. growing tendency among Canadian firms to phase out monetary bonuses entirely
- 2. debate surrounding the practice of tying supplementary vacation time to employee output
- 3. psychological consequences of prolonged absences on workplace productivity
- 4. strategies devised to resolve interpersonal friction stemming from incentive programs
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. growing tendency among Canadian firms to phase out monetary bonuses entirely: The article does not suggest a trend of eliminating monetary bonuses; Cranefield's policy supplements bonuses with vacation, not replaces them.
✅ 2. debate surrounding the practice of tying supplementary vacation time to employee output: The article presents multiple perspectives — Elliston championing, Otieno cautioning, and Venkatesh supporting — all centered on performance-linked vacation.
❌ 3. psychological consequences of prolonged absences on workplace productivity: While psychological aspects are touched upon, the article does not focus on the effects of absences on productivity.
❌ 4. strategies devised to resolve interpersonal friction stemming from incentive programs: Interpersonal conflict is raised by Otieno as a concern, but the article does not discuss resolution strategies.

**Q2:** None
- 1. are fundamentally incompatible with contemporary Canadian corporate culture
- 2. tend to yield greater benefits in smaller organizations than in larger ones
- 3. should incorporate more transparent and universally accessible performance benchmarks
- 4. risk undermining team cohesion by conveying unequal regard for employees' welfare
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. are fundamentally incompatible with contemporary Canadian corporate culture: Otieno critiques the policy's effects on collaboration but never claims it is incompatible with Canadian culture broadly.
❌ 2. tend to yield greater benefits in smaller organizations than in larger ones: Otieno's research examines mid-sized enterprises but he makes no comparative claims about organizational size.
❌ 3. should incorporate more transparent and universally accessible performance benchmarks: Otieno's concern is not about benchmark transparency but about the principle of making rest contingent on performance.
✅ 4. risk undermining team cohesion by conveying unequal regard for employees' welfare: Otieno explicitly states that perceived inequity in non-monetary rewards 'can erode the collaborative spirit' and that it signals remaining staff's well-being is 'of lesser organizational consequence.'

**Q3:** None
- 1. They furnish a concrete reward that employees can anticipate and organize around.
- 2. They eliminate the psychological pressure associated with annual performance evaluations.
- 3. They are perceived as less competitive and divisive than traditional monetary incentives.
- 4. They encourage employees to pursue professional development during their time away.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. They furnish a concrete reward that employees can anticipate and organize around.: Venkatesh states that 'the tangible nature of vacation days creates a reward employees can anticipate and plan around,' directly matching this option.
❌ 2. They eliminate the psychological pressure associated with annual performance evaluations.: Venkatesh discusses guilt about disconnecting, not pressure from performance evaluations.
❌ 3. They are perceived as less competitive and divisive than traditional monetary incentives.: Venkatesh does not characterize vacation days as less competitive; she contrasts their tangibility with abstract praise.
❌ 4. They encourage employees to pursue professional development during their time away.: Professional development during leave is never mentioned by Venkatesh or anyone in the article.

**Q4:** None
- 1. She attributes internal skepticism to a fundamental misunderstanding of the program's eligibility criteria.
- 2. She believes employee associations were primarily responsible for the initial opposition to the scheme.
- 3. She acknowledges implementation complexities but considers the results to have justified the effort.
- 4. She regrets not engaging external consultants before rolling out the initiative company-wide.
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. She attributes internal skepticism to a fundamental misunderstanding of the program's eligibility criteria.: Elliston does not attribute skepticism to misunderstanding; she acknowledges the need for careful calibration against favouritism perceptions.
❌ 2. She believes employee associations were primarily responsible for the initial opposition to the scheme.: No employee unions or associations are mentioned anywhere in the article.
✅ 3. She acknowledges implementation complexities but considers the results to have justified the effort.: Elliston 'concedes that the policy required careful calibration' yet 'remains resolute in her conviction that the experiment has yielded dividends,' showing she accepts challenges but values outcomes.
❌ 4. She regrets not engaging external consultants before rolling out the initiative company-wide.: Elliston expresses no regret; she remains confident in the policy's success.

**Q5:** None
- 1. negotiating for supplementary non-monetary workplace benefits with their employers
- 2. making use of the vacation time they are already entitled to take
- 3. transitioning effectively between competitive and collaborative work settings
- 4. sustaining consistent performance levels throughout an entire fiscal year
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. negotiating for supplementary non-monetary workplace benefits with their employers: The article discusses workers' reluctance to use leave, not difficulties in negotiating for benefits.
✅ 2. making use of the vacation time they are already entitled to take: The article mentions 'a pervasive reluctance among Canadian workers to utilize their existing leave allocations,' directly supporting this option.
❌ 3. transitioning effectively between competitive and collaborative work settings: While competition and collaboration are alluded to, the article does not discuss transitions between these environments.
❌ 4. sustaining consistent performance levels throughout an entire fiscal year: Venkatesh mentions 'sustained engagement throughout the fiscal year' as a benefit of the policy, not as something workers struggle with.

**Q6:** None
- 1. choose to acknowledge
- 2. seek to discipline
- 3. struggle to evaluate
- 4. refuse to compensate
- **Correct Answer:** Option 1
- **Explanation:** ✅ 1. choose to acknowledge: The article discusses various ways organizations recognize top performers, and the commenter frames the discussion around acknowledgement methods, fitting the article's theme of rewarding high achievers.
❌ 2. seek to discipline: The article concerns recognition and rewards, not disciplinary measures toward productive employees.
❌ 3. struggle to evaluate: While performance metrics are discussed, the commenter's focus is on recognition, not evaluation difficulty.
❌ 4. refuse to compensate: The article shows organizations actively compensating employees; refusal contradicts the article's premise entirely.

**Q7:** None
- 1. replaced
- 2. eliminated
- 3. governed
- 4. inspired
- **Correct Answer:** Option 3
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. replaced: 'Rest ought not to be replaced by output metrics' is grammatically awkward and misrepresents Otieno's argument, which is about rest being conditional on metrics, not substituted by them.
❌ 2. eliminated: 'Rest ought not to be eliminated by output metrics' implies metrics remove rest entirely, which is not Otieno's point; he argues rest should not be contingent upon metrics.
✅ 3. governed: 'Rest ought not to be governed by output metrics' accurately captures Otieno's argument that rest should be 'a fundamental entitlement' rather than controlled or determined by performance measures.
❌ 4. inspired: 'Rest ought not to be inspired by output metrics' is semantically illogical in this context and does not reflect Otieno's concerns.

**Q8:** None
- 1. proud of their colleagues
- 2. relieved by the outcome
- 3. indifferent toward the policy
- 4. devalued by the organization
- **Correct Answer:** Option 4
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. proud of their colleagues: The commenter describes negative feelings consistent with Otieno's warning about resentment, not positive sentiments like pride.
❌ 2. relieved by the outcome: Relief is a positive emotion that contradicts the commenter's alignment with Otieno's caution about perceived inequity.
❌ 3. indifferent toward the policy: Indifference contradicts the commenter's personal anecdote about the policy's negative emotional impact on excluded employees.
✅ 4. devalued by the organization: This aligns with Otieno's statement that excluded staff receive the 'implicit message that their well-being is of lesser organizational consequence,' which the commenter corroborates with personal experience.

**Q9:** None
- 1. divisive instrument
- 2. tangible incentive
- 3. abstract concept
- 4. generous gesture
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2

## Exam 7 — Part 7: Latin Dance Inquiry

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Ms. Rodriguez,
I am writing to inquire about joining your beginner Latin dance classes at Ritmo Dance Studio. I recently attended your studio's summer showcase and was absolutely amazed by the performances, particularly the salsa routines. The energy and joy of the dancers really inspired me to finally pursue my long-time interest in Latin dance.

I noticed on your website that you offer various beginner programs, but I'm a bit uncertain about which one would be most suitable for someone like me. I should mention that I have no previous dance experience, except for a few basic ballroom lessons I took five years ago. I'm particularly interested in learning salsa, but I see you also offer bachata and merengue classes for beginners.

My work schedule is quite flexible, as I work from home most days. However, I would prefer evening classes, ideally starting after 6:00 PM. I saw that you offer both group classes and private lessons, and I'm wondering which option you would recommend for a complete beginner. While private lessons might help me learn faster, I think I might enjoy the social aspect of group classes more.

I also have a few specific questions about your programs. First, do I need to bring my own dance partner, or do you rotate partners during group classes? Additionally, is there a specific dress code or shoe requirement for the classes? I noticed most dancers at the showcase were wearing special dance shoes, but I'm not sure if these are necessary for beginners.

One more thing - I saw on your social media that you sometimes organize social dance events for students. I'm particularly interested in these as they would provide opportunities to practice in a real dance environment. Could you tell me more about how often these events occur and whether beginners are welcome to participate?

Lastly, I understand that your new term starts next month. Could you please provide information about the registration process and current availability in your beginner classes? I'm eager to start as soon as possible and would appreciate any guidance you can offer about the best way to begin my dance journey at your studio.

Thank you for your time and consideration. I look forward to hearing from you and hopefully joining the Ritmo Dance Studio community soon.

Best regards,Michael Chen

## Exam 7 — Part 8: Wind Turbine Layout

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
WIND TURBINE BASICS
A Guide to Understanding Wind Energy Systems

Main Components
Rotor Blades
- Usually 3 blades per turbine
- Length: 30-50 meters
- Made of lightweight composite materials
- Designed to capture wind energy efficiently
- Regular maintenance required every 6 months

Tower Structure
- Height: 80-120 meters
- Steel or concrete construction
- Contains internal ladder and lift system
- Houses electrical cables and control systems
- Annual structural inspection needed

Nacelle Components
- Houses main mechanical parts
- Contains gearbox and generator
- Temperature control systems
- Wind speed and direction sensors
- Monthly maintenance checks required

Control Systems
Basic Operations: - Cut-in speed: 3-5 m/s
- Rated output: 12-15 m/s
- Cut-out speed: 25 m/s
- Automatic brake system for high winds
- Remote monitoring available 24/7

Safety Features
- Emergency shutdown system
- Lightning protection
- Aircraft warning lights
- Ice detection sensors
- Automatic fire suppression

Maintenance Schedule
Regular Service: Mon-Fri: 8:00 AM - 5:00 PM
Emergency Service: 24/7
Contact: 1-800-WINDPOWER
Email: service@windpower.com

Performance Monitoring
Online Dashboard Access: www.windmonitor.com
Username: operator
Password: provided during training

Important Notes: - Always follow safety protocols
- Report unusual sounds immediately
- Keep maintenance logs updated
- Weather conditions affect performance
- Annual certification required for operators

For Technical Support: Phone: 1-888-555-0123
Email: support@windpower.com
Website: www.windpower.com

## Exam 7 — Part 9: Commercial Aviation Evolution

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. The history of passenger aviation began shortly after the Wright brothers' successful first flight in 1903. The first commercial airline service was established in 1914, operating between St. Petersburg and Tampa, Florida, with a single-engine seaplane that could carry one passenger. During World War I, aircraft technology advanced rapidly, and by the 1920s, several airlines were offering regular passenger services. These early flights were primarily in small, propeller-driven aircraft that could carry between 10 and 20 passengers, flying at relatively low altitudes and speeds compared to modern standards.

B. The 1930s and 1940s saw significant developments in commercial aviation. The introduction of the Douglas DC-3 in 1935 revolutionized air travel, as it was the first aircraft to make passenger service profitable without relying on government mail subsidies. This aircraft could carry up to 28 passengers, fly at higher altitudes, and operate in most weather conditions. During World War II, technological advances in aircraft design and manufacturing led to larger, faster, and more reliable aircraft. The pressurized cabin was introduced, allowing planes to fly above adverse weather and provide greater passenger comfort.

C. The jet age began in the 1950s with the introduction of the de Havilland Comet, the world's first commercial jet airliner. Despite early setbacks due to structural problems, jet technology quickly became the standard for commercial aviation. The Boeing 707, introduced in 1958, proved enormously successful and helped establish the modern era of mass air travel. Airlines began offering more routes, and air travel became increasingly accessible to the middle class. The development of wide-body aircraft in the 1970s, such as the Boeing 747, dramatically increased passenger capacity and reduced ticket prices.

D. Today's commercial aviation industry bears little resemblance to its early days. Modern aircraft can carry hundreds of passengers, fly at speeds over 500 miles per hour, and travel thousands of miles without refueling. Advanced technology has made air travel significantly safer, more efficient, and more comfortable. The introduction of computerized reservation systems, online booking, and automated check-in processes has streamlined the travel experience. However, the industry continues to face challenges, including environmental concerns, rising fuel costs, and increasing security requirements in response to modern threats.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

- **Explanation:** Paragraph B mentions that the Douglas DC-3 introduced in 1935 'could operate in most weather conditions,' implying that earlier aircraft before this time were limited by weather conditions.

## Exam 7 — Part 10: Parking Lot Greening

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
The growing debate over transforming downtown parking lots into urban green spaces has intensified in Metro City, as municipal planners and community groups clash over the best use of valuable downtown real estate. The city's proposal to convert six major parking lots into community gardens and recreational areas has sparked both enthusiasm and concern among various stakeholders.

Sarah Chen, director of Urban Greening Initiative, strongly supports the conversion plan. 'These concrete spaces are essentially dead zones in our urban landscape,' Chen argues. 'Converting them into green spaces would not only improve air quality and reduce the urban heat island effect but would also create valuable community gathering spaces.' Chen points to successful similar projects in other cities, where transformed parking lots have become vibrant community hubs, supporting local food production and providing recreational opportunities.

However, James Morrison, president of the Downtown Business Association, expresses serious concerns about the proposal. 'While we support environmental initiatives, eliminating these parking lots would severely impact downtown businesses,' Morrison says. 'Our studies show that 65% of downtown customers rely on these parking facilities. Without adequate parking, we risk driving customers to suburban shopping centers, ultimately hurting our local economy.' Morrison suggests that the city should instead focus on developing green roofs and vertical gardens on existing buildings.

Urban planning expert Dr. Maria Rodriguez offers a middle-ground perspective. 'The solution isn't necessarily an all-or-nothing approach,' she explains. 'We could implement a hybrid model where some lots are converted while others are modernized into multi-use facilities with both parking and green space.' Rodriguez suggests incorporating rooftop gardens and solar panels into existing parking structures, creating what she calls 'smart parking solutions.'

Local resident and community activist Tom Peterson supports the conversion but with certain conditions. 'The key is ensuring these spaces truly serve the community,' Peterson says. 'We need a mix of community gardens, recreational areas, and possibly even small-scale urban farms. But we also need to address the parking concern by improving public transit options and creating alternative parking solutions before making these changes.'

### Questions & Answer Key

- 2. how to develop suburban shopping centers
- 3. the best use of downtown parking spaces
- 4. implementing green roof technologies

- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. whether to improve public transportation: The article mentions public transportation as part of Peterson's suggestion, but it's not the central conflict.
❌ 2. how to develop suburban shopping centers: Suburban shopping centers are mentioned only as a potential consequence, not the main conflict.

## Exam 8 — Part 7: Pottery Class Inquiry

Dear Ms. Thompson,
I am writing to inquire about the pottery classes offered at the Riverside Arts Center. I recently moved to the neighborhood and noticed your beautiful facility while walking past last weekend. I was particularly intrigued by the pottery pieces displayed in your front window and would love to learn how to create similar works.

I understand from your website that you offer various levels of pottery classes, but I'm a bit uncertain about which one would be most suitable for me. While I have never worked with clay before, I did take several art classes during my university years, including sculpture and painting. I'm wondering if this background would allow me to skip the absolute beginner's class and start at a more intermediate level.

I noticed that the fall semester begins next month, and I'm particularly interested in the evening classes as I work full-time during the day. Could you please provide information about the specific schedule for these classes? I would also appreciate details about the class sizes, as I prefer a learning environment where I can receive adequate individual attention from the instructor.

Additionally, I have a few questions about the materials and equipment. Does the course fee include all necessary supplies, or would I need to purchase anything separately? I saw that you have a small shop selling pottery tools, and I'm wondering if students receive any discount on these items. Also, I'm curious about the studio access policy - would I be able to use the facilities outside of class hours to practice?

One more thing - I noticed on your social media that you occasionally organize pottery workshops with guest artists. These look fascinating, and I would love to know if enrollment in regular classes gives priority access to these special events. I'm particularly interested in the upcoming workshop with Maria Rodriguez, whose work I've admired for years.

I understand that class spots fill up quickly, so I would greatly appreciate a response at your earliest convenience. If possible, I would also love to schedule a brief visit to tour the facility and perhaps observe a class in session before making my final decision.

Thank you for your time and consideration. I look forward to hopefully becoming part of your creative community.

Best regards,Sarah Chen

## Exam 8 — Part 9: Roman Mosaic Craftsmanship

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. Roman mosaics, dating back to the 2nd century BCE, represent one of the most enduring art forms of ancient Mediterranean civilization. These intricate designs were created by setting small pieces of colored stone, glass, or ceramic materials called tesserae into mortar to form patterns or pictures. Initially used as practical flooring solutions in Roman villas and public buildings, mosaics evolved from simple black and white geometric patterns into elaborate, multi-colored artistic masterpieces that depicted everything from daily life scenes to mythological stories.

B. The creation of mosaics was a meticulous process that required both artistic vision and technical expertise. Skilled craftsmen, known as tessellarii, would first prepare a solid foundation of multiple layers, including a bottom layer of rough stones and a top layer of fine mortar. They would then carefully place thousands of tesserae, often cutting them into specific shapes to fit precise patterns. The finest mosaics could contain up to 120 tesserae per square inch, demonstrating the extraordinary patience and precision required in this ancient craft.

C. While early Roman mosaics primarily featured geometric patterns and basic designs, by the 1st century CE, they had developed into sophisticated narrative scenes. These pictorial mosaics, found in wealthy homes and public buildings, often portrayed historical events, mythological tales, or scenes from daily life such as hunting, agriculture, and maritime activities. The famous Alexander Mosaic from Pompeii, depicting a battle between Alexander the Great and Persian King Darius III, exemplifies the remarkable level of detail and artistic achievement possible in this medium.

D. The legacy of Roman mosaics extends far beyond their original era, influencing art and architecture throughout history. Many of these ancient masterpieces have survived to the present day, protected by the layers of volcanic ash and debris that buried cities like Pompeii and Herculaneum. Modern archaeological discoveries continue to uncover new examples, providing valuable insights into Roman life, culture, and artistic practices. Today, the techniques developed by Roman artisans still influence contemporary mosaic artists, who continue to create works using methods remarkably similar to those employed two thousand years ago.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

### Questions & Answer Key

- 1. A

- 3. C
- 4. D
- 5. E
- **Correct Answer:** Option 5
- **Explanation:** According to paragraph A, Roman mosaics were 'Initially used as practical flooring solutions in Roman villas and public buildings,' which contradicts the statement that they were created primarily for decorative purposes.

## Exam 8 — Part 10: Public Sex Education

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
The ongoing debate about sex education in public schools has intensified as the school board considers updating its curriculum policies. While some advocate for comprehensive sex education, others maintain that abstinence-only programs better serve students' needs and align with community values.

Dr. Sarah Martinez, a public health researcher at Central University, strongly supports comprehensive sex education. 'Research consistently shows that students who receive comprehensive sex education are more likely to make informed decisions about their health,' she argues. 'These programs don't encourage sexual activity, as some fear. Instead, they provide accurate information about human anatomy, relationships, contraception, and sexually transmitted infections.' Martinez points to studies indicating lower teen pregnancy rates in districts with comprehensive programs.

However, James Wilson, director of the Family Values Coalition, believes abstinence-only education is the most appropriate approach for public schools. 'Comprehensive sex education often undermines family values and sends mixed messages to young people,' Wilson states. 'By teaching about contraception, we're implicitly suggesting that teenage sexual activity is acceptable. The focus should be on promoting abstinence as the only completely safe choice.' Wilson emphasizes that parents should be the primary source of guidance on such sensitive topics.

School nurse Patricia Thompson offers a middle-ground perspective based on her 15 years of experience. 'I've seen the consequences of both approaches,' she says. 'While abstinence is important to discuss, students also need accurate health information. Many parents aren't comfortable having these conversations, leaving teenagers vulnerable to misinformation from unreliable sources.' Thompson advocates for a balanced approach that emphasizes abstinence while including essential health information.

Dr. Robert Chen, an adolescent psychologist, supports Thompson's view but emphasizes the need for age-appropriate content. 'The question isn't whether to provide sex education, but how to deliver it effectively,' Chen explains. 'Programs should be tailored to students' developmental stages and include discussions about healthy relationships, consent, and decision-making skills. Simply telling teenagers to abstain without providing comprehensive information doesn't prepare them for real-world situations.'

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. whether parents should be involved in sex education
- 2. the effectiveness of different educational approaches
- 3. the age at which sex education should begin
- 4. who should teach sex education in schools
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. whether parents should be involved in sex education: The article doesn't show them disagreeing about parent involvement, though Wilson mentions parents should be primary sources of guidance.
✅ 2. the effectiveness of different educational approaches: This is the correct answer as Martinez supports comprehensive sex education based on research showing better outcomes, while Wilson supports abstinence-only education based on values.
❌ 3. the age at which sex education should begin: The article doesn't mention them discussing appropriate ages for sex education.
❌ 4. who should teach sex education in schools: The article doesn't mention them disagreeing about who should teach sex education.

## Exam 9 — Part 7: Debate Competition Inquiry

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
Dear Mr. Thompson,
I hope this message finds you well. I'm writing regarding my daughter Sarah's interest in joining the upcoming regional debate competition with Lincoln High's debate team. As you may know, Sarah has been an active member of the debate club for the past semester, and she's extremely enthusiastic about taking her involvement to the next level.

I understand from Sarah that the regional competition is scheduled for next month in Portland, and the team will be away for three days. While I fully support her participation, I'd appreciate some additional information about the arrangements. Sarah mentioned that the school will provide transportation to and from Portland, but I'd like to know more about the supervision and accommodation details during the stay.

Sarah has shown remarkable progress since joining the debate club. Her public speaking skills have improved significantly, and I've noticed she's become more confident in expressing her views at home. Just last week, she successfully convinced us to adopt a more comprehensive recycling system at home using the argumentation techniques she learned in the club! However, this would be her first overnight school trip, and naturally, we have some concerns.

I understand that the team needs to submit their final roster by next week. Before giving my consent, I would like to know about the daily schedule during the competition, meal arrangements, and emergency protocols. Sarah also mentioned that there might be some additional costs involved for team uniforms and registration fees. Could you please provide details about these expenses?

Additionally, Sarah has mentioned that the team practices will be intensified in the weeks leading up to the competition. As she's also involved in the school orchestra, I want to ensure that she can balance both commitments. Could you share the practice schedule so we can plan accordingly? She's particularly concerned about the practice sessions conflicting with her Thursday afternoon orchestra rehearsals.

I believe this competition would be an excellent opportunity for Sarah to develop her skills further and represent our school. She's already started researching the previous years' topics and is working on improving her rebuttal techniques. Your guidance has been instrumental in nurturing her interest in debate, and we're grateful for your dedication to the team.

Looking forward to hearing from you soon about these details.

Best regards,Linda Parker

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** The main purpose of this letter is to .....
- 1. express concerns about Sarah's academic performance
- 2. request information about a school event
- 3. complain about the debate club schedule

## Exam 9 — Part 8: Bird Feeder Comparison

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
BACKYARD BIRD FEEDER GUIDE
Your Complete Guide to Attracting Local Birds

Types of Feeders
Hopper Feeder
- Holds up to 2 pounds of seed
- Ideal for medium to large birds
- Weather resistant roof
- $25-35

Tube Feeder
- Perfect for small birds
- Holds 1 pound of seed
- Multiple feeding ports
- $15-25

Platform Feeder
- Open tray design
- Good for all bird sizes
- Easy to clean
- $20-30

Recommended Seeds
Basic Seed Mix
- Perfect for beginners
- Attracts various local birds
- $12 per 5lb bag

Premium Songbird Mix
- High-quality blend
- Contains sunflower seeds
- Attracts colorful songbirds
- $18 per 5lb bag

Maintenance Tips
Daily: - Check seed levels
- Remove wet or moldy seed
- Clean spilled seed below feeder

Weekly: - Wash feeding ports
- Inspect for damage
- Refill with fresh seed

Monthly: - Deep clean all feeders
- Sanitize with mild soap
- Check mounting hardware

Best Placement
- 10-12 feet from trees or bushes
- 5-6 feet above ground
- Away from windows
- Protected from rain when possible

Common Visitors
Spring/Summer: - Cardinals
- Blue Jays
- Chickadees
- Finches

Fall/Winter: - Sparrows
- Woodpeckers
- Nuthatches
- Juncos

For more information: Website: www.backyardbirding.com
Email: info@backyardbirding.com
Phone: 555-BIRD (2473)

Note: Different birds may visit depending on your location and season. Clean feeders regularly to prevent spread of disease.

## Exam 9 — Part 9: Hot Spring Balneotherapy

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
A. Natural hot springs, also known as thermal springs, are geological wonders that have been revered for their therapeutic properties throughout human history. These springs occur when groundwater is heated by geothermal energy and rises to the surface, carrying dissolved minerals from deep within the Earth. The temperature of these springs typically ranges from 20°C to 65°C (68°F to 149°F), though some can reach much higher temperatures. The mineral content varies depending on the rocks through which the water travels, but commonly includes sulfur, calcium, magnesium, and various trace elements.

B. The earliest documented use of hot springs for healing purposes dates back to ancient civilizations. The Romans built elaborate bathhouses around natural springs, while Japanese onsen traditions have existed for over a thousand years. Indigenous peoples across continents have long recognized the medicinal value of these thermal waters. The practice of using hot springs for health benefits, known as balneotherapy, gained significant popularity in Europe during the 18th and 19th centuries, when doctors began prescribing 'taking the waters' as a treatment for various ailments.

C. Modern scientific research has begun to validate many traditional beliefs about the healing properties of hot springs. Studies have shown that regular bathing in mineral-rich thermal waters can help alleviate symptoms of various conditions, including arthritis, fibromyalgia, and certain skin disorders. The combination of heat and mineral content helps improve blood circulation, reduce inflammation, and relax muscles. Additionally, the high pressure of water on the body during immersion has been found to improve lymphatic drainage and boost immune system function.

D. Despite the documented benefits, medical professionals caution that hot spring therapy should be approached with proper consideration. Excessive exposure to high temperatures can cause dehydration and heat stress, particularly in elderly individuals or those with heart conditions. The presence of certain minerals, while beneficial in moderate amounts, may cause adverse reactions in some people. Furthermore, natural hot springs can harbor harmful bacteria if not properly maintained, making it essential for visitors to choose reputable facilities and follow safety guidelines when enjoying these natural healing waters.

E. Not given in any of the above paragraphs.

- 5. E

- **Explanation:** Paragraph A mentions that hot springs have temperatures ranging from 20°C to 65°C, but there is no information in any paragraph about whether these temperatures remain constant throughout the year or fluctuate seasonally.

## Exam 9 — Part 10: Supermarket Nutritionist Mandate

### Prompt / Passage / Instructions
The growing debate over mandatory in-store nutritionists at grocery stores has sparked diverse opinions among health professionals and industry experts. The proposal, which would require supermarkets with annual revenues exceeding $5 million to employ certified nutritionists, has generated both support and criticism from various stakeholders.

Dr. Sarah Chen, a public health specialist at Metropolitan Health Institute, strongly advocates for the initiative. 'Having nutritionists available in grocery stores could significantly impact public health outcomes,' she argues. 'Many consumers struggle with making healthy food choices, understanding nutrition labels, and managing dietary restrictions. On-site nutritionists could provide immediate guidance and help shoppers make more informed decisions.' Chen points to successful pilot programs in several states where stores with in-house nutritionists reported increased sales of healthier foods and improved customer satisfaction.

However, James Morton, president of the Regional Grocery Store Association, opposes the mandate, citing practical and financial concerns. 'This requirement would place an unreasonable burden on grocery stores, particularly smaller chains that are already operating on thin profit margins,' Morton explains. 'The additional staffing costs would inevitably lead to higher food prices, potentially making healthy food less accessible to budget-conscious consumers.' He suggests that stores should have the flexibility to decide whether to offer nutritionist services based on their customer base and business model.

Consumer advocate Maria Rodriguez offers a middle-ground perspective. 'While having access to nutritional expertise is valuable, there are other ways to achieve this goal,' she suggests. 'Stores could offer scheduled consultation days or virtual nutrition services instead of full-time staffing.' Rodriguez believes that a one-size-fits-all approach might not serve all communities effectively and recommends exploring alternative solutions that balance consumer needs with business practicality.

Meanwhile, registered dietitian Lauren Thompson supports the proposal but acknowledges implementation challenges. 'The intention is excellent, but we need to consider the practical aspects,' Thompson says. 'There's already a shortage of registered dietitians, and requiring their presence in every major grocery store could strain the profession. Perhaps we should focus first on developing a sustainable model that includes both in-person and digital nutrition guidance options.'

### Questions & Answer Key
**Q1:** None
- 1. the effectiveness of nutritional guidance
- 2. the financial implications of the mandate
- 3. the quality of nutritionist training
- 4. the impact on food prices
- **Correct Answer:** Option 2
- **Explanation:** ❌ 1. the effectiveness of nutritional guidance: The text doesn't show they disagree about effectiveness.
✅ 2. the financial implications of the mandate: Dr. Chen advocates for the initiative while Morton opposes it specifically citing 'practical and financial concerns' and that it would 'place an unreasonable burden on grocery stores.'
❌ 3. the quality of nutritionist training: This is not mentioned as a point of disagreement.
❌ 4. the impact on food prices: While Morton mentions higher food prices as a consequence, this is part of the broader financial implications, not the central disagreement.
